Leapfrogging vortex rings for the 3-dimensional incompressible Euler equations
Juan Dávila, Manuel del Pino, Monica Musso, Juncheng Wei

Abstract
. A classical problem in fluid dynamics concerns the interaction of multiple vortex rings sharing a common axis of symmetry in an incompressible, inviscid 3 3 -dimensional fluid. Helmholtz (1858) observed that a pair of similar thin, coaxial vortex rings may pass through each other repeatedly due to the induced flow of the rings acting on each other. This celebrated configuration, known as leapfrogging , has not yet been rigorously established. We provide a mathematical justification for this phenomenon by constructing a smooth solution of the 3d Euler equations exhibiting this motion pattern.



1.
Introduction

We consider the 3-dimensional Euler equation for an ideal incompressible fluid given by

(1.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\ {\bf u}_{t}+({\bf u}\cdot{\nabla}){\bf u}=-{\nabla}p,\\
&\ {\rm div}\,{\bf u}=0,\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{3},\ t\geq 0.\\
\end{aligned}\right.

For a solution
{\bf u}(x,t)
of (
1.1
),
its vorticity is defined as

\vec{\omega}=\mathop{\rm curl}{\bf u}
. Then
\vec{\omega}

solves the Euler system (
1.1
) in its vorticity form,

(1.2)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\ {\vec{\omega}}_{t}+({\bf u}\cdot{\nabla}){\vec{\omega}}=(\vec{\omega}\cdot{\nabla}){\bf u},\quad{\bf u}=\mathop{\rm curl}\vec{\psi},\\
&\vec{\psi}(x,t)=\left(-\Delta\right)^{-1}\vec{\omega}\,=\,{1\over 4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}{1\over|x-y|}\vec{\omega}(y,t)\,dy,\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{3},\ t\geq 0.\\
\end{aligned}\right.

A vortex ring is an
axially symmetric solution of (
1.2
) which does not change shape in time, whose
vorticity is mostly concentrated inside a solid torus which moves with constant speed along
the symmetry axis.
The vortex lines form large circles that fill the torus, whereas fluid particles
spin around the vortex core within perpendicular cross sections characterized with a thin torus‐shaped region in which
the vorticity of the fluid is concentrated.
These objects were first described by Helmholtz
in his celebrated work
[
36
,
37
]
. He considered with great
attention the situation where the vorticity field is concentrated in a circular vortex-filament
of very small section, a thin vortex ring.
Helmholtz also analyzed the interaction between two or more similar coaxial vortex rings with thin sections
and similar translation speeds.
As pointed out by Jerrard and Smets
[
43
]
, his description reads:

We can now see generally how two ring-formed vortex-filaments having the same
axis would mutually affect each other, since each, in addition to its proper motion,
has that of its elements of
fluid as produced by the other. If they have the same
direction of rotation, they travel in the same direction; the foremost widens and
travels more slowly, the pursuer shrinks and travels faster till finally, if their
velocities are not too different, it overtakes the first and penetrates it. Then the
same game goes on in the opposite order, so that the rings pass through each other
alternately.

The motion pattern described by Helmholtz is often termed

leapfrogging
in
fluid mechanics.
Leapfrogging vortex rings are solutions of the Euler equations where several interacting
vortex rings sharing a common axis of symmetry move in the same direction along the symmetry axis and
pass through each other repeatedly due to the induced flow of the rings
acting on each other as depicted in Figure
1
.

Figure 1.
Leapfrogging vortex rings.

Even though this phenomenon
has been widely studied since
Helmholtz, as far as we know it has not yet been mathematically justified. In this paper we present what seems to be the first rigorous construction of a solution to the Euler equations with
a leapfrogging motion pattern.

The solution we construct belongs to the axisymmetric, no-swirl class, namely with a velocity field

{\bf u}(x,t)
which in standard cylindrical coordinates
(r,\theta,z)
takes the form

{\bf u}(x,t)=u^{r}(r,z,t)\,{\bf e}_{r}+u^{z}(r,z,t)\,{\bf e}_{z},\quad x=(r\cos\theta,r\sin\theta,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3},

where

{\bf e}_{r}=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta,0),\quad{\bf e}_{\theta}=(-\sin\theta,\cos\theta,0),\quad{\bf e}_{z}=(0,0,1),
and

(1.3)

(r,z)\,\in\,\Sigma=\{(r,z)\,/\,r>0,z\in\mathbb{R}\}.

The corresponding vorticity
\vec{\omega}=\mathop{\rm curl}{\bf u}
takes the form

\vec{\omega}(x,t)=\omega^{\theta}(r,z,t)\,{\bf e}_{\theta},\quad{\mbox{where }}\omega^{\theta}={\partial}_{z}u^{r}-{\partial}_{r}u^{z}.

The divergence free condition
\mathop{\rm div}{\bf u}=0
becomes

{\partial}_{r}(ru^{r})+{\partial}_{z}(ru^{z})=0{\quad\hbox{in }}\Sigma,\quad t>0.

This implies the existence of a scalar function
\psi^{\theta}(r,z,t)
such that

u^{r}=-{\partial}_{z}\psi^{\theta},\quad u^{z}={1\over r}{\partial}_{r}(r\psi^{\theta}){\quad\hbox{in }}\Sigma,\quad t>0.

The relation
\vec{\omega}=\mathop{\rm curl}{\bf u}
yields

-\left({\partial}_{z}^{2}+{\partial}_{r}^{2}+{1\over r}{\partial}_{r}-{1\over r^{2}}\right)\psi^{\theta}=\omega^{\theta}.

We notice that the vector-valued function

\vec{\psi}(x,t)=\psi^{\theta}(r,z,t)\,{\bf e}_{\theta}

satisfies
-\Delta\vec{\psi}=\vec{\omega}
.
The Euler equations (
1.2
) thus become

(1.4)

\begin{array}[]{l}{\partial}_{t}\omega^{\theta}+u^{r}{\partial}_{r}\omega^{\theta}+u^{z}{\partial}_{z}\omega^{\theta}=\frac{1}{r}u^{r}\omega^{\theta},\quad-[{\partial}_{z}^{2}+{\partial}_{r}^{2}+{1\over r}{\partial}_{r}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}]\psi^{\theta}=\omega^{\theta}\quad(r,z)\in\Sigma,\quad t>0.\end{array}

The axisymmetric Euler equation without swirl (
1.4
) has a formal singularity at
r=0
, which sometimes is inconvenient to work with. To remove the artificial singularity the following change of variable is usually used

\omega=\frac{\omega^{\theta}}{r},\quad\psi=\frac{\psi^{\theta}}{r}.

The functions
\omega
,
\psi
are respectively known as the
relative
vorticity and stream function. In terms of these new variables equation (
1.4
) becomes

(1.5)

\begin{array}[]{l}r\,{\partial}_{t}\omega+\nabla^{\perp}(r^{2}\psi)\cdot\nabla\omega=0,\quad-\Delta_{5}\psi=\omega{\quad\hbox{in }}\Sigma,\quad t>0,\end{array}

where

\nabla^{\perp}=(-{\partial}_{z},{\partial}_{r})

and

(1.6)

\Delta_{5}\psi=\partial_{rr}\psi+\frac{3}{r}\partial_{r}\psi+\partial_{zz}\psi,\quad x=(r,z),

supplemented with the conditions

{\partial}_{r}\psi(0,z,t)=0,\quad\lim_{|(r,z)|\to\infty}\psi(r,z,t)=0.

It is well-known that the initial value problem for (
1.5
) is globally well-posed in

L^{1}(rdrdz)\cap L^{\infty}(\Sigma)
, see
[
58
,
49
,
16
]
.
A bounded solution
\omega
to (
1.5
) with sufficient space
decay actually satisfies

(1.7)

t\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}r\omega(r,z,t)\,drdz=constant,\quad t\mapsto\|w(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=constant.

The same is true if the integral is taken in the time section of connected components of the support of
\omega
. We will use these facts in the formulation of a suitable first approximation
for a solution exhibiting the leapfrogging dynamics.

A
vortex ring
moving with constant speed
c
along the
z
-axis is a travelling wave solution of (
1.5
) with the form

\omega(r,z,t)=W_{0}(r,z-c\,t),\quad\psi(r,z,t)=\Psi_{0}(r,z-c\,t),

where
W_{0}
and
\Psi_{0}
solve

(1.8)

\nabla^{\perp}\left(r^{2}(\Psi_{0}-{c\over 2})\right)\cdot\nabla W_{0}=0,\quad-[\partial_{r}^{2}+\frac{3}{r}\partial_{r}+\partial_{z}^{2}]\Psi_{0}=W_{0}\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}\Sigma.

It is worth mentioning that if
\Psi_{0}(r,z)
satisfies a semilinear equation of the form

-[\partial_{r}^{2}+\frac{3}{r}\partial_{r}+\partial_{z}^{2}]\Psi_{0}=f\left(r^{2}(\Psi_{0}-{c\over 2})\right){\quad\hbox{in }}\Sigma,

for an arbitrary nonlinearity
f
, then
\Psi_{0},\ W_{0}=f\left(r^{2}(\Psi_{0}-{c\over 2})\right)
solves
(
1.8
). A first example of a solution with a compactly supported vorticity and positive relative vorticity was exhibited by Hill
[
46
]
.

In 1970, Fraenkel
[
30
]
(see also
[
53
]
) rigorously obtained a solution supported inside a torus with a tiny section of radius
\varepsilon>0
with a center located at a fixed distance
r_{0}>0
of the
z
-axis, and found the asymptotic expression

(1.9)

c=\frac{2}{r_{0}}|\log\varepsilon|(1+o(1))\quad\mbox{as}\quad\varepsilon\to 0.

Vortex rings have been analyzed in larger generality in
[
31
,
55
,
3
,
25
,
9
]
.
Expression
(
1.9
) corresponds to a special case of formal asymptotics
obtained by Da Rios in 1906
[
19
,
20
]
,
of thin vortex tubes around curves evolving by their binormal flow.
See
[
56
,
41
,
42
]
. Helicoidal travelling vortex tubes have been recently found in
[
23
]
.

Interacting vortex rings

We shall formally derive the dynamics
of
k\geq 2
thin, coaxial
vortex rings with comparable speeds given at main order by (
1.9
) in (
1.5
).
It is convenient to introduce the new unknown
W(r,z,\tau)
where

\omega(r,z,t)\,=\,W(r,z-2{r_{0}^{-1}}\,|\log\varepsilon|\,t,|\log\varepsilon|\,t\,),

so that Problem (
1.5
) in terms of
W(r,z,\tau)
,
\tau=|\log\varepsilon|\,t
, takes the form

(1.10)

|\log\varepsilon|\,r\,{\partial}_{\tau}W+{\nabla}^{\perp}(r^{2}\,(\Psi-r_{0}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|)\,)\cdot{\nabla}W\,=\,0,\quad-\Delta_{5}\Psi=W.

Let us assume the presence of a solution
W(r,z,\tau)
of
(
1.10
) which has the approximate form

(1.11)

rW(r,z,\tau)\,=\,\sum_{j=1}^{k}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{j}^{2}}U\left(\frac{x-P_{j}(\tau)}{\varepsilon_{j}}\right),\quad x=(r,z),\quad P_{j}(\tau)=(P_{j}^{1}(\tau),P_{j}^{2}(\tau)),

for small numbers
\varepsilon_{j}\to 0
and centers
P_{j}(\tau)
close to
(r_{0},0)
to be determined, where
U
is the Kaufmann-Scully vortex

(1.12)

U(y)\,=\,\frac{8}{(1+|y|^{2})^{2}},\quad y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

This ansatz is consistent with the fact that
\int rW\,drdz
should be preserved in time
on components of the support, see (
1.7
). On the other hand, the fact that the
L^{\infty}
-norm of
W
should be preserved in time on each component suggests that

P_{j}^{1}(\tau)\varepsilon_{j}^{2}
should be constant in time, say
=r_{0}\varepsilon^{2}
. Thus, we choose

\varepsilon_{j}=\varepsilon\frac{r_{0}}{\sqrt{P_{j}^{1}(\tau)}},

so that at main order, near
(r_{0},0)
we have

\displaystyle W(r,z,\tau)

\displaystyle=\,\sum_{j=1}^{k}\frac{1}{r_{0}\varepsilon^{2}}U\left(\frac{x-P_{j}(\tau)}{\varepsilon_{j}}\right),\quad x=(r,z).

Since the Green function
G(r,z)
solving

-[\partial_{r}^{2}+\frac{3}{r}\partial_{r}+\partial_{z}^{2}]G=8\pi\delta_{(r_{0},0)}\quad\text{in }\Sigma

satisfies
G(r,z)=-2\log(|x-(r_{0},0)|^{2})\,(1-\frac{3}{2r_{0}}(r-r_{0}))

near
(r_{0},0)
, up to an additive constant and lower order terms, at main order we have

\displaystyle\Psi_{\varepsilon}(r,z,\tau)

\displaystyle=\,\sum_{j=1}^{k}\frac{2}{P_{j}^{1}}\log\Big(\frac{1}{|x-P_{j}(\tau)|^{2}+\varepsilon_{j}^{2}}\Big)\,(1-\frac{3}{2P_{j}^{1}}(r-P_{j}^{1})),\quad x=(r,z),

Substituting this expression into equation (
1.10
) we obtain
that at main order and for each
j
,

(1.13)

\Big[-r|\log\varepsilon|\frac{dP_{j}}{d\tau}+{\nabla}^{\perp}_{x}\sum_{i\neq j}4r\log\frac{1}{|x-P_{i}|}\,-2{r-r_{0}\over r_{0}}|\log\varepsilon|\,{\bf e}_{2}\Big]\cdot{\nabla}U\left(\frac{x-P_{j}(\tau)}{\varepsilon_{j}}\right)\approx 0.

It is convenient to use the ansatz

P_{j}(\tau)=(r_{0},0)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\log\varepsilon|}}q_{j}(\tau),\quad q_{j}(\tau)=(q_{j}^{1}(\tau),q_{j}^{2}(\tau)).

Imposing vanishing of the left factor in (
1.13
) at
x=P_{j}
, neglecting lower order terms in a fixed interval
\tau\in[0,T]
, we arrive at the limiting system

(1.14)

\frac{dq_{j}}{d\tau}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-4\sum_{\ell\not=j}\,\,\frac{(q_{j}-q_{\ell})^{\perp}}{|q_{j}-q_{\ell}|^{2}}-2\,{q_{j}^{1}\over r_{0}^{2}}\,\,\left(\begin{matrix}0\\
1\end{matrix}\right),\quad\tau\in[0,T].

This is a Hamiltonian system for the energy

H_{k}(q_{1},\ldots,q_{k})=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-2\sum_{i\neq j}\log|q_{i}-q_{j}|-\frac{1}{r_{0}^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{k}|q_{j}^{1}|^{2}.

For instance, for
k=2
and restricting ourselves to
q_{1}=-q_{2}=q=(q^{1},q^{2})

we arrive at the system

(1.15)

\frac{dq}{d\tau}\,=\,\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-2\frac{q^{\perp}}{|q|^{2}}-2\,{q^{1}\over r_{0}^{2}}\,\,\left(\begin{matrix}0\\
1\end{matrix}\right),\quad\tau\in[0,T].

The level curves for its Hamiltonian
q\mapsto H_{2}(q,-q)
are depicted in Figure
2
.
Any solution of this system is periodic and lies on a closed level curve around the origin.

In short, the vorticity of
k
interacting
similar thin vortex rings looks like the superposition of vorticities of
k
individual rings with small cross section
\varepsilon
, whose centers evolve in time approximately following the
reduced leapfrogging dynamics
(
1.14
) being located at mutual distances of order

{1\over\sqrt{|\log\varepsilon|}}
.

Figure 2.
Level curves for
q\to H_{2}(q,-q)
.

Several authors investigated the reduced dynamics given by system (
1.14
), see for instance
[
26
,
27
,
18
,
7
,
38
,
45
,
46
,
50
,
1
,
8
]
. Numerical simulations of the leapfrogging, such as in
[
57
,
54
,
47
,
13
]
, provided theoretical evidence that this phenomenon should actually occur in the Euler equations. The leapfrogging phenomenon was experimentally confirmed in 1978 by Yamada and Matsui
[
59
]
. They used vortex rings made of air and used smoke for visualization and successfully created a leapfrogging pair of rings.

Our main result states the existence of a true smooth solution
\omega(r,z,t)
of Problem (
1.5
) asymptotically
obeying the dynamic law described above, for each given collisionless solution of System (
1.14
) on the time interval
[0,T].

It is more convenient to express, for a small
\varepsilon>0
, the problem in terms of the equivalent formulation (
1.10
)

(1.16)

|\log\varepsilon|\,r\,\frac{{\partial}W}{{\partial}\tau}+{\nabla}^{\perp}(r^{2}\,(\Psi-r_{0}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|)\,)\cdot{\nabla}W\,=\,0,\quad-\Delta_{5}\Psi=W,\quad x\in\Sigma,\quad\tau>0,

{\partial\over\partial r}\Psi(x,\tau)=0\quad{\mbox{on}}\quad x\in\partial\Sigma,\quad|\Psi(x,\tau)|\to 0\quad\text{as }|x|\to\infty,\,\tau>0.

We recall that, if
W(r,z,\tau)
solves this problem, then

\omega(r,z,t)\,=\,W(r,z-2{r_{0}^{-1}}\,|\log\varepsilon|\,t,\,|\log\varepsilon|\,t\,)\quad

solves (
1.5
).

Theorem 1
.

Let

q(\tau)=(q_{1}(\tau),\ldots q_{k}(\tau))
be a solution of System
(\ref{car1})
without collisions in
[0,T]
in the sense that

\inf\{|q_{i}(\tau)-q_{j}(\tau)|\ /\tau\in[0,T],\ i\neq j\}\,>\,0.
Then there exists a smooth solution
W_{\varepsilon}(r,z,\tau)
of Problem
(\ref{leap01})

such that for certain points
P_{j}^{\varepsilon}(\tau)
with the form

P_{j}^{\varepsilon}(\tau)=(r_{0},0)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\log\varepsilon|}}q_{j}(\tau)+O\Big(\frac{\log(|\log\varepsilon|)}{|\log\varepsilon|}\Big),\quad

we have

(1.17)

\displaystyle W_{\varepsilon}(r,z,\tau)

\displaystyle=\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}r_{0}}\,\sum_{j=1}^{k}U\left(\frac{x-P_{j}^{\varepsilon}(\tau)}{\varepsilon_{j}}\right)+\varphi(r,z,\tau),\quad x=(r,z)

where
U(y)=\frac{8}{(1+|y|^{2})^{2}}
,
\varepsilon_{j}^{2}=\varepsilon^{2}\frac{r_{0}}{P_{j}^{1}(\tau)}
. For
some number
C>0

we can estimate

|\varphi(r,z,\tau)|\,\leq\,C\sum_{j=1}^{k}\frac{\varepsilon^{2-\sigma}}{\varepsilon^{3-3\sigma}+\left|x-P_{j}^{\varepsilon}(\tau)\right|^{3}}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad x\in\Sigma,\ \tau\in[0,T],

for some
\sigma>0
small.

Theorem
1
is the first mathematically rigorous justification of the leapfrogging motion of vortex rings for the Euler equations since Helmholtz proposed it in 1858.
Next we make various comments on our result and connections with the literature.

\cdot

It has long been observed that vortex dynamics in the Euler equations exhibit strong analogies with the Ginzburg-Landau vortex evolution in two and three dimensions for the Gross-Pitaievskii equation. Jerrard and Smets
[
43
]
constructed axisymmetric solutions to the problem

iu_{t}=\Delta u+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}(1-|u|^{2})u{\quad\hbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{3}\quad

with several vortex rings precisely within the regime we are considering here, exhibiting the leapfrogging dynamics.
They state as a major open problem the corresponding result for the
3d
incompressible Euler equations. See also
[
44
]
for almost parallel GP vortex filaments, and
[
41
]
for a conditional result for the so-called vortex filament conjecture.
The interesting approach in
[
43
,
44
]
, based on limiting Ginzburg-Landau energy configurations, does not appear to be suitable for the Euler setting.

\cdot
Marchioro and Pulvirenti

[
52
]
analyzed smooth solutions with compactly supported, highly concentrated regular vorticities around a finite number of well-separated points for the
2d
Euler equations establishing the connection with the Kirchhoff-Routh vortex dynamics.
In
[
51
]
they previously did so for tiny vortex patches.
Extensions of those results to the evolution of thin axisymmetric vortex rings have been considered in
[
7
,
50
,
10
]
. In the recent work

[
10
]
evolution of compactly supported vortex rings under large separations has been recovered. However, the regime there considered does not allow to detect the leapfrogging dynamics.
The reason for this is that the method in those works does not provide a fine control on the evolution of the core of the vorticity.
In particular, their initially tiny local supports could expand in time, not allowing a fine control on the form of the solutions near the concentration rings which
in the leapfrogging dynamics are are at small order
|\log\varepsilon|^{-\frac{1}{2}}
distant to each other.

\cdot

We follow here an entirely different strategy to overcome that difficulty, using a scheme based on our previous work
[
21
]
on vortex desigularization in the
2d
-Euler case.
Problem (
1.5
), (
1.6
) can be regarded as an anisotropic version of the
2d
Euler equation. We first construct an accurate approximate solution, and then solve the full problem by linearization,
adjusting a small correction.
We need to substantially refine the procedure in
[
21
]
. The anisotropy of the equation creates error terms which can not just be treated as perturbations. That makes the improvement of approximation be much more delicate.
The approximate solution starts with the ansatz (
1.11
) where
U
is the the Kaufman-Scully vortex (
1.12
).
This profile is a convenient local choice for the building blocks of the rings, while not at all the only one possible. Profiles with a sufficiently fast decay could also be employed.
While a slight simplification in the approximation procedure could be obtained, that would be at the expense of complicating the a priori estimates.
The basic blocks (
1.12
) have been used to construct solutions with concentrated vorticities in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
in
[
21
]

and helicoidal travelling waves in
[
23
]
. It is worth mentioning that the nonlinear stability of fast-decay radial steady vortices is still an open problem. See the works

[
5
,
39
,
40
]
for recent linear stability results. The spectrum stability of vortex filament in
3d
-Euler
is a delicate matter, see

[
29
,
32
,
33
,
34
]
. Nonlinear stability of vortex ring solutions in
3d
-Euler case is a challenging problem. See the related remarkable paper by Bedrossian and Masmoudi
[
6
]
on nonlinear asymptotic stability for plane Couette flows.

\cdot
While the solution built is global-in time (as it is classical for the axi-symmetric no swirl setting) the phenomenon described is not expected to persist as
t\to+\infty
. More precisely, for each
T>0
we build a solution that approaches the leapfrogging dynamics in a suitable uniform sense as
\varepsilon\to 0
for
\tau\in[0,T]
in the scaled time variable
\tau=t/|\log\varepsilon|
. Numerical simulations
[
59
]
,
[
2
]
show that the leapfrogging dynamics does not persist in time beyond a few crossings of the vortex rings involved. In fact, even in the two-dimensional case the typical long-time behavior is mixing or turbulence, so that long-time uniform localized vorticity is not generic.

\cdot
We point out that the setting
k=2
,
q_{1}=-q_{2}
does not simplify the situation: the Euler equation
cannot be reduced to finding rotating or travelling wave type solutions. The existence of time-periodic solutions following the associated reduced periodic dynamics is a very difficult question. In case it exists it is probably extremely unstable. Global-in-time dynamics for other type of vortex configurations in the
2
-dimensional Euler equations has been constructed in
[
60
]
.

\cdot

We point out that the dynamics of a single vortex ring for the Navier-Stokes system has been considered in
[
11
]
,
and very recently in
[
35
]
with an approach closely connected with the one here, using the Lamb-Oseen self-similar vortex (which has a Gaussian decay).

\cdot

In Theorem
1
, all individual rings
in the vorticity expression (
1.17
) have similar positive circulations, which is what leads to the leapfrogging phenomenon. Another interesting situation is the case of rings with opposite sign circulations, namely dipole dynamics. This is related to various scenarios for potential singularities in axi-symmetric Euler flows, see
[
14
,
15
,
17
,
28
,
48
]
.

In the next section we explain in detail the scheme of the proof of Theorem
1
which is carried out in the subsequent sections.

2.
Scheme of the proof and organization of the paper.

The proposed strategy for our construction falls into two steps. First we derive an approximate solution, then we solve the full problem setting up an
inner-outer gluing scheme
.

The
inner-outer gluing scheme
has been a very
powerful tool in singularity formation problems for nonlinear elliptic and parabolic equations, see for instance
[
24
,
12
,
22
]
. In those applications, the use of maximum principle is essential. In
[
21
]
we extend this scheme to the Euler flow in
2
-dimensions and we find
regular
solutions with highly concentrated vorticities around a given number of moving points in the plane, the so-called
desingularized vortex problem
. This paper represents the first attempt to extend this scheme to the three-dimensional Euler flow and find regular solutions with highly concentrated vorticities around a given number of vortex rings interacting in accordance with the Leapfrogging dynamics.

We take advantage of the axi-symmetry and no-swirl assumption to recast the
3
-dimensional problem in the
2
-dimensional setting (
1.16
), which allows us to adapt some ideas from
[
21
]
. In the construction of solutions with vorticity highly concentrated around points for the
2
-dimensional Euler flow in
[
21
]
, each vortex is well described by a smooth radially symmetric function with fast decay at infinity, the Kaufman-Scully vortex
U
. The core of each vortex is of size
\varepsilon
and the interaction with other vortices can be easily controlled, as the distance of the centers of two different vortices is of order
O(1)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. On the contrary, radially symmetric profiles need an important non radial correction in order to well approximate vortex rings, the reason being the anisotropy in equation (
1.8
). The core of each ring is still of order
\varepsilon
but the relative distance between the centers of two rings is of size
|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}}
, which makes their interaction much stronger, and hence delicate to control. These are the features that make the steps in our argument quite involved: the construction of the approximation requires several consecutive adjustments and the inner-outer scheme to find the remainder of the solution has to be designed to properly describe the transition of the problem in different-scaled regions.

Construction of an approximate solution.

The basic building block for the construction of the leapfrogging is a single approximate travelling vortex ring with
highly
\varepsilon
-concentrated vorticity near a point
P_{0}=(r_{0},z_{0})
,
r_{0}>0
. Here
r_{0}
represents the radius of the ring, and
z_{0}
its vertical displacement. This is achieved finding
a constant
\alpha
and a stream function
\Psi_{\varepsilon}

almost
solving the equations for the travelling vortex ring

{\nabla}^{\perp}(r^{2}(\Psi_{\varepsilon}-\alpha|\log\varepsilon|))\cdot{\nabla}W_{\varepsilon}\sim 0,\quad{\mbox{where}}\quad W_{\varepsilon}=-\Delta_{5}\Psi_{\varepsilon},

in a neighbourhood of
P_{0}
, with the expectation that the vorticity

r\,W_{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup 8\pi\delta_{P_{0}},\quad\quad\mbox{as}\quad\varepsilon\to 0.

In accordance with (
1.8
), (
1.9
) and the discussion in the introduction, the constant
\alpha
is expected to satisfy
\alpha\sim{1\over r_{0}}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
and the vorticity
r\,W_{\varepsilon}
to have the form
{1\over\varepsilon^{2}\,r_{0}}U\left({x-P_{0}\over\varepsilon}\right)
.
More precisely, we find

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle={1\over r_{0}}+O(|\log\varepsilon|^{-1})

\displaystyle\Psi_{\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle={1\over r_{0}}\psi_{\varepsilon}\left({x-P_{0}\over\varepsilon}\right),\quad\psi_{\varepsilon}(y)=\left(\Gamma_{0}\left(y\right)-4\log\varepsilon\right)\left(1-{3\varepsilon\over 2r_{0}}y_{1}\right)+O(1)

\displaystyle W_{\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle={1\over\varepsilon^{2}r_{0}}w_{\varepsilon}\left({x-P_{0}\over\varepsilon}\right),\quad w_{\varepsilon}(y)=U(y)\left(1+{\varepsilon y_{1}\over 2r_{0}}\Gamma_{0}(y)\right)+U(y)O(\varepsilon y_{1})

in the region around
P_{0}
given by
|x-P_{0}|<\delta
, for some fixed
\delta>0
, uniformly as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
The function

\Gamma_{0}(y)=\log U(y)
satisfies the Liouville equation

(2.1)

-\Delta\Gamma_{0}=e^{\Gamma_{0}}=U(y)=\frac{8}{(1+|y|^{2})^{2}}{\quad\hbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{2}.

The precise derivation is carried out in Section §
3
, Proposition
3.1
.

The approximate leapfrogging solution for (
1.16
) will look at main order as the sum of
k
approximate vortex rings. For simplicity we rename the time-variable
\tau
in (
1.16
) to be
t
, so that
t\in[0,T]
. Let
k\geq 2
, and consider
k
points

P_{1}(t),\quad P_{2}(t),\quad\ldots\quad P_{k}(t),\quad P_{j}=(P_{j}^{1},P_{j}^{2})\in\Sigma

and
k
scaling parameters
\varepsilon_{1}(t),\ldots,\varepsilon_{k}(t)
.
We allow these points and these parameters to evolve in time, for
t\in[0,T]
and we assume they have the form

P_{j}=(r_{0},0)+{1\over\sqrt{|\log\varepsilon|}}q_{j}+\tilde{P}_{j}+{\bf a}_{j},\quad P_{j}^{1}(t)\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}(t)=r_{0}\,\varepsilon^{2}

with
q_{j}
satisfying the
reduced leapfrogging dynamics
(
1.14
) and

\sqrt{|\log\varepsilon|}\,|\tilde{P}_{j}|\to 0,\quad|{\bf a}_{j}|\lesssim\varepsilon^{3}\quad\quad\quad\mbox{as}\quad\varepsilon\to 0,

uniformly for
t\in[0,T]
. We refer to Subsection §
4.1
for the detailed description of these points and the scaling parameters.

To each point
P_{j}
, we associate the approximate vortex ring described before, namely a pair of stream function and vorticity
(\Psi_{j},W_{j})
, that then we add up together. This gives a good description of the approximate leapfrogging close to the points
P_{j}
, but it is far from satisfying the required boundary conditions

{\partial\over\partial r}\Psi(x,t)=0\quad{\mbox{on}}\quad\partial\Sigma\times[0,T),\quad|\Psi(x,t)|\to 0\quad\text{as }|x|\to\infty.

We use a cut-off function and correct the sum of stream functions to have at the same time the boundary conditions and the relation
-\Delta_{5}\Psi=W
satisfied.

For
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we set

(2.2)

\eta_{N}(s)=1,\quad{\mbox{for}}\quad s<N,\quad=0\quad{\mbox{for}}\quad s>2N

for a smooth cut-off function.
Define

\eta(x)=\eta_{1}\left({4|x-(r_{0},0)|\over\delta}\right),

with
\eta_{1}
as in (
2.2
) and
\delta<r_{0}
. In Section §
4
, Subsection §
4.2
, we prove the existence of a smooth function
H^{0}(x,t)
, which is uniformly bounded, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, for
(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T]
so that the pair

\Psi^{0}(x,t)=\eta(x)\,\sum_{j=1}^{k}\Psi_{j}+H^{0}(x,t),\quad{\mbox{and}}\quad W^{0}(x,t)=\eta(x)\,\sum_{j=1}^{k}W_{j},

is defined for
(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T]
and satisfies

{\partial\over\partial r}\Psi^{0}(x,t)=0\quad{\mbox{on}}\quad\partial\Sigma\times[0,T),\quad|\Psi^{0}(x,t)|\to 0\quad\text{as }|x|\to\infty,\quad-\Delta_{5}\Psi^{0}=W^{0}\quad(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T].

Let us define the Euler operators

(2.3)

\displaystyle S_{1}(W,\Psi)

\displaystyle=|\log\varepsilon|r\partial_{t}W+{\nabla}^{\perp}(r^{2}(\Psi-r_{0}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|))\cdot{\nabla}W,\quad

\displaystyle S_{2}(W,\Psi)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{5}\Psi+W.

Hence

S_{2}(W^{0},\Psi^{0})=0,\quad\quad(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T].

Besides
(\Psi^{0},W^{0})
is a good approximate solution to (
1.16
) around each point
P_{j}
in the following sense.
For any
j=1,\ldots,k
, consider the small ball around
P_{j}
of radius
|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}
,
B(P_{j},|\log\varepsilon|^{-1})
. Under our assumptions on the points
P_{j}
and the scaling parameters
\varepsilon_{j}
, one has that

B(P_{i},|\log\varepsilon|^{-1})\cap B(P_{j},|\log\varepsilon|^{-1})=\emptyset,\quad{\mbox{for}}\quad i\not=j,

for all
\varepsilon
small enough, at any
t\in[0,T]
.
In Subsections §
4.3
and §
4.4
we derive

\displaystyle\left|\,S_{1}(W^{0},\Psi^{0})\,(x,t)\,\right|\,

\displaystyle=\,O\left({\varepsilon^{-2}|\log\varepsilon|\over 1+|y|^{4}}\right),\quad x=(r,z)

where

\displaystyle y={x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon},\quad{\mbox{for}}\quad x\in B(P_{j},|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}),\,t\in[0,T].

This estimate can be achieved thanks to the choice of the points
P_{j}
at their main order, namely
P_{j}=P_{0}+{1\over\sqrt{|\log\varepsilon|}}q_{j}
, and the fact that
q_{j}
solve (
1.14
). Observe that on the boundary of the region
B(P_{j},|\log\varepsilon|^{-1})
one has
|S_{1}(W^{0},\Psi^{0})|=O(\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{5})
, while it is of order
O(\varepsilon^{-2}|\log\varepsilon|)
close around each
P_{j}
.

Next we modify
(W^{0},\Psi^{0})
in order to produce a better approximate solution
(W^{*},\Psi^{*})
.

In Section §
5
and §
6
we prove the existence of points
\tilde{P}_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,k
in the decomposition of
P_{j}=(r_{0},0)+{1\over\sqrt{|\log\varepsilon|}}q_{j}+\tilde{P}_{j}+{\bf a}_{j}
, and functions

\psi_{j}^{*},\,\phi_{j}^{*},\quad j=1,\ldots,k,\,\quad\psi^{*,out},\,\phi^{*,out}

so that

(W^{*},\Psi^{*})
given by

(2.4)

\displaystyle\Psi^{*}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\Psi^{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\eta_{j2}\over r_{j}}\psi_{j}^{*}({x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}},t)+\psi^{*,out}(x,t)

\displaystyle W^{*}(x,t)

\displaystyle=W^{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\eta_{j1}\over r_{j}\varepsilon_{j}^{2}}\phi_{j}^{*}({x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}},t)+\phi^{*,out}(x,t)

where

\eta_{jN}(x,t)=\eta_{N}\left(|\log\varepsilon|^{3}\,|x-P_{j}|\right),

is a better approximate leapfrogging. The function
\eta_{N}
is defined in (
2.2
).

In fact, the boundary conditions are satisfied and we get that

\displaystyle|S_{1}(W^{*},\Psi^{*})(x,t)|\ \leq

\displaystyle C\varepsilon^{1-\sigma_{*}}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\frac{1}{1+|y_{j}|^{3}}+C{\varepsilon^{4-{2\sigma_{*}}}\over 1+|x|^{4}},\quad y_{j}=\frac{x-P_{j}(t)}{\varepsilon},

\displaystyle|S_{2}(W^{*},\Psi^{*})(x,t)|\ \leq

\displaystyle C\varepsilon^{4-\sigma_{*}}\eta_{1}\left({4|x-(r_{0},0)|\over r_{0}}\right),

for all
x\in\Sigma
and
t\in[0,T]
, and
|y_{j}|<\varepsilon^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|^{-3}
. Here

\eta_{1}
is given by (
2.2
) and
\sigma_{*}
is a fixed positive number. The functions
(W^{*},\Psi^{*})
depend on the points
{\bf a}_{j}
which are left as parameters to adjust later. Notice that on the boundary of the region
B(P_{j},|\log\varepsilon|^{-1})
one has
|S_{1}(W^{*},\Psi^{*})|=O(\varepsilon^{4-{2\sigma_{*}}})
, while it is of order
O(\varepsilon^{1-\sigma^{*}})
close to each
P_{j}
.

Proposition
5.1
contains a precise description of the error term

S_{1}(W^{*},\Psi^{*})

in the inner regions close to the points
P_{j}
, at distance
|x-P_{j}|\lesssim|\log\varepsilon|^{-3}
, for each
j=1,\ldots,k
, as well as in the complementary outer region. Observe that the inner regions
|x-P_{j}|\lesssim|\log\varepsilon|^{-3}
well separate the points
P_{j}
, whose relative distance is of order
|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}}
. The modification to the original stream function
\Psi^{0}
is given by

\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\eta_{j2}\over r_{j}}\psi_{j}^{*}({x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}},t)+\psi^{*,out}(x,t).

The terms
\psi_{j}^{*}
are functions of the expanded variables
y={x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}}
and encode the local correction needed to improve the approximation near the points
P_{j}
. The cut-off functions
\eta_{j2}
(as well as
\eta_{j1}
for the vorticity) are designed in such a way to guarantee that
\Psi^{0}
is still the main term in the decomposition of
\Psi^{*}
in the region where the corresponding cut-off functions are non-zero.
The function
\psi^{*,out}
is a more regular function, expressed in terms of the original variable
x
and it is responsible of the improvement of the size of the error far from the points
P_{j}
. Similar decomposition describes the modification of the initial vorticity
W^{0}
.

In Section §
5
we describe how we find the
improvement
(W^{*},\Psi^{*})
and we describe the error of approximation. We make this statement precise in Proposition
5.1
. The proof of Proposition
5.1
is contained in Section §
6
. The construction of the approximation requires several refinements in regions close to the centers, which we call
inner improvements
(to get
\psi_{j}^{*}
and
\phi_{j}^{*}
), and one global adjustment in the region far from the centers, the
outer improvement
(to get
\psi^{*,out}
and
\phi^{*,out}
). At the beginning of Section §
6
, in Subsection §
6.1
, we describe the general strategy for improvement and then we proceed with the detailed description of each step in Subsections §
6.2
to §
6.12
.
For the sixth inner improvement and for the outer improvement we solve two linear transport equations. We study them respectively in Sections §
7
and §
8
.

Solving the full problem.

We look for a leapfrogging solution of equations (
1.16
) of the form

(2.5)

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=W^{*}+\varphi

\displaystyle\Psi

\displaystyle=\Psi^{*}+\psi

where
\varphi
and
\psi
are small corrections of the previously found approximation. It is in order to find
\varphi
and
\psi
that we
set up an inner-outer gluing scheme.
The solution
(W,\,\Psi)
will have the form (
2.5
) with

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\bar{\eta}_{j1}{1\over r_{j}\varepsilon_{j}^{2}}\phi_{j}({x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}},t)+\phi^{out}(x,t)

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\bar{\eta}_{j2}{1\over r_{j}}\psi_{j}({x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}},t)+\psi^{out}(x,t)

where

\bar{\eta}_{jN}(x,t)=\eta_{N}(|\log\varepsilon|^{5}|x-P_{j}|),

and
\eta_{N}
as in (
2.2
).

Comparing with (
2.4
), you may notice that this ansatz has the same form as the one used for the construction of the improved approximate leapfrogging of vortex rings
(W^{*},\Psi^{*})
. Observe though that here we are taking cut-offs slightly shorter than the ones taken in the definition of
(W^{*},\Psi^{*})
.

Let
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
be the Euler operators introduced in (
2.3
).
Then the operator
S_{1}
evaluated at
(W,\Psi)
becomes

\displaystyle S_{1}(W,\Psi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\bar{\eta}_{1j}\over\varepsilon_{j}^{4}}E_{j}[\phi_{j},\psi_{j}\,\psi^{out},P]+E^{out}[\phi^{out},\Psi^{out},\phi^{in},\psi^{in},P]

where

\displaystyle\phi^{in}(y,t)=(\phi_{1}(y,t),\ldots,\phi_{k}(y,t)),\quad\psi^{in}(y,t)=(\psi_{1}(y,t),\ldots,\psi_{k}(y,t)).

The operators
E_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,k
, and
E^{out}
are defined respectively

\displaystyle E_{j}^{in}

\displaystyle[\phi_{j},\psi_{j}\,\psi^{out},{\bf a}](y,t):=|\log\varepsilon|\varepsilon_{j}^{2}(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}){\partial}_{t}\phi_{j}-|\log\varepsilon|\left(\varepsilon_{j}\dot{\varepsilon_{j}}(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})\nabla\phi_{j}\cdot y-{\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\dot{P}_{j}\cdot\nabla\phi_{j}\right)

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\perp}\left((1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})^{2}(\Psi_{j}-|\log\varepsilon|+\psi_{j}^{*}+\psi_{j}+r_{j}\psi^{out})-\varepsilon_{j}|\log\varepsilon|\dot{{\bf a}_{j}}\right)\cdot\nabla\phi_{j}

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\perp}\left((1+{\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}\over r_{j}})^{2}(\psi_{j}+r_{j}\psi^{out})\right)\nabla(\varepsilon_{j}^{2}r_{j}W^{*})

\displaystyle+\varepsilon_{j}^{4}S_{1}(W^{*},\Psi^{*})(\varepsilon_{j}y+P_{j}),\quad|y|<3R,\quad R:={1\over\varepsilon|\log\varepsilon|^{5}}

with
\dot{}={d\over dt}
and
y={x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}}
, and

\displaystyle E^{out}

\displaystyle[\phi^{out},\Psi^{out},\phi^{in},\psi^{in},{\bf a}](x,t):=|\log\varepsilon|\,r\,\phi^{out}_{t}

\displaystyle+\nabla_{x}^{\perp}(r^{2}(\Psi^{*}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\bar{\eta}_{j2}\over r_{j}}\psi_{j}({x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}})+\psi^{out}-r_{0}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|))\nabla_{x}\phi^{out}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\left[r\,|\log\varepsilon|\,{\partial}_{t}\bar{\eta}_{j1}+\nabla_{x}^{\perp}(r^{2}(\Psi^{*}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\bar{\eta}_{j2}\over r_{j}}\psi_{j}({x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}})+\psi^{out}-r_{0}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|))\nabla\bar{\eta}_{1j}\right]{\phi_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}^{2}r_{j}}

\displaystyle+\left[\sum_{j=1}^{k}(\bar{\eta}_{2j}-\bar{\eta}_{1j})\nabla_{x}^{\perp}(r^{2}({\psi_{j}\over r_{j}}+\psi^{out}))+{r^{2}\psi_{j}\over r_{j}}\nabla_{x}^{\perp}\bar{\eta}_{2j}\right]\nabla_{x}W^{*}

\displaystyle+(1-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\bar{\eta}_{2j})\nabla^{\perp}(r^{2}\psi^{out})\cdot\nabla W^{*}+(1-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\bar{\eta}_{j1})S_{1}(W^{*},\Psi^{*})=0\quad(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T).

With all this set up, we notice that the pair
(W,\Psi)
is a solution to (
1.16
) if
(\phi^{in},\psi^{in},\phi^{out},\psi^{out})
solve
the
inner-outer gluing
system given by the inner problems

\displaystyle E_{j}^{in}

\displaystyle[\phi_{j},\psi_{j}\,\psi^{out},{\bf a}](y,t)=0,\quad(y,t)\in B(0;3R)\times[0,T)

\displaystyle-\Delta_{y}\psi_{j}

\displaystyle-{3\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}}{\partial}_{y_{1}}\psi_{j}=\phi_{j},\quad(y,t)\in B(0;3R)\times[0,T)

for all
j=1,\ldots,k
, coupled with the outer problem

\displaystyle E^{out}

\displaystyle[\phi^{out},\psi^{out},\phi^{in},\psi^{in},{\bf a}](x,t)=0,\quad(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T)

\displaystyle E_{1}^{out}

\displaystyle[\phi^{out},\psi^{out},\phi^{in},\psi^{in},{\bf a}](x,t)=0,\quad(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T)

where

\displaystyle E_{1}^{out}

\displaystyle:=\Delta_{5}\psi^{out}+\phi^{out}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}(\bar{\eta}_{j1}-\bar{\eta}_{j2}){\phi_{j}\over r_{j}\varepsilon_{j}^{2}}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}({\psi_{j}\over r_{j}}\Delta_{5}\bar{\eta}_{j2}+2\nabla_{x}\bar{\eta}_{j2}\nabla_{x}{\psi_{j}\over r_{j}}),\quad(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T)

coupled with the boundary and decay conditions on
\psi^{out}

{{\partial}\over{\partial}r}\psi^{out}(x,t)=0,\quad(x,t)\in{\partial}\Sigma\times[0,T],\quad|\psi^{out}(x,t)|\to 0,\quad\quad\mbox{as}\quad|x|\to\infty.

The solution predicted by Theorem
1
is obtained solving the inner-outer gluing system

\displaystyle E_{j}^{in}

\displaystyle[\phi_{j},\psi_{j}\,\psi^{out},{\bf a}](y,t)=0,\quad(y,t)\in B(0;3R)\times[0,T)

\displaystyle-\Delta_{y}\psi_{j}

\displaystyle-{3\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}}{\partial}_{y_{1}}\psi_{j}=\phi_{j},\quad(y,t)\in B(0;3R)\times[0,T),\quad j=1,\ldots,k

\displaystyle E^{out}

\displaystyle[\phi^{out},\psi^{out},\phi^{in},\psi^{in},{\bf a}](x,t)=0,\quad(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T)

\displaystyle E_{1}^{out}

\displaystyle[\phi^{out},\psi^{out},\phi^{in},\psi^{in},{\bf a}](x,t)=0,\quad(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T)

\displaystyle{{\partial}\over{\partial}r}\psi^{out}

\displaystyle(x,t)=0,\quad(x,t)\in{\partial}\Sigma\times[0,T],\quad|\psi^{out}(x,t)|\to 0,\quad\quad\mbox{as}\quad|x|\to\infty.

In
order to obtain the desired solution (with initial conditions equal to zero in all the
parameter functions) we will formulate the system as a fixed point problem for a
compact operator in a ball of a suitable Banach space. We will find a solution
by means of a degree theoretical argument. That involves establishing a priori
estimates for a homotopical deformation of the problem into a linear one. These arguments are performed in Sections §
10
and §
11
.
The rest of this paper is devoted to carrying out in detail the steps outlined
above.

3.
Approximate travelling vortex ring

In this section we define the basic building block for the construction of the leapfrogging. This object is an approximate travelling vortex ring with
highly
\varepsilon
-concentrated vorticity near a point
P\in\Sigma
. It is achieved finding
a constant
\alpha
and a stream function
\Psi_{\varepsilon}
”almost” solving in a neighbourhood of
P
the equations for the travelling vortex ring

(3.1)

\displaystyle S_{\alpha}(W_{\varepsilon},\Psi_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle:={\nabla}^{\perp}(r^{2}(\Psi_{\varepsilon}-\alpha|\log\varepsilon|))\cdot{\nabla}W_{\varepsilon}\sim 0,\quad{\mbox{where}}\quad W_{\varepsilon}=-\Delta_{5}\Psi_{\varepsilon}

where
\Delta_{5}
is the operator introduced in (
1.6
). We recall it here

\Delta_{5}\Psi=\partial_{rr}\Psi+\frac{3}{r}\partial_{r}\Psi+\partial_{zz}\Psi,\quad x=(r,z).

The point
P
represents the centre of a travelling ring, and we take it of the form

P=(\bar{r},\bar{z})=P_{0}+q,\quad P_{0}=(r_{0},0),

with
r_{0}>0
a fixed number, and
|q|\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. In accordance with the discussion in the introduction, it is expected that the vorticity
rW_{\varepsilon}
satisfies

(3.2)

r\,W_{\varepsilon}(r,z)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}U\left(\frac{x-P}{\varepsilon}\right)(1+o(1)),\quad x=(r,z)

where
U
is the rapidly decaying function in (
1.12
),
and that the constant
\alpha
satisfies
\alpha={1\over r_{0}}(1+o(1))
, with
o(1)\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. The associated stream function
\Psi_{\varepsilon}
will correspond to an
\varepsilon
-regularization of
the following Green’s function

-\Delta_{5}G(x;P)=8\pi\delta_{P},\quad{\partial\over\partial r}G(x;P)=0,\quad{\mbox{on}}\quad\partial\Sigma,\quad G(x;P)\to 0\quad\mbox{as}\quad|x|\to\infty.

We write the Green’s function
G
as

G(x;P)=\log\frac{1}{|x-P|^{4}}\big(1-\frac{3}{2\bar{r}}(r-\bar{r})+H(x;P)\big)+K(x;P),

where
H(\cdot;P)
and
K(\cdot;P)
satisfy respectively

(3.3)

\Delta_{5}\Big(\log\frac{1}{|x-P|^{4}}H(x;P)\Big)=-30\frac{(r-\bar{r})^{2}}{r\bar{r}|x-P|^{2}}+\frac{9}{2r\bar{r}}\log\frac{1}{|x-P|^{4}},

and

(3.4)

\Delta_{5}K(x;P)=0.

Let the
\varepsilon
-regularization of the Green’s function
G
be given by

(3.5)

G_{\varepsilon}(x;P):=\log\frac{1}{(\varepsilon^{2}+|x-P|^{2})^{2}}\big(1-\frac{3}{2\bar{r}}(r-\bar{r})+H(x;P)\big)+K(x;P).

Inserting this approximation in the operator defined in (
3.1
) produces a term of size
\varepsilon
in a neighborhood of
P
, when expressed in the expanded variable
y={x-P\over\varepsilon}
. We reduce the size to
\varepsilon^{2}
slightly modifying the approximation in a region close to
P
. For this purpose, we introduce the constant
A
given by the relation

(3.6)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y_{1}U(y)\left(\Gamma_{0}+A\right)\,{\partial\Gamma_{0}\over\partial y_{1}}\,dy=0,

where
\Gamma_{0}
is given in (
2.1
),
and the function
\Gamma:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
defined as

(3.7)

\Gamma(y):=\int_{\rho}^{\infty}{(1+\eta^{2})^{2}\over\eta^{3}}\int_{0}^{\eta}{s^{3}\over 1+s^{2}}U(s)(\Gamma_{0}(s)+A)\,ds\,d\eta,\quad\rho=|y|.

A direct computation gives that
\Gamma
solves

\Delta(y_{1}\Gamma)+U\,y_{1}\,\Gamma+y_{1}U(y)\left(\Gamma_{0}+A\right)=0\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}\quad\mathbb{R}^{2}

and satisfies

\Gamma(y)=O\left({\log(1+|y|)\over 1+|y|}\right),\quad\quad\mbox{as}\quad|y|\to\infty.

We make our construction precise in the next Proposition.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
r_{0}
be a fixed positive number and
P=(\bar{r},\bar{z})=P_{0}+q
, with
P_{0}=(r_{0},0)
and
|q|\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
Define
\alpha=\alpha(\varepsilon,P)
as

(3.8)

\alpha={1\over\bar{r}}-{A+\log 8-6-2\bar{r}{\partial}_{r}K(P;P)-4K(P;P)\over 4\bar{r}|\log\varepsilon|},

where
A
is given by (
3.6
), and
\Psi_{\varepsilon}[P](x)
as

(3.9)

\displaystyle\bar{r}\,\Psi_{\varepsilon}[P](x)

\displaystyle=G_{\varepsilon}(x;P)+{r-\bar{r}\over 2\bar{r}}\Gamma({x-P\over\varepsilon}),

where
G_{\varepsilon}(x;P)
is in (
3.5
) and
\Gamma
in (
3.7
).
Set

W_{\varepsilon}[P](x)=-\Delta_{5}\Psi_{\varepsilon}[P](x).

Then
for any fixed
\delta>0
small and any
x
with
|x-P|<\delta
,
setting

y={x-P\over\varepsilon}=\rho\,e^{i\theta},\quad|y|<{\delta\over\varepsilon},

S_{\alpha}(W_{\varepsilon},\Psi_{\varepsilon})
as defined in (
3.1
) has the following expansion

(3.10)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{4}

\displaystyle S_{\alpha}(W_{\varepsilon},\Psi_{\varepsilon})(\varepsilon y+P)={\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\over 1+|y|^{4}}E_{2}(\rho,\theta,\varepsilon,P)

\displaystyle+{\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|\over 1+|y|^{3}}|\log(\varepsilon(1+|y|))|[E_{1}(\rho,\theta,\varepsilon,P)+E_{3}(\rho,\theta,\varepsilon,P)]

\displaystyle+O\left({\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}\over 1+|y|^{2}}\,\right),\quad\quad\mbox{as}\quad\varepsilon\to 0.

Here we have written
E_{k}(\rho,\theta,\varepsilon,P)
for a function of the form

(3.11)

\displaystyle E_{k}(\rho,\theta,\varepsilon,P)=E_{k,1}(\rho,\varepsilon,P)\cos(k\theta)+E_{k,2}(\rho,\varepsilon,P)\sin(k\theta)

where

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{2}\Bigl|(1+\rho)^{j}\frac{\partial^{j}E_{k,i}}{\partial\rho^{j}}\Bigr|+\Bigl|\nabla_{P}E_{k,i}\Bigl|\lesssim 1,\quad\quad\mbox{as}\quad\varepsilon\to 0.

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

Let us fix
\delta>0
and consider the region of points
x
with
|x-P|<\delta
. We use the expanded variable

y={x-P\over\varepsilon},\quad P=(\bar{r},\bar{z})

which we also identify with polar coordinates
y=\rho e^{i\theta}
,
\rho=|y|
.

From the definition of
\Psi_{\varepsilon}
given in (
3.9
) we get the following expansions, for
|y|<{\delta\over\varepsilon}
,

(3.12)

\displaystyle\Psi_{\varepsilon}[P](x)

\displaystyle={1\over\bar{r}}\psi^{0}(y),\quad{\mbox{where}}

\displaystyle\psi^{0}(y)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{0}(y)-4\log\varepsilon-\log 8+K(P;P)

\displaystyle+\frac{\varepsilon y_{1}}{2\bar{r}}\Big(-3\Gamma_{0}(y)+A_{\varepsilon}-4K(P,P)+\Gamma(y)\Big)\,+\varepsilon^{2}\theta_{1}[P](y)+\varepsilon^{3}\theta_{2}[P](y),

and

(3.13)

\displaystyle\frac{r^{2}}{\bar{r}}\Big(\Psi_{\varepsilon}[P](x)-\alpha|\log\varepsilon|\Big)=\Gamma_{0}(y)-(4-\alpha\bar{r})\log\varepsilon-\log 8+K(P;P)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2\bar{r}}\Big(\Gamma_{0}(y)+\bar{A}+\Gamma(y)\Big)\,\varepsilon y_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}\theta_{1}[P](y)+\varepsilon^{3}\theta_{2}[P](y).

In the above expansions,
\bar{A}
and
A_{\varepsilon}
are constants given by

(3.14)

\bar{A}=A-6,\quad A_{\varepsilon}=4(4-\alpha\bar{r})\log\varepsilon+\bar{A}+4\log 8

and
\Gamma=\Gamma(y)
is as in (
3.7
). Moreover
\theta_{1}[P](y)
and
\theta_{2}[P](y)
denote generic reminders with the following form

(3.15)

\displaystyle\theta_{1}[P](y)

\displaystyle=\Biggl[a(P)+b_{1}(P)\cos 2\theta+b_{2}(P)\sin 2\theta

\displaystyle+|\log(\varepsilon(1+|y|))|(a(P)+b_{1}(P)\cos 2\theta+b_{2}(P)\sin 2\theta)\Biggl]\,O(|y|^{2})

and

(3.16)

\theta_{2}[P](y)=a(P)O(|y|^{3}|\log(\varepsilon(1+|y|)|))

for
a
,
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
smooth functions of
P
, uniformly bounded as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
Here
\Gamma_{0}(y)=\log U(y)
as in (
2.1
).

Formula (
3.12
) gives the asymptotic expansion of the stream function
\Psi_{\varepsilon}
associated to our approximate vortex ring. In order to describe the asymptotic expansion of the vorticity
W_{\varepsilon}
, let us introduce

(3.17)

f(r^{2}(\Psi-\alpha|\log\varepsilon|))={8\over\bar{r}}e^{-K(P;P)}\varepsilon^{2-\alpha\bar{r}}f_{0}\Big(\frac{r^{2}}{\bar{r}}(\Psi-\alpha|\log\varepsilon|)\Big),\quad{\mbox{with}}\quad f_{0}(s)=e^{s}

and

(3.18)

\displaystyle E[\Psi](x)

\displaystyle:=\Delta_{5}\Psi+f(r^{2}(\Psi-\alpha|\log\varepsilon|)).

The proof of Proposition
3.1
follows from showing that
\alpha
and
\Psi_{\varepsilon}
are so that the following expansion for
E[\Psi_{\varepsilon}]
, given by (
3.18
)-(
3.17
), holds true:
for all
|y|<{\delta\over\varepsilon}
we have

(3.19)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}\,\bar{r}\,E[\Psi_{\varepsilon}](x)

\displaystyle={\varepsilon^{2}\over 1+|y|^{2}}\Biggl[a(P)+b_{1}(P)\cos 2\theta+b_{2}(P)\sin 2\theta

\displaystyle+|\log(\varepsilon(1+|y|))|(a(P)+b_{1}(P)\cos 2\theta+b_{2}(P)\sin 2\theta)\Biggl]

\displaystyle+O({\varepsilon^{3}\over 1+|y|})|\log(\varepsilon(|y|+1))|,\,\quad

uniformly as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Here

a
,
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
denote smooth functions of
P
, uniformly bounded together with their derivative as
\varepsilon\to 0
, whose definition may change from line to line.

Assume expansion (
3.19
) is true. Since
W_{\varepsilon}[P]=-\Delta_{5}\Psi_{\varepsilon}[P]
, we have

S_{\alpha}(W_{\varepsilon},\Psi_{\varepsilon})=\nabla^{\perp}(r^{2}(\Psi_{\varepsilon}-\alpha|\log\varepsilon|))\cdot\nabla(E[\Psi_{\varepsilon}])

and expansion (
3.10
) readily follows from
(
3.13
) and (
3.19
).

We also observe that, in the region
|y|<{\delta\over\varepsilon}
, the vorticity of the approximate vortex ring can be described as

(3.20)

\displaystyle W_{\varepsilon}[P](x)

\displaystyle={1\over\varepsilon^{2}\bar{r}}w_{\varepsilon}[P](y)

\displaystyle w_{\varepsilon}[P](y)

\displaystyle=U(y)\left(1+{\varepsilon y_{1}\over 2\bar{r}}(\Gamma_{0}+\bar{A}+\Gamma)+\varepsilon^{2}\theta_{1}[P](y)+\varepsilon^{3}\theta_{2}[P](y)\right)

where
U(y)
and
\Gamma_{0}(y)
are defined in (
2.1
),
\bar{A}
in (
3.14
),
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
in (
3.15
) and (
3.16
).
Hence
rW_{\varepsilon}(x)
approaches, locally around
P
, a Dirac delta, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, in accordance with the expectation (
3.2
).
We will make use of these estimates in the next Section.

As we explained before, we want to prove the validity of (
3.19
).
We start with the observation that, since the operator in (
3.18
) is invariant under translations in the
z
-direction, it is not restrictive to assume that
P=(\bar{r},0)
and to work in the class of functions that are even in the variable
z
.

To simplify notation, we drop the dependence on
P
in the functions
G_{\varepsilon}
,
H
and
K
.

Then for
|x-P|<\delta
we have

G_{\varepsilon}(x)=(\Gamma_{0}(y)-4\log(\varepsilon)-\log 8)\Big(1-\frac{3}{2\bar{r}}\varepsilon y_{1}+H(P+\varepsilon y)\Big)+K(P+\varepsilon y),\quad y={x-P\over\varepsilon}

where we mean
H(P+\varepsilon y)=H(P+\varepsilon y;P)
, etc. Observe that
{\partial}_{z}K(P)=0
by symmetry.
From (
3.3
) we get that
H(x;P)
has the following expansion as
x\to P

(3.21)

\displaystyle H(x)=a_{1}(P)(r-\bar{r})^{2}+a_{2}(P)z^{2}+b(P){|x-P|^{2}\over\log|x-P|^{2}}+O(|x-P|^{3})

where
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
,
b
are constants whose value depends on
P
.
Using (
3.21
), we expand

\displaystyle G_{\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{0}(y)-4\log\varepsilon-\log 8+K(P)-\frac{3}{2\bar{r}}\left[\Gamma_{0}(y)-4\log\varepsilon-\log 8-{2\bar{r}\over 3}\partial_{r}K(P)\right]\varepsilon y_{1}

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{2}\theta_{1}[P](y)+\varepsilon^{3}\theta_{2}[P](y)

where
\theta_{1}
,
\theta_{2}
are reminders that can be described as in (
3.15
) and (
3.16
).

For
\Psi_{P}(x)=\frac{1}{\bar{r}}G_{\varepsilon}(x),

we get

\displaystyle\frac{r^{2}}{\bar{r}}\Big(\Psi_{P}(x)-\alpha|\log\varepsilon|\Big)=\Gamma_{0}(y)-(4-\alpha\bar{r})\log\varepsilon-\log 8+K(P)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2\bar{r}}\Big(\Gamma_{0}(y)-(4-4\alpha\bar{r})\log\varepsilon+2\bar{r}\partial_{r}K(P)+4K(P)-\log(8)\Big)\,\varepsilon y_{1}

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{2}\theta_{1}[P](y)+\varepsilon^{3}\theta_{2}[P](y),

and

(3.22)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle 8\over\bar{r}}e^{-K(P)}\varepsilon^{2-\alpha\bar{r}}f\Big(\frac{r^{2}}{\bar{r}}(\Psi_{P}(x)-\alpha|\log\varepsilon|)\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}\bar{r}}U(y)\exp\Big[\frac{1}{2\bar{r}}\Big(\Gamma_{0}(y)-(4-4\alpha\bar{r})\log\varepsilon+2\bar{r}\partial_{r}K(P)+4K(P)-\log 8\Big)\,\varepsilon y_{1}

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{2}\theta_{1}[P](y)+\varepsilon^{3}\theta_{2}[P](y)\Big],

for functions
\theta_{1}
,
\theta_{2}
of the form (
3.15
) and (
3.16
).

Next we compute

\displaystyle\Delta_{5}\Psi_{P}=(1)+(2)

where

\displaystyle(1)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\bar{r}}\Delta_{5}\Big[(\Gamma_{0}({x-P\over\varepsilon})-4\log\varepsilon-\log 8)\Big(1-\frac{3}{2\bar{r}}(r-\bar{r})\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle(2)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\bar{r}}\Delta_{5}[(\Gamma_{0}({x-P\over\varepsilon})-4\log\varepsilon-\log 8)H],

since by definition
\Delta_{5}K=0
.
Setting again
\varepsilon y=x-P
, we get

\displaystyle(1)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\bar{r}}\Big[\Big(-\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}U(y)+\frac{3}{\varepsilon(\bar{r}+\varepsilon y_{1})}\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}(y)\frac{y_{1}}{\rho}\Big)\Big(1-\frac{3}{2\bar{r}}\varepsilon\ y_{1}\Big)-\frac{3}{\varepsilon\bar{r}}\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}(\rho)\frac{y_{1}}{\rho}-\frac{9}{2r\bar{r}}(\Gamma_{0}-4\log\varepsilon-\log 8)\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\bar{r}}\Big[-\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}U(y)+\frac{3}{2\varepsilon\bar{r}}U(y)y_{1}-\frac{15}{2}\frac{\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}(\rho)}{\rho}\frac{y_{1}^{2}}{r\bar{r}}-\frac{9}{2r\bar{r}}(\Gamma_{0}-4\log\varepsilon-\log 8)\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}\bar{r}}\Big[-U(y)+\frac{3}{2\bar{r}}\varepsilon U(y)y_{1}+30{(r-\bar{r})^{2}\over\bar{r}r}{\varepsilon^{2}\over\varepsilon^{2}+|x-\bar{P}|^{2}}-\frac{9}{2r\bar{r}}\varepsilon^{2}\log{1\over(\varepsilon^{2}+|x-\bar{P}|^{2})^{2}}\Big].

From (
3.3
)
we get

\displaystyle(2)

\displaystyle=-30\frac{(r-\bar{r})^{2}}{r\bar{r}^{2}|x-P|^{2}}+\frac{9}{2r\bar{r}^{2}}\log\frac{1}{|x-P|^{4}}+\frac{1}{\bar{r}}\Delta_{5}[(\Gamma_{0}({x-P\over\varepsilon})-4\log\varepsilon-\log 8-\log{1\over|x-P|^{4}})H]

\displaystyle=-30\frac{(r-\bar{r})^{2}}{r\bar{r}^{2}|x-P|^{2}}+\frac{9}{2r\bar{r}^{2}}\log\frac{1}{|x-P|^{4}}+\frac{2}{\bar{r}}\Delta_{5}[\log{|{x-P\over\varepsilon}|^{2}\over 1+|{x-P\over\varepsilon}|^{2}}H]

Combining the above expressions we conclude that

(3.23)

\displaystyle-\Delta_{5}\Psi_{P}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\bar{r}}\Big[\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}U(y)-\frac{3}{\varepsilon\bar{r}}U(y)y_{1}+O(\frac{1}{1+\rho^{2}})\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\bar{r}\varepsilon^{2}}U(y)\Big[1-\frac{3}{\bar{r}}\varepsilon y_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}\theta_{1}[P](y)+\varepsilon^{3}\theta_{2}[P](y)\Big],

where
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
are described in (
3.15
) and (
3.16
).

Putting together (
3.22
) and (
3.23
) we find that

(3.24)

\varepsilon^{2}E[\Psi_{P}]=\frac{1}{\bar{r}}[\varepsilon E_{0}+\Theta_{\varepsilon}(y)]

where

\displaystyle E_{0}(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{y_{1}}{2\bar{r}}U(y)\Big(\Gamma_{0}(y)-4(1-\alpha\bar{r})\log\varepsilon+2\bar{r}\partial_{r}K(P)+4K(P)-\log 8+6\Big),

and

\displaystyle\Theta_{\varepsilon}(y)

\displaystyle=U(y)\exp\Big[\frac{1}{2\bar{r}}\Big(\Gamma_{0}(y)-(4-4\alpha\bar{r})\log\varepsilon+2\bar{r}\partial_{r}K(P)+4K(P)-\log 8\Big)\,\varepsilon y_{1}

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{2}\theta_{1}[P](y)+\varepsilon^{3}\theta_{2}[P](y)\Big]

\displaystyle-U(y)\Big[1+\frac{1}{2\bar{r}}\Big(\Gamma_{0}(y)-(4-4\alpha\bar{r})\log\varepsilon+2\bar{r}\partial_{r}K(P)+4K(P)-\log 8\Big)\,\varepsilon y_{1}\Big]

\displaystyle+U(y)\left[\varepsilon^{2}\theta_{1}[P](y)+\varepsilon^{3}\theta_{2}[P](y)\right],

where
\theta_{1}
,
\theta_{2}
denote again generic functions of the form (
3.15
), (
3.16
).
A closer look at this expression
gives that
\Theta_{\varepsilon}(y)
can be described as follows

(3.25)

\displaystyle\Theta_{\varepsilon}(y)

\displaystyle={\varepsilon^{2}\over 1+|y|^{2}}\Biggl[a(P)+b_{1}(P)\cos 2\theta+b_{2}(P)\sin 2\theta

\displaystyle+|\log(\varepsilon(1+|y|))|(a(P)+b_{1}(P)\cos 2\theta+b_{2}(P)\sin 2\theta)\Biggl]

\displaystyle+O({\varepsilon^{3}\over 1+|y|})\log(\varepsilon(1+|y|))a(P)

uniformly as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Also here

a
,
b_{1}
,
b_{2}
stand for generic smooth functions of
P
, uniformly bounded as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

In order to reduce the size of the error term in (
3.24
) we solve

(3.26)

\displaystyle\Delta\bar{\psi}+U(y)\bar{\psi}+\varepsilon E_{0}(y)=0\quad{\mbox{in}}\quad\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\lim_{|y|\to\infty}\bar{\psi}(y)=0.

It is here when we introduce the function
\Gamma
and we use the explicit definition of
\alpha
as given in (
3.8
).

It is known that all bounded solutions to

\Delta\bar{\psi}+U(y)\bar{\psi}=0\quad{\mbox{in}}\quad\mathbb{R}^{2}

are given by linear combinations of

\frac{\partial\Gamma_{0}}{{\partial}y_{i}}(y),\quad i=1,2,\quad 2+\nabla\Gamma_{0}\cdot y.

This result can be found in
[
4
]
. By the standard Fredholm alternative for this problem we need
the following solvability condition satisfied

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E_{0}(y)\frac{\partial\Gamma_{0}}{{\partial}y_{1}}(y)\,dy\ =\ 0.

Infact, the error term
E_{0}
is by definition even with respect to the variable
y_{2}
. Hence
the remaining solvability conditions

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E_{0}(y)\frac{\partial\Gamma_{0}}{{\partial}y_{2}}(y)\,dy\ =\ 0,\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E_{0}(y)\left(2+\nabla\Gamma_{0}\cdot y\right)\,dy\ =\ 0

are automatically satisfied by symmetry.

Let us then compute

\displaystyle 2\bar{r}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E_{0}(y)\frac{\partial\Gamma_{0}}{{\partial}y_{1}}(y)\,dy\

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(y)y_{1}\frac{\partial\Gamma_{0}}{{\partial}y_{1}}(y)\,dy\right)(2\bar{r}\partial_{r}K(P)+4K(P)-\log 8+6-4(1-\alpha\bar{r})\log\varepsilon)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(y)y_{1}\frac{\partial\Gamma_{0}}{{\partial}y_{1}}(y)\Gamma_{0}\,dy.

Then
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E_{0}(y)\frac{\partial\Gamma_{0}}{{\partial}y_{1}}(y)\,dy\ =\ 0

is satisfied if we
choose
\alpha
as in (
3.8
). In terms of
P
we have

(3.27)

\alpha=\frac{1}{\bar{r}}+\frac{\beta(P)}{|\log\varepsilon|},\quad\beta(P)=-{A+\log 8-6-2\bar{r}{\partial}_{r}K(P;P)-4K(P;P)\over 4\bar{r}}.

With this choice of
\alpha

we can solve (
3.26
).
Writing in polar coordinates
y=\rho e^{i\theta}
we observe that
E_{0}
has the form

E_{0}(y)=Q(\rho)\cos(\theta)
with
Q(\rho)=O(\frac{\log\rho}{\rho^{3}})
as
\rho\to\infty
.
A direct computation yields to

(3.28)

\bar{\psi}(y;P)={\varepsilon\over 2\bar{r}}\Gamma(\rho)y_{1}.

Using now the whole expression of
\Psi_{\varepsilon}
as in (
3.9
), we decompose

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}E[\Psi_{\varepsilon}]

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}E[\Psi_{P}]+{\varepsilon^{3}\over\bar{r}}[({\partial}_{rr}+{\partial}_{zz})\bar{\Gamma}+f^{\prime}(\Psi_{P})\bar{\Gamma}]+\varepsilon^{3}{3\over\bar{r}r}{\partial}_{r}\bar{\Gamma}

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{2}[f(\Psi_{P}+\varepsilon\bar{\Gamma})-f(\Psi_{P})-f^{\prime}(\Psi_{P})\varepsilon\bar{\Gamma}]

where
\bar{\Gamma}={r-\bar{r}\over 2\bar{r}}\Gamma({x-P\over\varepsilon})
and
f
is

f(s)={8\over\bar{r}}e^{-K(P;P)}\varepsilon^{2-\alpha\bar{r}}f_{0}\Big(\frac{r^{2}}{\bar{r}}(s-\alpha|\log\varepsilon|)\Big).

For
|y|<{\delta\over\varepsilon}
,

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(\Psi_{P})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}U(y)\exp\Big[\frac{1}{2\bar{r}}\Big(\Gamma_{0}(y)-(4-4\alpha\bar{r})\log\varepsilon+2\bar{r}\partial_{r}K(P)+4K(P)-\log 8\Big)\,\varepsilon y_{1}

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{2}\theta_{1}[P](y)+\varepsilon^{3}\theta_{2}[P](y)\Big]\times(1+{\varepsilon y_{1}\over\bar{r}})^{2}

\displaystyle={1\over\varepsilon^{2}}U(y)\Big[1+\frac{1}{2\bar{r}}\Big(\Gamma_{0}(y)-(4-4\alpha\bar{r})\log\varepsilon+a(P)\Big)\varepsilon y_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}\theta_{1}[P](y)+\varepsilon^{3}\theta_{2}[P](y)\Big]

where
\theta_{1}
,
\theta_{2}
satisfy (
3.15
), (
3.16
).
We have

\displaystyle{\varepsilon^{3}\over\bar{r}}

\displaystyle[({\partial}_{rr}+{\partial}_{zz})\bar{\Gamma}+f^{\prime}(\Psi_{P})\bar{\Gamma}]={1\over\bar{r}}[\Delta_{y}\bar{\psi}+U(y)\bar{\psi}]

\displaystyle+U(y)\bar{\psi}\Big[\frac{1}{2\bar{r}}\Big(\Gamma_{0}(y)-(4-4\alpha\bar{r})\log\varepsilon+a(P)\Big)\varepsilon y_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}\theta_{1}[P](y)+\varepsilon^{3}\theta_{2}[P](y)\Big].

Recall now that
\varepsilon^{2}E[\Psi_{P}]
can be written as in (
3.24
). Since
\bar{\psi}
solves (
3.26
) and has the form (
3.28
), we get

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}E

\displaystyle[\Psi_{P}]+{\varepsilon^{3}\over\bar{r}}[({\partial}_{rr}+{\partial}_{zz})\bar{\Gamma}+f^{\prime}(\Psi_{P})\bar{\Gamma}]=\Theta_{\varepsilon}(y)

where
\Theta_{\varepsilon}
can be described as in (
3.25
). Besides, using the form of the function
\Gamma
, as described in (
3.9
), one sees with a direct inspection that

\varepsilon^{3}{3\over\bar{r}r}{\partial}_{r}\bar{\Gamma}+\varepsilon^{2}[f(\Psi_{P}+\varepsilon\bar{\Gamma})-f(\Psi_{P})-f^{\prime}(\Psi_{P})\varepsilon\bar{\Gamma}]=\Theta_{\varepsilon}(y),

with
\Theta_{\varepsilon}
another function of the form (
3.25
). This concludes the proof of (
3.19
).

∎

4.
First approximate leapfrogging

The rest of the paper is devoted to find a solution to Problem (
1.10
) with the properties described in Theorem
1
. With a little abuse of notation, from now on we will use the variable
t
instead of
\tau
. Given
r_{0}>0
, we look for
(\Psi,W)
solving

(4.1)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &|\log\varepsilon|\,r\,{\partial}_{t}W+{\nabla}^{\perp}(r^{2}(\Psi-r_{0}^{-1}\,|\log\varepsilon|))\cdot{\nabla}W=0\quad{\mbox{in}}\quad\Sigma\times[0,T)\\
&-\Delta_{5}\Psi=W,\quad\quad{\mbox{in}}\quad\Sigma\times[0,T)\\
&{\partial\over\partial r}\Psi(x,t)=0\quad{\mbox{on}}\quad\partial\Sigma\times[0,T),\quad|\Psi(x,t)|\to 0\quad\text{as }|x|\to\infty.\end{aligned}\right.

We recall that
\Sigma=\{x=(r,z)\,:\,r>0,\,z\in\mathbb{R}\}
, see (
1.3
), and

\Delta_{5}\Psi=\partial_{rr}\Psi+\frac{3}{r}\partial_{r}\Psi+\partial_{zz}\Psi.

This Section is devoted to define a first approximate solution to (
4.1
), given as a sum of approximate travelling vortex rings, as built in Section
3
. These travelling vortex rings are centered at different points, at relative distance
|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}}
one from each other, all of them collapsing to
(r_{0},0)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Let us be more precise.

4.1.
The parameter functions

Fix an integer
k\geq 2
and consider points
P_{j}=P_{j}(t)
, for
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, which evolve in time and have the form

(4.2)

\displaystyle P_{j}

\displaystyle=P_{j}(t)=(r_{j}(t),z_{j}(t)),\quad t\in[0,T)\quad{\mbox{with}}

\displaystyle P_{j}

\displaystyle={\bf P}_{j}+{\bf a}_{j}(t),\quad{\bf P}_{j}=P_{0}+P_{j}^{0}(t)+P^{1}_{j}(t),\quad P_{0}:=(r_{0},0).

Let us describe the different terms in the decomposition of
P_{j}
. The points
P_{j}^{0}(t)=(r^{0}_{j}(t),z^{0}_{j}(t))
are explicit and will be determined towards the end of this section, in the form

(4.3)

P_{j}^{0}={1\over\sqrt{|\log\varepsilon|}}q_{j}+Q_{j},\quad\|Q_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}[0,T)}+\|\dot{Q}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}[0,T)}\lesssim{\log|\log\varepsilon|\over|\log\varepsilon|},

where
q_{j}
are the given solutions to the
leapfrogging dynamics
(
1.14
).

The points
P_{j}^{1}(t)=(r_{j}^{1}(t),z_{j}^{1}(t))
in (
4.2
) will also be determined in the process of the construction of an approximate leapfrogging solution and they will satisfy

(4.4)

\|P^{1}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}[0,T)}+\|\dot{P^{1}_{j}}\|_{L^{\infty}[0,T)}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2-\sigma},

for some
\sigma>0
, small and independent of
\varepsilon
.

The points
{\bf a}_{j}(t)=(a_{j1}(t),a_{j2}(t))
are free parameters to adjust at the end of our proof. For the moment, we ask they are continuous functions in
[0,T]
for which
\dot{\bf a}_{j}
exists and such that

(4.5)

\|{\bf a}_{j}\|_{C^{1}[0,T)}\lesssim\varepsilon^{3+\sigma}.

The following notation will be used to identify the different sets of points in the decomposition of
P_{j}
given in (
4.2
)

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle=(P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}),\quad P^{b}=(P_{1}^{b},\ldots,P_{k}^{b}),\quad b=0,1,\quad{\bf a}=({\bf a}_{1},\ldots,{\bf a}_{k}).

Since the relative distance between two points is of order
|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}}

we have

\{x\,:\,|x-P_{i}|<|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\}\cap\{x\,:\,|x-P_{\ell}|<|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\}=\emptyset

for all
\varepsilon
small and
i\not=\ell
.
Besides, under assumptions (
4.2
), (
4.3
), (
4.4
) and (
4.5
), we have that

\|P_{j}(t)-(r_{0},0)\|_{L^{\infty}[0,T]}={c\over\sqrt{|\log\varepsilon|}},\quad\forall j=1,\ldots,k,

for some constant
c
uniformly bounded and bounded away from
0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Given the points
P_{j}
as described in (
4.2
), we introduce positive functions

\varepsilon_{j}=\varepsilon_{j}(t)>0
such that, for all
j=1,\ldots,k
,

(4.6)

r_{j}(t)\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}(t)=r_{0}\,\varepsilon^{2}\quad\quad\mbox{for all}\quad\quad t\in[0,T),

where
r_{j}(t)
is the first component of the point
P_{j}(t)
.

From (
4.2
)–(
4.5
) we get that for all
j=1,\ldots,k
,

(4.7)

\|\varepsilon_{j}-\varepsilon\|_{L^{\infty}[0,T)}+\|\dot{\varepsilon}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}[0,T)}\lesssim\varepsilon|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}},\quad\forall\quad j=1,\ldots,k,\quad\quad\mbox{as}\quad\varepsilon\to 0.

For such
\varepsilon_{j}(t)
and
P_{j}(t)
, let
\alpha_{j}
be given as in (
3.8
), Proposition
3.1
so that

\alpha_{j}[P_{j}](t)={1\over r_{j}(t)}+{\beta(P_{j}(t))\over|\log\varepsilon_{j}(t)|},

where
\beta(s)
is the smooth function defined as in (
3.27
).
Observe that

\alpha_{j}[P_{0}](t)={1\over r_{0}}+{\beta(P_{0})\over|\log\varepsilon|}

is constant in time.
We also have

(4.8)

r_{0}^{-1}-\alpha_{j}[P_{j}](t)={r_{j}-r_{0}\over r_{0}r_{j}}-{\beta(P_{j})\over|\log\varepsilon_{j}|},

and
\|r_{0}^{-1}-\alpha_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}[0,T)}=O(|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}})
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

4.2.
The function
H^{0}
and definition of the very first approximation

For any
j=1,\ldots,k
, we define

(4.9)

\Psi_{j}^{0}(x,t):=\Psi_{\varepsilon_{j}(t)}[P_{j}(t)](x,t),\quad W_{j}^{0}(x,t):=-\Delta_{5}\Psi_{\varepsilon_{j}(t)}[P_{j}(t)](x),

where
\Psi_{\varepsilon}[P](x)
is the approximate travelling vortex ring introduced in
(
3.9
), Proposition
3.1
. Since we are assuming that the point
P_{j}(t)
evolves with time, the functions
\Psi_{j}^{0}
and
W_{j}^{0}
also depend on the time variable
t\in[0,T]
, and their dependence on time is through
P_{j}
(and
\varepsilon_{j}
).
Writing

(4.10)

\Psi_{j}^{0}(x,t)={1\over r_{j}}\psi^{0}_{j}(y),\quad y={x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}}

from (
4.9
) we get

(4.11)

W_{j}^{0}(x,t)={1\over\varepsilon_{j}^{2}r_{j}}w_{j}^{0}(y),\quad{\mbox{with}}\quad-\Delta_{5,j}\psi_{j}^{0}=w_{j}^{0}

where

(4.12)

\Delta_{5,j}={\partial}_{y_{1}}^{2}+{\partial}_{y_{2}}^{2}+{3\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}}\,{\partial}_{y_{1}}.

Let
\delta>0
be as in Proposition
3.1
. We can assume
\delta<r_{0}
. We have that in the region
|x-P_{j}|<\delta

(4.13)

\displaystyle w_{j}^{0}(y)

\displaystyle=U(y)\left(1+{\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}\over 2r_{j}}(\Gamma_{0}+\bar{A}+\Gamma)+\varepsilon^{2}\theta_{1}[P_{j}](y)+\varepsilon^{3}\theta_{2}[P_{j}](y)\right),\quad y={x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}},

where
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
are functions also described by (
3.15
) and (
3.16
) respectively. This expansion has been obtained in Section
3
, formula (
3.20
).

The starting point of our construction is to assume that the vorticity of a leapfrogging of vortex rings is at main order the sum of the vorticities of vortex rings. We do the same with the stream functions, which we then multiply by a cut off function to make it
0
at infinity.

Having introduced the points

P=(P_{1},P_{2},\ldots,,P_{k}),

define

\bar{\Psi}^{0}(x,t)=\eta(x)\,\sum_{j=1}^{k}\Psi_{j}^{0}(x,t),\quad W^{0}(x,t)=\eta(x)\,\sum_{j=1}^{k}W_{j}^{0}(x,t),

where
\eta
is the smooth cut-off function given by

\eta(x)=\eta_{1}\left({4|x-(r_{0},0)|\over\delta}\right),

with
\eta_{1}
as in (
2.2
). We then immediately see that
|\bar{\Psi}^{0}(x,t)|\to 0
as
|x|\to\infty
and that
{{\partial}\over{\partial}r}\bar{\Psi}^{0}(x,t)=0
on
{\partial}\Sigma
, for any
t\in[0,T]
. On the other hand we no longer have that
-\Delta_{5}\bar{\Psi}^{0}=W^{0}
.
We shall then slightly modify
\bar{\Psi}^{0}
by a function
H^{0}
to have, for any
t\in[0,T]

\Delta_{5}\left(\bar{\Psi}^{0}+H^{0}\right)(x,t)+W^{0}(x,t)=0,\quad x\in\Sigma.

For this purpose, consider the linear problem

(4.14)

\Delta_{5}\psi+h=0,\quad{\mbox{in }}\Sigma,\quad{\partial\psi\over\partial r}=0\quad{\mbox{on}}\,\,{\partial}\Sigma,\quad\psi(x)\to 0,\,\,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|x|\to\infty,

for a smooth function
h
satisfying

(4.15)

|h(x)|\leq{C\over 1+|x|^{2+\nu}},

where
C>0
. Recall that
\Delta_{5}={{\partial}^{2}\over{\partial}r^{2}}+{3\over r}{{\partial}\over{\partial}r}+{{\partial}^{2}\over{\partial}z^{2}}
, for
x=(r,z)\in\Sigma
.

We have

Lemma 4.1
.

Assume
h
satisfies (
4.15
), with
\nu>0
. Then there exist a solution
\psi={\mathcal{T}}(h)
to (
4.14
) and a constant
C_{1}>0
such that

(1+|x|)|\nabla\psi(x)|+|\psi(x)|\leq{C_{1}\over 1+|x|^{\min(\nu,3)}}.

Proof.

Recalling that the Laplacian of a radially symmetric function in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is

\Delta_{Y}={d^{2}\over ds^{2}}+{n-1\over s}{d\over ds},\quad Y=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}),\quad s=\sqrt{Y_{1}^{2}+\ldots Y_{n}^{2}}

we interpret the differential operator in (
4.14
) as the Laplacian in
\mathbb{R}^{5}
and recast Problem (
4.14
) in
\mathbb{R}^{5}
.
Define

\Psi(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{4},z)=\psi(\sqrt{Y_{1}^{2}+\ldots Y_{4}^{2}},z),\quad r=\sqrt{Y_{1}^{2}+\ldots Y_{4}^{2}},\quad Y_{5}=z

and

H(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{4},z)=h(\sqrt{Y_{1}^{2}+\ldots Y_{4}^{2}},z),\quad r=\sqrt{Y_{1}^{2}+\ldots Y_{4}^{2}},\quad\quad Y_{5}=z.

To solve Problem (
4.14
) we find bounded (non-singular) solutions
\Psi
to

\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{5}}\Psi+H=0,\quad{\mbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{5},\quad\Psi(Y)\to 0,\quad\quad\mbox{as}\quad|Y|\to\infty.

We define
\Psi
using the Newtonian potential in
\mathbb{R}^{5}
as

\Psi(Y)={1\over 15\omega_{5}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{5}}{1\over|Z-Y|^{3}}H(Z)\,dZ,

with
\omega_{5}
the volume of the unit
5
-ball. Moreover

\nabla\Psi(Y)=-{3\over 15\omega_{5}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{5}}{(Z-Y)\over|Z-Y|^{5}}H(Z)\,dZ.

From (
4.15
) we get that
(1+|Y|^{2+\nu})|H(Y)|\lesssim 1
. Estimating the above integrals splitting them in the region
|Z|<{|Y|\over 2}
and its complement we get

(1+|Y|)|\nabla\Psi(Y)|+|\Psi(Y)|\lesssim{1\over 1+|Y|^{\min(3,\nu)}}.

Going back to the original variables
x=(r,z)\in\Sigma
we get the required estimates.
∎

For any
t\in[0,T]
, we denote by
H^{0}[P]={\mathcal{T}}\left(\Delta_{5}\bar{\Psi}^{0}+W^{0}\right)
the solution to

(4.16)

\Delta_{5}H^{0}+\Delta_{5}\bar{\Psi}^{0}+W^{0}=0\quad{\mbox{in }}\Sigma,\quad{\partial H^{0}\over\partial r}=0\quad{\mbox{on }}{\partial}\Sigma

with
H^{0}[P](x,t)\to 0
as
|x|\to\infty
, for all
t\in[0,T]
.

A direct computation gives

(4.17)

\displaystyle\Delta_{5}\bar{\Psi}^{0}+W^{0}

\displaystyle=(\Delta_{5}\eta(x))\sum_{j=1}^{k}\Psi_{j}^{0}+2\sum_{j=1}^{k}\nabla_{r,z}\eta\cdot\nabla_{r,z}\Psi_{j}^{0}.

The function
\Delta_{5}\bar{\Psi}^{0}+W^{0}

is smooth, uniformly bounded as
\varepsilon\to 0
and with compact support.
From Lemma
4.1
and (
4.17
) we get that

(1+|x|)|\nabla H^{0}[P]|+|H^{0}[P]|\lesssim{1\over 1+|x|^{3}},\quad

uniformly for
t\in[0,T]
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

For

P=(P_{1},P_{2},\ldots,,P_{k})
, we can now define the first approximate solution to (
4.1
) to be

(4.18)

\displaystyle\Psi^{0}[P]

\displaystyle=\eta(x)\sum_{j=1}^{k}\Psi_{j}^{0}+H^{0}[P],\quad W^{0}[P]=\eta(x)\sum_{j=1}^{k}W_{j}^{0}.

4.3.
The very first error

We recast Problem (
4.1
) as the problem of finding

(W,\Psi)
with

S_{1}(W,\Psi)=S_{2}(W,\Psi)=0\quad{\mbox{in}}\quad\Sigma\times[0,T),\quad{\partial\Psi\over\partial r}=0\quad{\mbox{on }}{\partial}\Sigma\times[0,T).

Here
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are the Euler operators introduced in (
2.3
).
The leapfrogging of vortex rings are then solutions
(W,\Psi)
with
W
and
\Psi
close respectively to
W^{0}
and
\Psi^{0}
, and
|\Psi(x,t)|\to 0
as
|x|\to\infty
.
By construction what we have so far is that

\displaystyle S_{2}(W^{0},\Psi^{0})

\displaystyle=0\quad\Sigma\times[0,T),\quad{\partial\over\partial r}\Psi^{0}(r,z,t)=0\quad\partial\Sigma\times[0,T),

\displaystyle|\Psi^{0}(x,t)|\to 0,\quad\quad\mbox{as}\quad|x|\to\infty.

We shall now describe
S_{1}(W^{0},\Psi^{0})
. We can write

(4.19)

\displaystyle S_{1}(W^{0},\Psi^{0})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{k}E_{j}^{0},\quad{\mbox{where}}

\displaystyle E_{j}^{0}

\displaystyle=|\log\varepsilon|\,r\,\eta\,{\partial}_{t}W^{0}_{j}+{\nabla}^{\perp}(r^{2}(\Psi^{0}-r_{0}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|))\cdot{\nabla}(\eta W_{j}^{0}).

We start with the following general remark.

Remark 4.1
.

For a function
W(x,t)
given in the form

W(x,t)=\frac{1}{r_{j}\varepsilon_{j}^{2}}w\Bigl(\frac{x-P_{j}}{\varepsilon_{j}},t\Bigr),

for some function
w(y,t)
,
y={x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}}
we have

(4.20)

\varepsilon_{j}^{4}|\log\varepsilon|\,r\,{\partial}_{t}W=\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}){\partial}_{t}w-\varepsilon_{j}|\log\varepsilon|\nabla w\cdot\dot{P_{j}}+B_{0}(w),

where

(4.21)

\displaystyle B_{0}(\phi)=-\varepsilon_{j}\dot{\varepsilon}_{j}(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})\nabla\phi\cdot y-{\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\dot{P}_{j}\cdot\nabla\phi

An equivalent expression, which will be useful in the sequel, is

(4.22)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}^{4}|\log\varepsilon|r{\partial}_{t}W=|\log\varepsilon|

\displaystyle\Bigg[-\varepsilon_{j}\nabla w\cdot\dot{P}_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}^{2}{\partial}_{t}w+\varepsilon_{j}\dot{\varepsilon}_{j}(y_{1}{\partial}_{1}w-y_{2}{\partial}_{2}w)

\displaystyle-{\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\over r_{j}}\dot{z}_{j}y_{1}{\partial}_{2}w+{\varepsilon_{j}^{3}\over r_{j}}y_{1}{\partial}_{t}w-{\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\over r_{j}}\dot{\varepsilon}_{j}y_{1}\nabla w\cdot y\Bigg].

Proof of (
4.20
)
. For
x=\varepsilon_{j}y+P_{j}
,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}^{4}|\log\varepsilon|\,r\,{\partial}_{t}W

\displaystyle=|\log\varepsilon|(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})\Biggl[\varepsilon_{j}^{2}w_{t}-({\dot{r}_{j}\over r_{j}}+{2\dot{\varepsilon}_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}})\varepsilon_{j}^{2}w-\varepsilon_{j}\dot{\varepsilon}_{j}\nabla w\cdot y-\varepsilon_{j}\nabla w\cdot\dot{P}_{j}\Biggl].

From our first assumption (
4.6
) on the parameters
r_{j}
and
\varepsilon_{j}
we get

(4.23)

\frac{\dot{r}_{j}}{r_{j}}=-2\frac{\dot{\varepsilon}_{j}}{\varepsilon_{j}},\quad\quad\mbox{for all}\quad t

which gives (
4.20
).

Proof of (
4.22
)
. This expression follows from observing that

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}^{4}|\log\varepsilon|r{\partial}_{t}W=|\log\varepsilon|

\displaystyle\Bigg[-\varepsilon_{j}\nabla w\cdot\dot{P}_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}^{2}{\partial}_{t}w-\varepsilon_{j}\dot{\varepsilon}_{j}\nabla w\cdot y

\displaystyle-{\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\dot{P}_{j}\cdot\nabla w+{\varepsilon_{j}^{3}\over r_{j}}y_{1}{\partial}_{t}w-{\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\over r_{j}}\dot{\varepsilon}_{j}y_{1}\nabla w\cdot y\Bigg]

and (again (
4.23
) for
P_{j}(t)=(r_{j}(t),z_{j}(t))
)

\displaystyle-\varepsilon_{j}\dot{\varepsilon}_{j}\nabla w\cdot y-{\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\dot{P}_{j}\cdot\nabla w=\varepsilon_{j}\dot{\varepsilon}_{j}(y_{1}{\partial}_{1}w-y_{2}{\partial}_{2}w)-{\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\over r_{j}}\dot{z}_{j}y_{1}{\partial}_{2}w.

Recalling (
4.13
) and using (
4.20
) and (
4.11
) we easily get that the first term in
E_{j}^{0}
in (
4.19
) is given by

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}^{4}|\log\varepsilon|\,r\,\eta\,{\partial}_{t}W^{0}_{j}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon_{j}|\log\varepsilon|(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})\,\eta\,\dot{P}_{j}\cdot\nabla w^{0}_{j}

\displaystyle+\varepsilon_{j}|\log\varepsilon|(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})\,\eta\,\left[\varepsilon_{j}{\partial}_{t}w^{0}_{j}-\dot{\varepsilon}_{j}\nabla w^{0}_{j}\cdot y\right].

We now pass to the second term in
E_{j}^{0}
given in (
4.19
). It is convenient to use the decomposition

\displaystyle r^{2}\,\left(\Psi^{0}-r_{0}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|\right)

\displaystyle=r^{2}\,\left(\eta\Psi_{j}^{0}-\alpha_{j}|\log\varepsilon_{j}|\right)

\displaystyle+r^{2}\,\left(\alpha_{j}|\log\varepsilon_{j}|-r_{0}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|\right)+r^{2}\,\left(H^{0}+\eta\sum_{\ell\not=j}\Psi^{0}_{\ell}\right).

Consider the region around the point
P_{j}
defined by
\{x\,:\,|x-P_{j}|<|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\}
. In this region one has that
\eta(x)=1
, for all
\varepsilon
small, as a consequence of the assumptions on the points
P_{j}
.
We write, for
y={x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}}
,
|y|<\varepsilon_{j}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}
,

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{4}_{j}{\nabla}^{\perp}(r^{2}(\Psi^{0}-r_{0}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|))\cdot{\nabla}W_{j}^{0}

\displaystyle=\nabla^{\perp}\left((1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})^{2}(\psi_{j}^{0}-r_{j}\alpha_{j}|\log\varepsilon_{j}|)\right)\cdot\nabla w^{0}_{j}

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\perp}(\tilde{\varphi}_{j}(\varepsilon_{j}y+P_{j};P)\,\,)\,\cdot\nabla w^{0}_{j},

where

(4.24)

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}_{j}(x;P)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\varphi}_{j}(\varepsilon_{j}y+P_{j};P)=r_{j}(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})^{2}\times

\displaystyle\Biggl(\alpha_{j}|\log\varepsilon_{j}|-r_{0}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|\,\,+H^{0}[P](\varepsilon_{j}y+P_{j})+\sum_{\ell\not=j}{1\over r_{\ell}}\,\,\psi_{\ell}^{0}({\varepsilon_{j}\over\varepsilon_{\ell}}y+{P_{j}-P_{\ell}\over\varepsilon_{\ell}})\Biggl),

with
H^{0}
the correction introduced in (
4.16
). In this region we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}^{4}\,E_{j}^{0}(P_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}y)

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon_{j}|\log\varepsilon|(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})\,\dot{P}_{j}\cdot w^{0}_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}|\log\varepsilon|(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})\,\left[\varepsilon_{j}{\partial}_{t}w^{0}_{j}-\dot{\varepsilon}_{j}\nabla w^{0}_{j}\cdot y\right]

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\perp}\left((1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})^{2}(\psi_{j}^{0}-r_{j}\alpha_{j}|\log\varepsilon_{j}|)\right)\cdot\nabla w^{0}_{j}+\nabla^{\perp}(\tilde{\varphi}_{j}(\varepsilon_{j}y+P_{j};P)\,\,)\,\cdot\nabla w^{0}_{j}.

Reordering the terms,

E_{j}^{0}
takes the form

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}^{4}\,E^{0}_{j}(P_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}y)

\displaystyle=\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(y,t)\cdot\nabla w^{0}_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}){\partial}_{t}w^{0}_{j}+|\log\varepsilon|B_{0}(w_{j}^{0})

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\perp}\left((1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})^{2}(\psi_{j}^{0}-r_{j}\alpha_{j}|\log\varepsilon_{j}|)\right)\cdot\nabla w^{0}_{j},\quad y={x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}}

where
B_{0}
is defined in (
4.21
) and

(4.25)

{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(y,t)=\varepsilon_{j}|\log\varepsilon|\,\dot{P}_{j}^{\perp}\cdot y+\tilde{\varphi}_{j}(\varepsilon_{j}y+P_{j};P)-\tilde{\varphi}_{j}(P_{j};P).

In the same region, the contribution of
E_{i}^{0}
for
i\not=j
has the form

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}^{4}\,E^{0}_{i}(P_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}y)

\displaystyle={\varepsilon_{j}^{4}\over\varepsilon_{i}^{4}}\,\varepsilon_{i}^{4}\,E^{0}_{i}(({\varepsilon_{j}\over\varepsilon_{i}}{P_{j}-P_{i}\over\varepsilon_{j}}+{\varepsilon_{j}\over\varepsilon_{i}}y)=O({\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}\over 1+|y|})\quad\quad y={x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}}.

From the result in Proposition
3.1
we recognize that

(4.26)

\displaystyle(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})^{2}(\psi_{j}^{0}-r_{j}\alpha_{j}|\log\varepsilon_{j}|)=\Gamma_{0}(y)-(4-\alpha_{j}r_{j})\log\varepsilon_{j}-\log 8+K(P_{j};P_{j})

\displaystyle+\frac{\varepsilon_{j}\,y_{1}}{2r_{j}}\Big(\Gamma_{0}(y)+\bar{A}+\Gamma(y)\Big)\,+\varepsilon^{2}\theta_{1}[P_{j}](y)+\varepsilon^{3}\theta_{2}[P_{j}](y),

where
\bar{A}
is the constant defined in (
3.14
),

\Gamma=\Gamma(y)
as in (
3.7
),
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
have the form described in (
3.15
) and (
3.16
). Moreover

\displaystyle\nabla^{\perp}\left((1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})^{2}(\psi_{j}^{0}-r_{j}\alpha_{j}|\log\varepsilon_{j}|)\right)\cdot\nabla w^{0}_{j}

\displaystyle=r_{j}^{2}\,\varepsilon_{j}^{4}\,S_{\alpha_{j}}(W_{j}^{0};\Psi_{j}^{0})(\varepsilon_{j}y+P_{j})

where the operator
S_{\alpha}
is given by (
3.1
). From estimates (
3.10
) on this term, we conclude that, for
\rho=|y|=|{x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}}|<\varepsilon_{j}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}
,

(4.27)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}^{4}

\displaystyle S_{1}(W^{0},\Psi^{0})(P_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}y)=\varepsilon_{j}^{4}\sum_{i=1}^{k}E^{0}_{i}(P_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}y)

\displaystyle=\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(y,t)\cdot\nabla w^{0}_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}){\partial}_{t}w^{0}_{j}+|\log\varepsilon|B_{0}(w^{0}_{j})
