\displaystyle+{\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\over 1+|y|^{4}}E_{2}(\rho,\theta,t,\varepsilon)+{\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}\over 1+|y|^{3}}[E_{1}(\rho,\theta,t,\varepsilon)+E_{3}(\rho,\theta,t,\varepsilon)]+O\left({\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}\over 1+|y|^{2}}\right)\,

as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
To get this estimate we have used (
3.10
). The functions
E_{j}(\rho,\theta,t,\varepsilon)
have the following form

\displaystyle E_{j}(\rho,\theta,t,\varepsilon)=E_{j,1}(\rho,\varepsilon,P(t))\cos(j\theta)+E_{j,2}(\rho,\varepsilon,P(t))\sin(j\theta)

where

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{2}\Bigl|(1+\rho)^{i}\frac{\partial^{i}E_{j,\ell}}{\partial\rho^{i}}\Bigr|+\Bigl|\nabla_{P}E_{j,\ell}\Bigr|\lesssim 1\quad\ell=1,2,\quad\quad\mbox{as}\quad\varepsilon\to 0,

uniformly for
t\in[0,T)
.

4.4.
Dynamics for
P^{0}_{j}
and local reduction of the first error.

It is possible to reduce the size of the error term (
4.27
) in each of the regions
|{x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}}|<\varepsilon_{j}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}
,
j=1,\ldots,k
, by choosing properly the points
P_{j}^{0}
as in (
4.3
).
Write the function
\tilde{\varphi}_{j}
in (
4.24
) as

\tilde{\varphi}_{j}(x;P)=\varphi_{j}(x;P)+\varepsilon^{2}\theta_{j\varepsilon_{j}}(x;P)

with

(4.28)

\varphi_{j}(x;P)={r^{2}\over r_{j}}\Biggl(\alpha_{j}|\log\varepsilon_{j}|-r_{0}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|\,\,+H^{0}(x;P)+\sum_{\ell\not=j}{1\over r_{\ell}}\,\,\bar{\psi}_{\ell}^{0}(x;P_{\ell})\Biggl)

\displaystyle\bar{\psi}_{\ell}^{0}(x;P_{\ell})

\displaystyle=\log\frac{1}{|x-P_{\ell}|^{4}}\big(1-\frac{3}{2r_{\ell}}(r-r_{\ell})+H(x;P_{\ell})\big)+K(x;P_{\ell})+\varepsilon_{\ell}\Gamma(x;P_{\ell})

\displaystyle{\mbox{where}}\quad\varepsilon_{\ell}\Gamma(x;P_{\ell})={r-r_{\ell}\over 2r_{\ell}}\Gamma({x-P_{\ell}\over\varepsilon_{\ell}})\quad{\mbox{and}}

\displaystyle\theta_{j\varepsilon_{j}}(x;P)

\displaystyle={1\over\varepsilon^{2}}\sum_{\ell\not=j}{r^{2}\over r_{j}r_{\ell}}\log\frac{|x-P_{\ell}|^{4}}{(\varepsilon_{\ell}^{2}+|x-P_{\ell}|^{2})^{2}}\big(1-\frac{3}{2r_{\ell}}(r-r_{\ell})+H(x;P_{\ell})\big).

We refer to (
3.3
), (
3.4
) and (
3.9
) for the definition of
H
,
K
and
\Gamma
. We Taylor expand

(4.29)

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}_{j}

\displaystyle(P_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}y;P)=\ \tilde{\varphi}_{j}(P_{j};P)+\varepsilon_{j}{\nabla}_{x}\varphi_{j}(P_{j};P)\cdot y+\bar{\varphi}_{j}

\displaystyle\bar{\varphi}_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=2}^{4}\frac{\varepsilon_{j}^{k}}{k!}D_{x}^{k}\varphi_{j}(P_{j};P)[y]^{k}\,+\,\varepsilon_{j}^{3}D_{x}\theta_{j\varepsilon_{j}}(P_{j};P)[y]+{\varepsilon_{j}^{4}\over 2}D_{x}^{2}\theta_{j\varepsilon_{j}}(P_{j};P)[y]^{2}+Q(\varepsilon_{j}y,P)

where
Q(z,\zeta)
is a function smooth in its arguments that satisfies

|{\nabla}_{z}Q(z,\zeta)|\ \leq\ C\varepsilon|z|^{4}.

Recalling the definition of
{\mathcal{R}}_{j}
in (
4.25
) we can write

\displaystyle\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(y,t)

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon_{j}|\log\varepsilon|\,\dot{P}_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}\nabla_{x}^{\perp}\varphi_{j}(P_{j};P)+\nabla^{\perp}\bar{\varphi}_{j},

and we choose the points
P^{0}_{j}
in (
4.2
)-(
4.3
)
to satisfy the ODEs system

(4.30)

-|\log\varepsilon|\,\dot{P_{j}^{0}}+\nabla_{x}^{\perp}\varphi_{j}(P_{0}+P_{j}^{0};P_{0}+P^{0})=0,\quad j=1,\ldots k.

Here
P_{0}=(r_{0},0)
and
P^{0}=(P_{1}^{0},\ldots,P_{k}^{0})
. We use (
4.8
) to write

\nabla_{x}\varphi_{j}^{\perp}(P_{j};P)=\Theta_{j}(P)=\Theta_{j}^{0}(P)+\Theta_{j}^{1}(P)

where for
P_{j}=(r_{j},z_{j})
,

\displaystyle\Theta_{j}^{0}(P)

\displaystyle=-4\sum_{\ell\not=j}\,\,\frac{(P_{j}-P_{\ell})^{\perp}}{|P_{j}-P_{\ell}|^{2}}-2\,{r_{j}-r_{0}\over r_{0}^{2}}\,|\log\varepsilon|\,{\bf e_{2}}.

Recall now the form of the points
P_{j}^{0}
from (
4.3
)

P_{j}^{0}={1\over\sqrt{|\log\varepsilon|}}q_{j}+Q_{j}.

Here
q_{j}
are the given solutions to the
leapfrogging dynamics
(
1.14
), which gets rewritten as

-|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}\dot{q}_{j}+\Theta_{j}^{0}(P_{0}+{1\over\sqrt{|\log\varepsilon|}}q)=0\quad t\in[0,T].

We choose
Q_{j}
to solve the initial value problem

\displaystyle-\dot{Q}_{j}

\displaystyle+\tilde{\Theta}_{j}(t,Q)=0\quad t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle\tilde{\Theta}_{j}(t,Q)

\displaystyle:={1\over|\log\varepsilon|}\left[\Theta_{j}^{0}(P_{0}+P^{0})-\Theta_{j}^{0}(P_{0}+{1\over\sqrt{|\log\varepsilon|}}q)\right]+{1\over|\log\varepsilon|}\Theta_{j}^{1}(P_{0}+P^{0}),

\displaystyle Q_{j}(0)

\displaystyle=0.

A direct computation gives that

\|{1\over|\log\varepsilon|}\Theta_{j}^{1}(P_{0}+{1\over\sqrt{|\log\varepsilon|}}q)\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}\lesssim{\log|\log\varepsilon|\over|\log\varepsilon|}

The functions
\tilde{\Theta}_{j}
are Lipschitz continuous in
Q
in the set

\,\|Q_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}[0,T)}\lesssim{\log|\log\varepsilon|\over|\log\varepsilon|},

and continuous in
t
, for
t\in[0,T]
. Standard ODEs theory ensures the existence of a solution
(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{k})
satisfying the bounds (
4.3
).

This choice for
P^{0}
automatically reduces from
\varepsilon
to
\varepsilon^{2}
the size of the term
\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(y,t)\cdot\nabla w^{0}_{j}
in the first line of the error of approximation described by (
4.27
).
Let us now analyze the terms in (
4.27
) given by

\varepsilon_{j}|\log\varepsilon|(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})\left[\varepsilon_{j}{\partial}_{t}w^{0}_{j}-\dot{\varepsilon}_{j}\nabla w^{0}_{j}\cdot y\right]-\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|y_{1}\dot{P}_{j}\cdot\nabla w^{0}_{j}.

They have the same form as the terms in the following line in (
4.27
), with the only difference that in this case the functions
E_{j}(\rho,\theta,t,\varepsilon)
depend on
\dot{P}
, not only on
P
. This fact is consequence of (
4.22
) and of our assumptions on the points
P_{j}
.

We are thus in a position to conclude that if we choose the points
P^{0}
of the form (
4.3
) to satisfy (
4.30
), the error of approximation (
4.27
) in each region
|x-P_{j}|<|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}
can be described as, for
y={x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}}
,
\rho=|y|

(4.31)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}^{4}S_{1}(W^{0},\Psi^{0})

\displaystyle(P_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}y)=\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(y,t;P)\cdot\nabla w_{j}^{0}+{\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\over 1+|y|^{4}}E_{2}(\rho,\theta,t,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle+{\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}\over 1+|y|^{3}}[E_{1}(\rho,\theta,t,\varepsilon)+E_{3}(\rho,\theta,t,\varepsilon)]+O\left({\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}\over 1+|y|^{2}}\right)\,

as
\varepsilon\to 0
, where
the functions
E_{j}(\rho,\theta,t,\varepsilon)
now depends also on
\dot{P}
, and have the form

(4.32)

\displaystyle E_{j}[P,{\partial}_{t}P](\rho,\theta,t,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=E_{j,1}(\rho,\varepsilon,P(t),\dot{P}(t))\cos(j\theta)

\displaystyle+E_{j,2}(\rho,\varepsilon,P(t),\dot{P}(t))\sin(j\theta),

where

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{2}\Bigl|(1+\rho)^{i}\frac{\partial^{i}E_{j,\ell}}{\partial\rho^{i}}\Bigr|+\Bigl|\nabla_{P}E_{j,\ell}\Bigr|+\Bigl|\nabla_{\dot{P}}E_{j,\ell}\Bigr|\lesssim 1\quad\ell=1,2,\quad\quad\mbox{as}\quad\varepsilon\to 0.

Besides,

(4.33)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(y,t;P)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{j}|\log\varepsilon|{d\over dt}(P_{j}-P_{j}^{0})^{\perp}\cdot y+\tilde{\varphi}_{j}(\varepsilon_{j}y+P_{j};P)-\tilde{\varphi}_{j}(P_{j};P)

\displaystyle-\nabla_{x}\varphi_{j}(\bar{P}_{j}^{0};\bar{P}^{0})\,\varepsilon_{j}y.

where
\bar{P}^{0}_{j}(t)=P_{0}+P_{j}^{0}(t)
.

In the complementary region
\cap_{j=1}^{k}\{x\in\Sigma\,:|x-P_{j}|>|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\,\}
, it is convenient to describe the error in the original variable
(x,t)
. Given the expression for
S_{1}(W^{0},\Psi^{0})(x,t)
from (
4.19
), this error is automatically zero in the region
|x-P_{0}|>{\delta\over 2}
. Thus we consider
|x-P_{0}|\leq{\delta\over 2}
. Taking possibly
\varepsilon
smaller, for points in this region we have that

|x-P_{j}|<{\delta},\quad\forall j=1,\ldots,k.

Hence the expansions in Proposition
3.1
and (
3.20
) hold true. A direct computation gives that in the complementary region
\cap_{j=1}^{k}\{x\in\Sigma\,:|x-P_{j}|>|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\,\}
one has

(4.34)

S_{1}(W^{0},\Psi^{0})(x,t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}{O(\varepsilon^{2-\sigma})\over(\varepsilon^{4}+|x-P_{j}|^{4})},\quad\nabla_{x}S_{1}(W^{0},\Psi^{0})(x,t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}{O(\varepsilon^{2-\sigma})\over(\varepsilon^{5}+|x-P_{j}|^{5})}

for some
\sigma>0
arbitrarily small.

5.
Improvement of the approximation

In the previous section we introduced the functions
\Psi^{0}(x,t)
and
W^{0}(x,t)
defined in (
4.18
), where
P
is a collection of
k
points of the form

P=(P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}),\quad P_{j}(t)=P_{0}+P^{0}_{j}(t)+P^{1}_{j}(t)+{\bf a}_{j}(t),\quad P_{0}=(r_{0},0).

So far we have defined
P_{j}^{0}
explicitly with the form (
4.3
) to solve (
4.30
), while
P^{1}_{j}
and
{\bf a}_{j}
are still parameters that are assumed to satisfy (
4.4
) and (
4.5
) respectively.

The next step in our argument is to modify
(W^{0},\Psi^{0})
in order to produce a better approximate solution
(W^{*},\Psi^{*})
. We do it taking
(W^{*},\Psi^{*})
of the form

(5.1)

\displaystyle\Psi^{*}(x,t;P)

\displaystyle=\Psi^{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\eta_{j2}\over r_{j}}\psi_{j}^{*}({x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}},t)+\psi^{*,out}(x,t)

(5.2)

\displaystyle W^{*}(x,t;P)

\displaystyle=W^{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\eta_{j1}\over r_{j}\varepsilon_{j}^{2}}\phi_{j}^{*}({x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}},t)+\phi^{*,out}(x,t)

where

(5.3)

\eta_{jN}(x,t)=\eta_{N}\left(|\log\varepsilon|^{\zeta}\,|x-P_{j}|\right),

and
\eta_{N}
is given by (
2.2
).
Here
\zeta>1
is a positive number, independent of
\varepsilon
. Since the relative distance between
P_{j}
and
P_{i}
,
j\not=i
, is of the order
|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}}
, we have

P_{i}\in{\mbox{support}}\,(\eta_{i2}),\quad{\mbox{support}}\,(\eta_{i2})\cap{\mbox{support}}\,(\eta_{j2})=\emptyset,\quad i\not=j.

It will be convenient to choose

\zeta=3.

This will guarantee that
\Psi^{0}
and
W^{0}
are the main terms in the decomposition of
\Psi^{*}
and
W^{*}
in the region where the corresponding cut-off functions
\eta_{j2}
,
\eta_{j1}
are non-zero.

If we insert the expressions of
\Psi^{*}
and
W^{*}
given by (
5.1
) and (
5.2
) in the Euler operator
S_{1}
(see (
2.3
)) we get

(5.4)

\displaystyle S_{1}(W^{*},\Psi^{*})=\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\eta_{1j}\over\varepsilon_{j}^{4}}E_{j}^{in}[\phi_{j}^{*},\psi_{j}^{*}\,\psi^{*,out},P]+E^{out}[\phi^{*,out},\psi^{*,out},\phi^{*,in},\psi^{*,in},P],

where

\phi^{*,in}=(\phi_{1}^{*},\ldots,\phi_{k}^{*}),\quad\psi^{*,in}=(\psi_{1}^{*},\ldots,\psi_{k}^{*}).

The inner-operators
E_{j}^{in}
,
j=1,\ldots,k
, are defined as

\displaystyle E_{j}^{in}

\displaystyle[\phi_{j},\psi_{j}\,\psi^{out},P](y,t):=|\log\varepsilon|\varepsilon_{j}^{2}(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}){\partial}_{t}\phi_{j}+|\log\varepsilon|B_{0}(\phi_{j})

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\perp}\left((1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})^{2}(\psi_{j}^{0}-r_{j}\alpha_{j}|\log\varepsilon_{j}|+\psi_{j}+r_{j}\psi^{out})+{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(y,t;P)\right)\cdot\nabla\phi_{j}

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\perp}\left((1+{\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}\over r_{1}})^{2}(\psi_{j}+r_{j}\psi^{out})\right)\nabla(w^{0}_{j}+\sum_{i\not=j}{r_{j}\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\over r_{i}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}}w^{0}_{i})

(5.5)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon_{j}^{4}S_{1}(W^{0},\Psi^{0})(\varepsilon_{j}y+P_{j}),\quad|y|<3R_{j},\quad R_{j}:={1\over\varepsilon_{j}|\log\varepsilon|^{\zeta}}

with
y={x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}}
,
t\in[0,T)
, and
{\mathcal{R}}_{j}
defined in (
4.33
). Moreover
B_{0}
is the operator defined in (
4.21
),
which can be equivalently written as

\displaystyle B_{0}(\phi)=\varepsilon_{j}\dot{\varepsilon}_{j}(y_{1}{\partial}_{1}\phi-y_{2}{\partial}_{2}\phi)-{\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\over r_{j}}\dot{\varepsilon}_{j}y_{1}y\cdot\nabla\phi-{\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\over r_{j}}\dot{z}_{j}y_{1}{\partial}_{2}\phi

see (
4.20
) and (
4.22
) for the derivation of
B_{0}
, and its equivalent form.
The outer-operator
E^{out}
is given by

(5.6)

\displaystyle E^{out}

\displaystyle[\phi^{out},\psi^{out},\phi^{in},\psi^{in},P](x,t):=|\log\varepsilon|\,r\,\partial_{t}\phi^{out}

\displaystyle+\nabla_{x}^{\perp}(r^{2}(\Psi^{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\eta_{j2}\over r_{j}}\psi_{j}({x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}})+\psi^{out}-r_{0}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|))\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi^{out}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\left[r\,|\log\varepsilon|\,{\partial}_{t}\bar{\eta}_{j1}+\nabla_{x}^{\perp}(r^{2}(\Psi^{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\eta_{j2}\over r_{j}}\psi_{j}({x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}})+\psi^{out}-r_{0}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|))\nabla\bar{\eta}_{1j}\right]{\phi_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}^{2}r_{j}}

\displaystyle+\left[\sum_{j=1}^{k}(\eta_{2j}-\eta_{1j})\nabla_{x}^{\perp}(r^{2}({\psi_{j}\over r_{j}}+\psi^{out}))+{r^{2}\psi_{j}\over r_{j}}\nabla_{x}^{\perp}\eta_{2j}\right]\cdot\nabla_{x}W^{0}

\displaystyle+(1-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\eta_{2j})\nabla^{\perp}(r^{2}\psi^{out})\cdot\nabla W^{0}+(1-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\eta_{j1})S_{1}(W^{0},\Psi^{0})=0\quad(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T).

If we now insert the expressions of
\Psi^{*}
and
W^{*}
given by (
5.1
) and (
5.2
) in the Euler operator
S_{2}
(see (
2.3
)) we get

(5.7)

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle[\Psi^{*},W^{*}]=S_{2}[\phi^{*,in},\psi^{*,in},\phi^{*,out},\psi^{*,out},P],\quad{\mbox{with}}

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle[\phi^{in},\psi^{in},\phi^{out},\psi^{out},P]=\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\eta_{1j}\over r_{j}\varepsilon_{j}^{2}}[\Delta_{5,j}\psi_{j}+\phi_{j}]+\Delta_{5}\psi^{out}+\phi^{out}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{k}(\eta_{j1}-\eta_{j2}){\Delta_{5,j}\psi_{j}\over r_{j}\varepsilon_{j}^{2}}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}({\psi_{j}\over r_{j}}\Delta_{5}\eta_{j2}+2\nabla_{x}\eta_{j2}\nabla_{x}{\psi_{j}\over r_{j}}).

Here
\Delta_{5,j}
is the differential operator in the expanded
y
-variable defined in (
4.12
), while
\Delta_{5}
is the differential operator defined in the original
x
-variable as given in (
1.6
).
We require conditions on the boundary and at infinity on
\psi^{*,out}
: for all
t\in[0,T]

{\partial\psi^{*,out}\over\partial r}(x,t)=0,\quad{\mbox{on}}\quad\partial\Sigma\times[0,T],\quad|\psi^{*,out}(x,t)|\to 0,\quad\quad\mbox{as}\quad|x|\to\infty.

We are able to prove that there exist points
P_{1}^{1},\ldots,P_{k}^{1}
in (
4.2
) such that, for any choice of points
{\bf a}_{1},\ldots,{\bf a}_{k}
in (
4.2
) satisfying (
4.5
), it is possible to construct a good approximate leapfrogging of vortex rings
(W^{*},\Psi^{*})
with the form (
5.1
)-(
5.2
). This is the content of next Proposition.

Proposition 5.1
.

There exist points

{\bf P}=({\bf P}_{1},\ldots,{\bf P}_{k}),\quad{\bf P}_{j}=P_{0}+P^{0}_{j}+P^{1}_{j},\quad P_{0}=(r_{0},0)

satisfying (
4.4
)-(
4.5
) for some
\sigma>0
small, and functions

\phi^{*,in}:=(\phi_{1}^{*}(y,t),\ldots,\phi_{k}^{*}(y,t)),\quad\psi^{*,in}:=(\psi_{1}^{*}(y,t),\ldots,\psi_{k}^{*}(y,t)),\quad\phi^{*,out},\quad\psi^{*,out}

in (
5.1
), (
5.2
)
such that, for any points
{\bf a}
satisfying (
4.5
), the following facts hold. For

P={\bf P}+{\bf a},

we have

\displaystyle E_{j}^{in}[\phi_{j}^{*},\psi_{j}^{*}\,\psi^{*,out},P](y,t)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{j}\nabla^{\perp}\left[\left(|\log\varepsilon|\dot{\bf a}^{\perp}_{j}+D_{x}{\nabla}_{x}\varphi_{j}({\bf P}_{j};{\bf P})[{\bf a}]\right)\cdot y\right]\nabla U

\displaystyle+{\mathcal{E}}_{j}[\phi_{j}^{*},\psi_{j}^{*}\,\psi^{*,out},{\bf a}](y,t)

where
\varphi_{j}
is given in (
4.28
) and

\Bigl|{\mathcal{E}}_{j}[\phi_{j}^{*},\psi_{j}^{*}\,\psi^{*,out},{\bf a}](y,t)\Bigl|=O\left({\varepsilon^{5-\sigma_{*}}\over 1+|y|^{3}}\right),\quad|y|<3R_{j},\quad R_{j}:={1\over\varepsilon_{j}|\log\varepsilon|^{3}}

for all
j=1,\ldots,k
, and

\left|E^{out}[\phi^{*,out},\psi^{*,out},\phi^{*,in},\psi^{*,in},P](x,t)\right|\lesssim{\varepsilon^{4-2\sigma_{*}}\over 1+|x|^{4}},\quad(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T]

for some
\sigma_{*}>0
arbitrarily small.
We refer to (
5
) and (
5.6
) for the explicit definitions of
E_{j}^{in}
and
E^{out}
.
Besides,

\displaystyle(1+|y|)|\nabla\psi_{j}^{*}(y,t)|+|\psi_{j}^{*}(y,t)|

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{2-\sigma_{*}},\quad|y|<3R_{j},\,t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle(1+|y|^{2})|\phi_{j}^{*}(y,t)|

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{2-\sigma_{*}},\quad|y|<3R_{j},\,t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle(1+|x|^{4})|\phi^{*,out}(x,t)|+(1+|x|^{2})|\psi^{*,out}(x,t)|

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{2-2\sigma_{*}},\quad(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T].

This result is telling that the new approximate solution

(W^{*},\Psi^{*})
in
(\ref{f110})
-
(\ref{f220})

with the parameter functions considered above produces total errors in
(\ref{fullS10})
and (
5.7
) which can be estimated as follows

\displaystyle|S_{1}(W^{*},\Psi^{*})(x,t)|\ \leq

\displaystyle C\varepsilon^{1-\sigma_{*}}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\frac{1}{1+|y_{j}|^{3}}+C{\varepsilon^{4-2\sigma_{*}}\over 1+|x|^{4}},\quad y_{j}=\frac{x-P_{j}(t)}{\varepsilon},

\displaystyle|S_{2}(W^{*},\Psi^{*})(x,t)|\ \leq

\displaystyle C\varepsilon^{4-\sigma_{*}}\eta_{1}\left({4|x-(r_{0},0)|\over r_{0}}\right),

for all
x\in\Sigma
and
t\in[0,T]
.
By construction these errors are also uniformly Lipschitz in the parameter points
{\bf a}
.
Besides, in combination with (
3.17
)-(
4.13
) we get that, for all
j=1,\ldots,k
,

(5.8)

\displaystyle W^{*}(x,t;P)

\displaystyle={1\over r_{j}\varepsilon_{j}^{2}}\,f\left((1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})^{2}(\psi_{j}^{0}-r_{j}\alpha_{j}|\log\varepsilon_{j}|+\psi_{j}^{*})\right)\left(1+O(\varepsilon^{2})\right),

\displaystyle{\mbox{where}}\quad f(s)

\displaystyle=8\,e^{-K(P_{j},P_{j})}\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2-\alpha_{j}r_{j}}\,e^{s},\quad{\mbox{and}}\quad\Psi^{*}(x,t;P)={1\over r_{j}}\,\psi_{j}^{0}+O(\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|)

uniformly in the region
|x-P_{j}|<|\log\varepsilon|^{-3}
, with
x=P_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}y
. The definition of
\psi_{j}^{0}
is given in (
4.10
), see also (
4.26
).
We also have

(5.9)

\displaystyle(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})^{2}(\psi_{j}^{0}-r_{j}\alpha_{j}|\log\varepsilon_{j}|+\psi_{j}^{*})

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{0}(y)-(4-\alpha_{j}r_{j})\log\varepsilon_{j}-\log 8+K(P_{j};P_{j})

\displaystyle+\frac{\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}}{2r_{j}}\Big(\Gamma_{0}(y)+\bar{A}+\Gamma(y)\Big)\,+b_{j}^{*}(y,t)+b_{j}^{**}(y,t)

where

|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}|{\partial}_{t}b_{j}^{*}(y,t)|+(1+|y|)|\,{\nabla}_{y}b_{j}^{*}(y,t)|+|b_{j}^{*}(y,t)|\ \leq\ C\varepsilon^{2}\,(1+|y|^{2})\,\log(2+|y|).

In
b_{j}^{**}(y,t)
we collect all reminding terms which depend on
\dot{\bf a}
. On these terms we have the following control

(1+|y|)|\,{\nabla}_{y}b_{j}^{**}(y,t)|+|b_{j}^{**}(y,t)|\ \leq\ C\varepsilon^{2+\bar{\sigma}}\,(1+|y|^{2})\,

for
y={x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}}
,
|y|<|\log\varepsilon|^{-3}
, for some fixed
\bar{\sigma}>0
.
Next section will be devoted to
build this approximate solution and to prove Proposition
5.1
.

6.
Improvement of the approximation: Proof of Proposition
5.1

The approximate solution
(W^{*},\Psi^{*})
predicted by Proposition
5.1
is constructed improving the inner errors
E_{j}^{in}
defined in (
5
) ten successive times, and improving the outer error
E^{out}
defined in (
5.6
) once.

It is useful to have at hand a more explicit expression of the inner-operators
E_{j}^{in}
in (
5
).
The key observation is that in the region

|x-P_{j}|<3|\log\varepsilon|^{-\zeta}
, we have, for
y={x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}}
,

(6.1)

\displaystyle(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})^{2}

\displaystyle(\psi_{j}^{0}-r_{j}\alpha_{j}|\log\varepsilon_{j}|)+{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(y,t;P)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{0}(y)-(4-\alpha r_{0})\log\varepsilon_{j}-\log 8+K(P_{j};P_{j})

\displaystyle+\frac{\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}}{2r_{j}}\Big(\Gamma_{0}(y)+\bar{A}+\Gamma(y)\Big)\,+{\mathcal{R}}^{0}_{j}(y,t;P),\quad{\mbox{where}}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}^{0}_{j}(y,t;P)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{j}|\log\varepsilon|(\dot{P}_{j}-\dot{P}_{j}^{0})^{\perp}\cdot y+\varepsilon_{j}[{\nabla}_{x}\varphi_{j}(P_{j};P)-{\nabla}_{x}\varphi_{j}(\bar{P}_{j}^{0};\bar{P}^{0})]\cdot y

\displaystyle+\,\varepsilon_{j}^{3}D_{x}\theta_{j\varepsilon_{j}}(P_{j};P)[y]+\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}[P](y)+\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{3\over 2}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}[P](y)

with
\bar{P}^{0}_{j}=P_{0}+P_{j}^{0}
,
\bar{P}^{0}=(\bar{P}^{0}_{1},\ldots,\bar{P}_{k}^{0})
,

{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
denote functions of the form, for
y=\rho e^{i\theta}
,

(6.2)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}[P](y)

\displaystyle=\Biggl[a(P)+b_{1}(P)\cos 2\theta+b_{2}(P)\sin 2\theta\Biggl]\,O(|y|^{2})

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}[P_{0}](y)

\displaystyle=a(P)O(|y|^{3}),

for
a
,
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
smooth functions of the points
P
, which are uniformly bounded as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Also:
\bar{A}
is the explicit constant defined by (
3.14
) and

\Gamma=\Gamma(y)
is the function introduced in (
3.7
).

Proof of (
6.1
).

In order to prove (
6.1
), we combine an expansion of the term
(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})^{2}(\psi_{j}^{0}-r_{j}\alpha_{j}|\log\varepsilon_{j}|)
following the lines to get (
3.13
).
From (
4.29
) and (
4.33
) we get

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(y,t;P)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{j}|\log\varepsilon|(\dot{P}_{j}-\dot{P}_{j}^{0})^{\perp}\cdot y+\varepsilon_{j}[{\nabla}_{x}\varphi_{j}(P_{j};P)-{\nabla}_{x}\varphi_{j}(\bar{P}_{j}^{0};\bar{P}^{0})]\cdot y

\displaystyle+\,\varepsilon_{j}^{3}D_{x}\theta_{j\varepsilon_{j}}(P_{j};P)[y]+\sum_{k=2}^{4}\frac{\varepsilon_{j}^{k}}{k!}D_{x}^{k}\varphi_{j}(P_{j};P)[y]^{k}+{\varepsilon_{j}^{4}\over 2}D_{x}^{2}\theta_{j\varepsilon_{j}}(P_{j};P)[y]^{2}+Q(\varepsilon_{j}y,P)

Under our assumptions (
4.2
), (
4.4
) and (
4.5
) on the form and size of the points
P_{j}
, we have that

(6.3)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(y,t;P)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{j}|\log\varepsilon|(\dot{P}_{j}-\dot{P}_{j}^{0})^{\perp}\cdot y+\varepsilon_{j}[{\nabla}_{x}\varphi_{j}(P_{j};P)-{\nabla}_{x}\varphi_{j}(\bar{P}_{j}^{0};\bar{P}^{0})]\cdot y

\displaystyle+\,\varepsilon_{j}^{3}D_{x}\theta_{j\varepsilon_{j}}(P_{j};P)[y]+\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}[P](y)+\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{3\over 2}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}[P](y)

where

{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
satisfy (
6.2
). This fact gives (
6.1
).

∎

6.1.
Strategy for the improvement

Replacing (
6.1
) in (
5
) and using (
4.6
) we re-write the inner operator
E^{in}_{j}
(see (
5
)) as

\displaystyle E_{j}^{in}[\phi_{j},\psi_{j},\psi^{out},P](y,t)

\displaystyle:=L_{1}(\phi_{j})+L_{2}(\phi_{j})+L_{3}(\psi_{j})+Q(\psi_{j},\phi_{j})+L_{3}(r_{j}\psi^{out})+Q(\psi^{out},\phi_{j})

(6.4)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon_{j}^{4}S_{1}(W^{0},\Psi^{0})(\varepsilon_{j}y+P_{j}),

where

(6.5)

\displaystyle L_{1}(\phi)

\displaystyle:=|\log\varepsilon|\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}){\partial}_{t}\phi+|\log\varepsilon|B_{0}(\phi)

\displaystyle L_{2}(\phi)

\displaystyle:=\nabla^{\perp}\left(\Gamma_{0}+\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}}\,y_{1}\,\Big(\Gamma_{0}(y)+\bar{A}+\Gamma(y)\Big)\,+{\mathcal{R}}^{0}_{j}(y,t;P)\right)\cdot\nabla\phi

\displaystyle L_{3}(\psi)

\displaystyle:=\nabla^{\perp}((1+{\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}\over r_{1}})^{2}\psi)\cdot\nabla(w^{0}_{j}+\sum_{i\not=j}w_{i}^{0})

\displaystyle Q(\psi,\phi)

\displaystyle:=\nabla^{\perp}\left((1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})^{2}\psi\right)\cdot\nabla\phi.

We recall the definition of the operator
B_{0}
in (
4.21
).

The inner error is improved using three different types of mechanisms. The choice of the mechanism is dictated by the form of the part of the error we aim at removing.

Suppose the error

E(y,t),
with
y=\rho e^{i\theta}
,
has a Fourier decomposition in the
\theta
-variable given by

E(y,t)=E(\rho e^{i\theta},t)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}E_{n}(\rho,t)e^{i\,n\,\theta},\quad E_{n}(\rho,t)=\int_{0}^{2\pi}E(\rho e^{in\theta})e^{in\theta}\,d\theta.

We call
E_{n}
the
n-
th mode in the Fourier decomposition of
E
.

The elliptic improvement.
If the part of the error we want to remove has no
0
-th mode in its Fourier decomposition then we will solve using the elliptic operator

L[\psi]\,:=\,\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma_{0}\cdot\nabla\phi+\nabla^{\perp}\psi\cdot\nabla U,\quad\phi=-\Delta_{5,j}\psi.

You recover
L[\psi]
from
L_{2}(\phi_{j})+L_{3}(\psi_{j})
in (
6.5
) just formally taking
\varepsilon_{j}=0
,
\omega_{j}^{0}=U
and
\omega_{i}^{0}=0
for
i\not=j
.
Using the fact that
-\Delta\Gamma_{0}=f_{0}(\Gamma_{0})=U
where
f_{0}(u)=e^{u}
and
-\Delta_{5,j}\psi=\phi
we see that

\displaystyle L[\psi]\ =

\displaystyle-\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma_{0}\cdot\nabla[\Delta_{y}\psi+{3\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}}{\partial}_{y_{1}}\psi+f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0})\psi].

In polar coordinates
y=\rho e^{i\theta}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we
check that

L[\psi]=-\frac{4}{\rho^{2}+1}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\theta}[\Delta_{y}\psi+{3\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}}{\partial}_{y_{1}}\psi+f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0})\psi].

It is enough to use the simplified version of this operator given by

(6.6)

L_{0}[\psi]:=-\frac{4}{\rho^{2}+1}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\theta}[\Delta_{y}\psi+f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0})\psi],\quad\phi=-\Delta_{5,j}\psi.

For terms in the error of Fourier mode
1
or higher, we will solve with the elliptic operator in (
6.6
). When the error has mode
1
, it will be possible to solve only under certain orthogonality conditions, which requires a proper adjustment of the points
P_{j}^{1}
. We call this procedure the
elliptic improvement
. We will discuss the solvability and a-priori bounds for problems of the form
L_{0}(\psi)=E
in Lemma
6.1
.

The ODEs improvement.
If the part of inner error
E(y,t)
we want to remove has
0
-th mode in its Fourier decomposition, we will solve

|\log\varepsilon|\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,{\partial}_{t}\phi=E,\quad\phi(0,\cdot)=0.

This is the main part of
L_{1}(\phi)
in (
6.5
). We can solve the above ODEs
at the expenses of losing two power of
\varepsilon_{j}
(and gaining one power of
|\log\varepsilon|
) in the estimates for the solution. We will see that the construction is decided by powers of
\varepsilon
, in the sense that the exact number of powers of
|\log\varepsilon|
we gain will be easily absorbed in a slightly smaller power of
\varepsilon
. We call this procedure the
ODEs improvement
. It has the advantage of keeping track of the Fourier modes of the solutions.

The transport improvement.
After the fifth inner improvement, we will need to reduce not only the size of the error, expressed in terms of powers of
\varepsilon
, but also its decay rate in the
\rho=|y|
-variable. In this case we will solve the transport-type equation

L_{1}(\phi)+L_{2}(\phi)=E.

We call this procedure the
transport improvement
. A-priori estimates for solutions of this transport-type equation are contained in Lemma
6.2
. After solving this linear equation, we will have lost control on the Fourier modes of the solution
\phi
, but the structure of the problem will automatically give a new error whose main term has no
0
-th mode in its Fourier decomposition. This will be crucial for the scheme of improvement to work.

We summarize the complete process of improvement in the following diagram: for
\rho={|x-P_{j}|\over\varepsilon_{j}}

e_{1}={\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\over 1+\rho^{4}}E_{2}\,\underset{{\mathcal{E}}}{\Rightarrow}\,e_{2}={\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}\over 1+\rho^{3}}E_{1}\,\underset{{\mathcal{E}\&P^{1}}}{\Rightarrow}\,e_{3}={\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{4}\over 1+\rho^{2}}E_{0}\,\underset{{\mathcal{O}DEs}}{\Rightarrow}\,e_{4}={\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{6}\over 1+\rho^{3}}E_{12}\sin\theta\,\underset{{\mathcal{E}}\&P}{\Rightarrow}

\,e_{5}={\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{8}\over 1+\rho^{2}}E_{2}\,\underset{{\mathcal{E}}}{\Rightarrow}\,e_{6}={\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{8}\over 1+\rho}E_{0}\,\underset{{\mathcal{T}}}{\Rightarrow}\,e_{7}={\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{8}\over 1+\rho^{5}}E_{1}\,\underset{{\mathcal{O}ut}}{\Rightarrow}\,e_{8}={\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+\rho^{5}}E_{1}\,\underset{{\mathcal{E}\&P^{1}}}{\Rightarrow}\,

e_{9}={\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+\rho^{4}}E_{0}\,\underset{{\mathcal{O}DEs}}{\Rightarrow}\,e_{10}={\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+\rho^{5}}E_{12}\sin\theta\,\underset{{\mathcal{E}}\&P^{1}}{\Rightarrow}\,e_{11}={\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+\rho^{4}}E_{2}\,\underset{{\mathcal{E}}}{\Rightarrow}\,e_{12}={\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+\rho^{3}}E_{0}.\,

Here
{\mathcal{E}}
stands for
inner elliptic improvement
,

{\mathcal{E}}\&P^{1}
stands for
inner elliptic improvement with adjustment of the points
P^{1}
,
{\mathcal{T}}
stands for
inner transport improvement
,
{\mathcal{O}DEs}
stands for
inner ODEs improvement
, and
{\mathcal{O}ut}
stands for
outer improvement
.
Besides
b
is a positive number whose value may change from line to line and within the same line.

Let us explain how to interpret the diagram: we start with an initial error of which we aim at eliminating the part of size
\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|
, decay in space
{1\over 1+\rho^{4}}
and Fourier mode
2
: we write it as
e_{1}={\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\over 1+\rho^{4}}E_{2}
. To do so, we proceed with the inner elliptic improvement: we write
\underset{{\mathcal{E}}}{\Rightarrow}
. After this correction is done, we have a new error. Of the new error we aim now at eliminating the main term, which has size
\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}
, decay in space
{1\over 1+\rho^{3}}
and Fourier mode
1
: we write
e_{2}={\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}\over 1+\rho^{3}}E_{1}
. And so on. Notice that the errors
e_{4}
and
e_{10}
has mode
1
, but only with
\sin\theta
(mode
1
, odd in
y_{2}
).

Before starting the process of improvement of the approximation,
we observe that the expression for the initial error
\varepsilon_{j}^{4}S_{1}(W^{0},\Psi^{0})(P_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}y)

in (
4.31
) gets a simpler form if we use (
6.1
). Using (
6.3
) and (
3.20
), we write

(6.7)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(y,t;P)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;P)+\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}[P](y)+\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{3\over 2}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}[P](y)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;P)

\displaystyle:=\varepsilon_{j}|\log\varepsilon|(\dot{P}_{j}-\dot{P}_{j}^{0})\cdot y+\varepsilon_{j}[{\nabla}_{x}\varphi_{j}(P_{j};P)-{\nabla}_{x}\varphi_{j}(P_{j}^{0};P^{0})]\cdot y

\displaystyle+\,\varepsilon_{j}^{3}D_{x}\theta_{j\varepsilon_{j}}(P_{j};P)[y],

and

\displaystyle w_{j}^{0}

\displaystyle=U(y)\left(1+{\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}\over 2r_{j}}(\Gamma_{0}+\bar{A}+\Gamma)+\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}[P](y)+\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{3\over 2}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}[P](y)\right),

with
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
,
i=1,2
as in (
6.2
).
Under the constraints (
4.2
)-(
4.3
)-(
4.4
)-(
4.5
) on the points
P_{j}
, we get

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(y,t;P)\cdot\nabla w_{j}^{0}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{R}}^{00}_{j}(y,t;P)\cdot\nabla U+{\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\over 1+|y|^{4}}E_{2}(\rho,\theta,t,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle+{\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}\over 1+|y|^{3}}(E_{1}+E_{3})(\rho,\theta,t,\varepsilon)+O\left({\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}\over 1+|y|^{2}}\right)\,;

hence

\varepsilon_{j}^{4}S_{1}(W^{0},\Psi^{0})(P_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}y)

in (
4.31
) becomes

(6.8)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}^{4}S_{1}(W^{0},\Psi^{0})

\displaystyle(P_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}y)=\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;P)\cdot\nabla U+{\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\over 1+|y|^{4}}E_{2}(\rho,\theta,t,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle+{\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}\over 1+|y|^{3}}(E_{1}+E_{3})(\rho,\theta,t,\varepsilon)+O\left({\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}\over 1+|y|^{2}}\right)\,

as
\varepsilon\to 0
, where
the functions
E_{i}(\rho,\theta,t,\varepsilon)
now depends also on
{\partial}_{t}P
, and have the form (
4.32
). These estimates are valid for
|y|<3\varepsilon_{j}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|^{-\zeta}
.

We are now ready to start improving. At each step of improvement we analyze the term of the error we want to remove, we describe the strategy to do it and we compute the new error produced by the correction.

6.2.

First inner improvement.
The first improvement of the error will remove part of the mode-2 term of size
\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|
given by

{\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\over 1+|y|^{4}}E_{2}(\rho,\theta,t,\varepsilon)

in the error
\varepsilon_{j}^{4}S_{1}(W^{0},\Psi^{0})(\varepsilon_{j}y+P_{j})
computed in (
6.8
). This term can be decomposed as the sum of two parts, one depending on
{\partial}_{t}P
and one not:

{\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\over 1+|y|^{4}}E_{2}={\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\over 1+|y|^{4}}E_{2}^{*}[P]+{\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\over 1+|y|^{4}}E_{2}^{**}[P,\dot{P}].

In fact the origin of
{\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\over 1+|y|^{4}}E_{2}^{**}[P,\dot{P}]
is

\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}){\partial}_{t}w^{0}_{j}
,
|\log\varepsilon|B_{0}(w_{j}^{0})
and

{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(y,t;P)\cdot\nabla w_{j}^{0}
, and the dependence on
\dot{P}
is linear. We check that

{\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\over 1+|y|^{4}}E_{2}^{**}[P,{\partial}_{t}P]={\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\over 1+|y|^{4}}E_{2}^{**}[r_{0}{\bf e}_{1}+P^{0}+P^{1},(\dot{P}^{0}+\dot{P}^{1})]+{\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|\over 1+|y|^{4}}E_{2}

with
E_{2}
satisfying (
4.32
). See (
4.2
) for the assumptions on
P
,
P^{0}
and
P^{1}
.
We will remove the part of the error

{\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\over 1+|y|^{4}}E_{2}
given by

(6.9)

e_{1}:={\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\over 1+|y|^{4}}E_{2}^{*}[P]+{\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\over 1+|y|^{4}}E_{2}^{**}[(r_{0},0)+P^{0}+P^{1},(\dot{P}^{0}+\dot{P}^{1})],

thus leaving out what depends on
\dot{\bf a}
.
We will do it solving the simplified linear elliptic operator

L_{0}[\psi]:=-\frac{4}{\rho^{2}+1}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\theta}[\Delta_{y}\psi+f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0})\psi].

introduced in (
6.6
).
We freeze the time variable and consider the problem

(6.10)

\displaystyle{4\over 1+\rho^{2}}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\theta}

\displaystyle\left[\Delta\psi+e^{\Gamma_{0}(y)}\psi\right]+g(y)=0{\quad\hbox{in }}B(0,{8\over\varepsilon|\log\varepsilon|^{\zeta}}),\quad

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle=0{\quad\hbox{in }}\partial B(0,{8\over\varepsilon|\log\varepsilon|^{\zeta}}),\quad\phi=-\Delta_{5,j}\psi,

for a bounded function
g:B(0,{8\over\varepsilon|\log\varepsilon|^{\zeta}})\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
.
A necessary condition for the solvability of (
6.10
) is that

(6.11)

\int_{0}^{2\pi}g(\rho e^{i\theta})\,d\theta=0\quad\mbox{for all}\quad\rho\in(0,8R_{\varepsilon}),\quad R_{\varepsilon}={1\over\varepsilon|\log\varepsilon|^{\zeta}}.

As

L_{0}[Z_{\ell}]=0,\quad Z_{\ell}(y)={\partial}_{y_{\ell}}\Gamma_{0}(y).

we also assume the orthogonality conditions

(6.12)

\int_{B(0,8R_{\varepsilon})}(1+|y|^{2})\,g(y)\,Z_{\ell}(y)\,dy\,=\,0,\quad\ell=1,2.

We have the validity of the following result.

Lemma 6.1
.

Assume that
3\leq m\leq 5
and

(6.13)

|g(y)|\ \leq\ (1+|y|)^{-m}

There exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small and

g\in L^{\infty}(B(0,8R_{\varepsilon}))
that satisfies conditions
(\ref{ort0})
,
(\ref{ort2})
and
(\ref{decay})
,
there exists a unique solution
(\psi,\phi)
of equation
(\ref{linear1})
that satisfies

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\psi(\rho e^{i\theta})\,d\theta=0\quad\mbox{for all}\quad\rho\in(0,8R_{\varepsilon})

and the estimate

\displaystyle|\psi(y)|\,+\,(1+|y|)\,|{\nabla}\psi(y)|\,+\,(1+|y|^{2})\,|\phi(y)|

\displaystyle\leq\ C{(1+|y|)^{4-m}}\,\begin{cases}\log\Big(\frac{16\varepsilon^{-1}}{|y|+1}\Big)&\hbox{ if \hskip 10.22217pt}m=5\\
1&\hbox{ if \hskip 10.22217pt}3<m<5\\
\log\Big(\frac{16\varepsilon^{-1}}{|y|+1}\Big)&\hbox{ if \hskip 10.22217pt}m=3\end{cases}

Proof.

Let
y=\rho\,e^{i\theta}
and decompose
\psi
and
g
in Fourier series in the
\theta
-variable

g(\rho e^{i\theta})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}g_{n}(\rho)e^{i\,n\,\theta},\quad\psi(\rho e^{i\theta})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}p_{n}(\rho)e^{i\,n\,\theta}.

Condition (
6.11
) amounts to
g_{0}\equiv 0
. Imposing
p_{0}\equiv 0
,
equation (
6.10
) decouples into the infinitely many problems.

(6.14)

\mathcal{L}_{n}[p_{n}]:=\partial^{2}_{\rho}p_{n}+\frac{1}{\rho}\partial_{\rho}p_{n}-\frac{n^{2}}{\rho^{2}}p_{n}+\frac{8p_{n}}{(1+\rho^{2})^{2}}=\frac{i(1+\rho^{2})}{4n}g_{n}(\rho),\quad p_{n}(8R_{\varepsilon})=0.

For each
n\neq 0
, there exists a positive function
\zeta_{n}(\rho)
such that
\mathcal{L}_{n}[\zeta_{n}]=0
and

\zeta_{n}(\rho)=\rho^{|n|}(1+o(1))\quad\text{as }\rho\to 0

For
n=\pm 1
we explicitly have

\zeta_{n}(\rho)=\frac{\rho}{1+\rho^{2}}
, while for
|n|\geq 2
we have

\zeta_{n}(\rho)=\rho^{|n|}(1+o(1))\quad\text{as }\rho\to+\infty.

Problem (
6.14
) is uniquely solved by the formula

p_{n}(\rho)=\mathcal{L}_{n}^{-1}[g_{n}]\,:=\frac{i}{4n}\zeta_{n}(\rho)\int_{\rho}^{8R_{\varepsilon}}\frac{dr}{r\zeta_{n}(r)^{2}}\int_{0}^{r}(1+s^{2})g_{n}(s)\zeta_{n}(s)\,s\,ds.\quad

Let us consider the case
|n|\geq 2
. We have

|p_{n}(\rho)|\ \leq\ \mathcal{L}_{n}^{-1}[(1+\rho)^{-\alpha}]\leq c_{n}(1+\rho)^{|n|}\int_{\rho}^{8R_{\varepsilon}}(1+r)^{-|n|+3-\alpha}dr

so that

|p_{n}(\rho)|\ \leq\ c_{n}(1+\rho)^{4-\alpha}

since

m+|n|>4
.
Let us denote
\bar{P}(\rho)=\mathcal{L}_{2}^{-1}[(1+\rho)^{\alpha}]
we claim that for some
\gamma>0
and all
|n|\geq 2
we have the validity of
the estimate

|p_{n}(\rho)|\ \leq\ \frac{\gamma}{n^{3}}\bar{P}(\rho).

That follows from the fact that the right hand side defines a positive supersolution for the real and imaginary parts of (
6.14
). Indeed, if
\gamma
is taken sufficiently large we get

\mathcal{L}_{n}\big[\frac{\gamma}{n^{3}}\bar{P}(\rho)\big]+\frac{1+\rho^{2}}{4n}|g_{n}(\rho)|\leq\gamma\frac{4-n^{2}}{10\rho^{2}}(1+\rho)^{4+\alpha}

Fourier modes
\pm 1
need to be separately treated because
\zeta_{1}(\rho)
decays at infinity.
At this point we observe that

Z_{1}(y)=\zeta_{1}(\rho)\cos\theta,\quad Z_{2}(y)=\zeta_{1}(\rho)\sin\theta

and that the orthogonality conditions (
6.12
) assumed are equivalent to

\int_{0}^{8R_{\varepsilon}}(1+\rho^{2})\,g_{\pm 1}(\rho)\zeta_{1}(\rho)\,\rho\,d\rho=0.

Therefore we can write

p_{\pm 1}(\rho)=\mp\frac{i}{4}\zeta_{1}(\rho)\int_{\rho}^{8R_{\varepsilon}}\frac{dr}{r\zeta_{1}(r)^{2}}\int_{r}^{8R_{\varepsilon}}(1+s^{2})g_{\pm 1}(s)\zeta_{1}(s)\,s\,ds.\quad

and we obtain, if we now assume
3<m\leq 5
,

|p_{\pm 1}(\rho)|\ \lesssim\ \begin{cases}(1+\rho)^{-1}\log{\frac{16R_{\varepsilon}}{\rho+1}}&\hbox{ if }m=5\\
(1+\rho)^{4-m}&\hbox{ if }3<m<5\\
(1+\rho)\log{\frac{16R_{\varepsilon}}{\rho+1}}&\hbox{ if }m=3.\end{cases}

The desired result then follows from addition of the above estimates since

|\psi(y)|\ \leq\ \sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}|p_{n}(|y|)|.

Finally, since
\psi
satisfies the equation

\Delta\psi+e^{\Gamma}_{0}\psi=-\frac{1}{4}(1+\rho^{2})\int_{0}^{\theta}g(\rho,\theta)\,d\theta,{\quad\hbox{in }}B(0,8R_{\varepsilon}),\quad\psi=0{\quad\hbox{on }}{\partial}B(0,8R_{\varepsilon}),

the bounds for
\phi
and
{\nabla}\psi
follow from standard elliptic estimates.

∎

We extend the function
e_{1}
in (
6.9
) to be equal to
0
outside
B(0,3R_{j})
. Under the assumptions (
4.2
) on
P
,
P^{0}
and
P^{1}
, we have that

(1+|y|^{4})\left(|e_{1}(y,t)|+|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}|{\partial}_{t}e_{1}(y,t)|\right)\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|.

Moreover the function
e_{1}
satisfies authomatically the orthogonality conditions (
6.11
) and (
6.12
).
Let

\psi^{1}_{j}=\psi_{j}^{1}(y,t)
and
\phi_{j}^{1}(y,t)=-\Delta_{5,j}\psi_{j}^{1}
be the solution to (
6.10
) when
g=e_{1}
, as predicted by Lemma
6.1
. We have

|\psi^{1}_{j}(y,t)|\,+\,(1+|y|)\,|{\nabla}\psi_{j}^{1}(y,t)|\,\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|,\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}B(0,3R_{j})\times[0,T].

and

\,(1+|y|^{3})\,|\nabla_{y}\phi_{j}^{1}(y,t)|+\,(1+|y|^{2})\,|\phi_{j}^{1}(y,t)|\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}B(0,3R_{j})\times[0,T].

Having left out what depends on
\dot{\bf a}
in the part of the error
e_{1}
we are considering (see (
6.9
)), we can harmlessly differentiate in time equations (
6.10
), to also get

\,(1+|y|^{2})\,|{\partial}_{t}\phi_{j}^{1}(y,t)|\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}B(0,3R_{j})\times[0,T].

Besides, a consequence of the proof of Lemma
6.1
is that the Fourier decomposition of
\psi_{j}^{1}
only contains mode-2 terms. Hence the Fourier decomposition of the function
\phi_{j}^{1}(y,t)

has a mode-
2
term of size
\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|
, mode-
1
and mode-
3
terms of size
\varepsilon_{j}^{3}|\log\varepsilon|
or smaller.

Using these properties of the functions
\psi_{j}^{1}
,
\phi_{j}^{1}
, and the notations introduced in (
6.5
) we get

\displaystyle L_{1}(\phi_{j}^{1})={\varepsilon_{j}^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{3\over 2}\over 1+|y|^{2}}E_{2}+{\varepsilon_{j}^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{3\over 2}\over 1+|y|}E_{1},

\displaystyle\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma_{0}\cdot\nabla[{3\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}}{\partial}_{y_{1}}\psi_{j}^{1}]={\varepsilon_{j}^{3}|\log\varepsilon|\over 1+|y|^{3}}(E_{1}+E_{3})+{\varepsilon_{j}^{4}|\log\varepsilon|\over 1+|y|^{2}}(E_{0}+E_{2}),

and

\displaystyle L_{2}[\phi_{j}^{1}]+L_{3}[\psi_{j}^{1}]-L[\psi_{j}^{1}]+Q(\psi_{j}^{1},\phi_{j}^{1})={\varepsilon_{j}^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}\over 1+|y|^{3}}(E_{1}+E_{3})+{\varepsilon_{j}^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}\over 1+|y|^{2}}E_{i\geq 0}.

In the above expression
E_{j}
denotes the
j
-th mode in the Fourier decomposition of
E(y,t)
, in accordance with the notation introduced in (
3.11
).
Inserting this information in (
6.1
) we get the description of the new error, in the region
|x-P_{j}|<3|\log\varepsilon|^{-\zeta}
, for
y={x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}}
,

\displaystyle E_{j}^{in}

\displaystyle[\phi_{j}^{1},\psi_{j}^{1},0,P](y,t):=\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;P)\cdot\nabla U+{\varepsilon_{j}^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}\over 1+|y|^{3}}(E_{1}+E_{3})

\displaystyle+{\varepsilon_{j}^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}\over 1+|y|^{2}}(E_{0}+E_{2})+{\varepsilon_{j}^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}\over 1+|y|}\,E_{i\geq 0},

as
\varepsilon\to 0
, where
the functions
E_{j}(\rho,\theta,t,\varepsilon)
have the form described in (
4.32
). For our next improvement it is convenient to write the error as a term
of size
\varepsilon_{j}^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}
-size in Fourier mode
1
or higher, with spacial decay bounded by
{1\over 1+|y|^{3}}
, and that does depend on
P
,
\dot{P}^{0}
, but not on
\dot{P}^{1}+\dot{\bf a}
. We use the fact that the error depends in a linear way on
{\partial}_{t}P
and assumptions (
4.4
)-(
4.5
) to conclude that

(6.15)

\displaystyle E_{j}^{in}

\displaystyle[\phi_{j}^{1},\psi_{j}^{1},0,P](y,t):=\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;P)\cdot\nabla U+{\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}\over 1+|y|^{3}}(E_{1}+E_{3})[P,\dot{P}^{0}]++O\left({\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}\over 1+|y|^{2}}\right)

6.3.

Second inner improvement.
Our next step is to eliminate the terms in the error (
6.15
)
of size
\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}
. A difference with the first improvement is that this time these terms posses a mode
1
.
We will solve again an elliptic problem of the form (
6.10
), but only at the expenses of asking that the orthogonality conditions (
6.12
) for the right-hand side are satisfied. We shall see that this is possible with an adjustment of the points
P
.

In the process of the construction of the approximation
(\Psi^{*},W^{*})
, we will need to correct these points several times. All these corrections are encoded in the point we called
P^{1}
in (
4.2
)-(
4.4
). The final definition of
P^{1}
is given by

(6.16)

P^{1}(t)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{4}P^{1\ell}(t).

where
P^{1\ell}
correspond to successive explicit adjustments of
P^{1}
. Take
\ell=1
, for the first adjustment, and
for

\bar{P}^{1}=(\bar{P}^{1}_{1},\ldots,\bar{P}^{1}_{k}),\quad\bar{P}^{1}_{j}=(r_{0},0)+P^{0}_{j}+P_{j}^{11},

we take

e_{2}(y,t):=\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;\bar{P}^{1})\cdot\nabla U+{\varepsilon_{j}^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}\over 1+|y|^{3}}(E_{1}+E_{3})[\bar{P}^{1},{\partial}_{t}P^{0}](\rho,\theta,t,\varepsilon).

We directly check that
\int_{0}^{2\pi}e_{2}(\rho e^{i\theta},t)\,d\theta=0
, and
e_{2}
satisfies the decay (
6.13
) with
m=3
. In fact, under the assumptions (
4.2
) on
P
, we have

(1+|y|^{3})(|e_{2}(y,t)|+|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}|{\partial}_{t}e_{2}(y,t)|)\lesssim\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{2},\quad|y|<3R_{j}.

The orthogonality conditions

\int_{B_{8R_{j}}}(1+|y|^{2})e_{2}(y,y)Z_{\ell}(y)\,dy=0,\quad\ell=1,2

become a system of ODEs for the point
P^{11}=(P_{1}^{11},\ldots,P_{k}^{11})
that has the form

(6.17)

\varepsilon_{j}|\log\varepsilon|\left[\dot{P}_{j}^{11}+AP^{11}+|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}}B(P^{11})\right]=\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{3}f(\bar{P}^{1})

where
A
is the
2\times 2
matrix defined by

A=|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}D^{2}_{x}\varphi_{j}(\bar{P}_{j}^{0};P^{0}),\quad t\in[0,T),

and
B

B(P^{11})=|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}[{\nabla}_{x}\varphi_{j}(\bar{P}_{j}^{1};\bar{P}^{1})-{\nabla}_{x}\varphi_{j}(P_{j}^{0};P^{0})-D^{2}_{x}\varphi_{j}(\bar{P}_{j}^{0};P^{0})P^{11}].

See (
4.28
) for the definition of
\varphi_{j}
. Under our assumptions (
4.3
) on the points
P^{0}
, we have

A=O(1),\quad B(P^{11})=(|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}}),\quad t\in[0,T),

uniformly as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Besides,
f(\bar{P}^{1})
is a smooth function of
P^{11}
, which is uniformly bounded, together with its derivative, for
t\in[0,T)
, uniformly as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
Standard ODEs theory gives that, for all
\varepsilon>0
small enough there exists a unique solution
P^{11}
to (
6.17
) with initial condition
P^{11}(0)=0
, which satisfy the bounds

\|P^{11}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}[0,T)}+\|\dot{P}^{11}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}[0,T)}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}.

We denote by
\psi_{j}^{2}=\psi_{j}^{2}(y,t)
and
\phi_{j}^{2}(y,t)=-\Delta_{5,j}\psi_{j}^{2}
the solution to (
6.10
), with
g=e_{2}
,
whose existence and estimates are given by Lemma
6.1
. We have that
\int_{0}^{2\pi}\psi_{j}^{2}(\rho e^{i\theta},t)\,d\theta=0
and

|\psi^{2}_{j}(y,t)|\,+\,(1+|y|)\,|{\nabla}\psi_{j}^{2}(y,t)|\,\lesssim\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{3}\,(1+|y|),\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}B(0,3R_{j})\times[0,T].

We also have

\,(1+|y|^{2})\,|\nabla_{y}\phi_{j}^{2}(y,t)|+\,(1+|y|)\,|\phi_{j}^{2}(y,t)|\lesssim\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{3}\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}B(0,3R_{j})\times[0,T].

For the same reason we did it before, we can differentiate in time the equation, to also get

\,(1+|y|)\,|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}\,|{\partial}_{t}\phi_{j}^{2}(y,t)|\lesssim\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{3}\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}B(0,3R_{j})\times[0,T].

From Lemma
6.1
we also get that the Fourier decomposition of
\psi_{j}^{2}
only contains Fourier mode-
1
and mode-
3
terms. Hence the Fourier decomposition of the function
\phi_{j}^{2}(y,t)

has mode-
1
and mode-
3
terms of size
\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{3}
, terms of mode-
0
, mode-
2
or higher of size
\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{3}
or smaller.
Using these properties and the notations introduced in (
6.5
) we get

\displaystyle L_{1}(\phi_{j}^{2})={\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{7\over 2}\over 1+|y|}E_{i\geq 0},

\displaystyle\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma_{0}\cdot\nabla[{3\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}}{\partial}_{y_{1}}\psi^{2}_{j}]={\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{3}\over 1+|y|^{2}}(E_{0}+E_{i\geq 2})+{\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{3}\over 1+|y|}E_{i\geq 0},

\displaystyle L_{2}[\phi_{j}^{2}]+L_{3}[\psi_{j}^{2}]-L[\psi_{j}^{2}]+Q(\psi_{j}^{2},\phi_{j}^{2})={\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{4}\over 1+|y|^{2}}(E_{0}+E_{i\geq 2})+{\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{4}\over 1+|y|}E_{i\geq 0}

and

\displaystyle Q(\psi_{j}^{1},\phi_{j}^{2})+Q(\psi_{j}^{2},\phi_{j}^{1})={\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{4}\over 1+|y|}E_{i\geq 0}.

Define

\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\phi_{j}^{i},\quad\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\psi_{j}^{i}.

We get the
new error

\displaystyle E_{j}^{in}

\displaystyle[\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{2},\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{2},0,P](y,t):=[\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;P)-\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;\bar{P}^{1})]\cdot\nabla U

(6.18)

\displaystyle+{\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{4}\over 1+|y|^{2}}\,(E_{0}+E_{i\geq 2})[P,\dot{P}]+{\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{4}\over 1+|y|}\,E_{i\geq 0}[P,\dot{P}].

Using the definition of
{\mathcal{R}}^{00}_{j}
in (
6.7
), we check that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;P)

\displaystyle-{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;\bar{P}^{1})=\varepsilon_{j}|\log\varepsilon|(\dot{P}_{j}-{d\over dt}\bar{P}_{j}^{1})^{\perp}\cdot y

\displaystyle+\varepsilon_{j}[{\nabla}_{x}\varphi_{j}(P_{j};P)-{\nabla}_{x}\varphi_{j}(\bar{P}_{j}^{1};\bar{P}^{1})]\cdot y

\displaystyle+\,\varepsilon_{j}^{3}D_{x}\theta_{j\varepsilon_{j}}(P_{j};P)[y]-\varepsilon_{j}^{3}D_{x}\theta_{j\varepsilon_{j}}(\bar{P}^{1}_{j};\bar{P}^{1})[y].

6.4.

Third inner improvement.
Our next step is the elimination of the Fourier mode-
0
term of size
\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{4}
in formula (
6.3
). We define
\phi_{j}^{3}
as follows

\phi_{j}^{3}(y,t)=-{\varepsilon^{4}\,|\log\varepsilon|^{3}\over 1+|y|^{2}}\,\int_{0}^{t}\varepsilon_{j}^{-2}(s)E_{0}(\rho,\theta,s,\varepsilon)\,ds.

It solves

\displaystyle|\log\varepsilon|\varepsilon_{j}^{2}{\partial}_{t}\phi_{j}^{3}

\displaystyle+{\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{4}\over 1+|y|^{2}}\,E_{0}=0\quad(y,t)\in B(0,3R_{j})\times[0,T)

\displaystyle\phi_{j}^{3}(y,0)

\displaystyle=0,

it satisfies

(1+|y|)|\nabla_{y}\phi_{j}^{3}(y,t)|+|\phi_{j}^{3}(y,t)|\lesssim{\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{3}\over 1+|y|^{2}}\,,\quad

and its Fourier decomposition only has mode
0
.
Let
\psi_{j}^{3}
be the solution to

-\Delta_{5,j}\psi_{j}^{3}=\phi_{j}^{3}\quad(y,t)\in B(0,4R_{j})\times[0,T),\quad\psi_{j}^{3}=0\quad(y,t)\in\partial B(0,4R_{j})\times[0,T).

Let
p(y,t)
, with
p=0
on
\partial B(0,4R_{j})
be the positive radial solution to

\Delta_{y}p+{4\over 1+|y|^{2}}=0,\quad y\in B(0,4R_{j}),\quad p(\rho,t)=4\int_{\rho}^{4R_{j}}{\log(1+s^{2})\over s}\,ds.

Then

\Delta_{y}p+{3\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}}{\partial}_{1}p+{2\over 1+|y|^{2}}\leq 0,

thanks to the fact that
R_{j}={\delta\over\varepsilon_{j}|\log\varepsilon|^{\zeta}}
with
\zeta>1
. Take
\bar{\psi}(y,t)=M\,\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{3}\,p
, for some
M>0
. This is a super solution for

\Delta_{5,j}\psi+\phi_{j}^{3}\leq 0,

and gives that

|\psi_{j}^{3}(y,t)|+|(1+|y|)\nabla\psi_{j}^{3}(y,t)|\lesssim\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,|\log\varepsilon|^{5}.

Besides the Fourier decomposition
of the function
\psi_{j}^{3}

has mode-
0
terms of size
\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{5}
, terms of mode-
1
or higher of size
\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{5}
or smaller.

It is important to notice that
B_{0}(g)
only contains Fourier mode-
2
or mode-
1
terms if
g
is a Fourier mode-
0
function. We refer to (
4.21
) and Remark
4.1
for the definition of
B_{0}
and equivalent formulations. This observation yields that

L_{1}(\phi_{j}^{3})-|\log\varepsilon|\varepsilon_{j}^{2}{\partial}_{t}\phi_{j}^{3}={\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{4}\over 1+|y|^{2}}E_{2}+{\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{4}\over 1+|y|}E_{1}.

If
f
is a Fourier mode-
2
function and
g
is a Fourier mode-
0
function, then

\nabla^{\perp}f\cdot\nabla g

is a Fourier mode-
2
function. We use this, together with the explicity expression of
{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{0}
in (
6.1
) and the explicit form of the operator
L_{2}
in (
6.5
) to get

L_{2}(\phi_{j}^{3})={\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{4}\over 1+|y|^{3}}E_{1,2}\sin\theta+{\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{4}\over 1+|y|^{2}}E_{2}+{\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{4}\over 1+|y|}E_{i\geq 0}.

The key fact is that the main error of size
\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{4}
has a Fourier mode-
1
term, but only containing
\sin\theta
, not
\cos\theta
: it is of the form described in (
4.32
) for
j=1
and
E_{j,1}=0
. A similar expression is valid for
L_{3}(\psi_{j}^{3})
, with two more powers of
|\log\varepsilon|
:

L_{3}(\psi_{j}^{3})={\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{6}\over 1+|y|^{3}}E_{1,2}\sin\theta+{\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{6}\over 1+|y|^{2}}E_{2}+{\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{6}\over 1+|y|}E_{i\geq 0}.

We also have

Q(\psi_{j}^{3},\phi_{j}^{3})={\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{8}\over 1+|y|}E_{i\geq 0}

Q(\psi_{j}^{1},\phi_{j}^{2})+Q(\psi_{j}^{2},\phi_{j}^{1})={\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{6}\over 1+|y|^{4}}E_{2}+{\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{6}\over 1+|y|}E_{i\geq 0}

With this in mind, calling

\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{3}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\phi_{j}^{i},\quad\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{3}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\psi_{j}^{i}

we get the new error

\displaystyle E_{j}^{in}

\displaystyle[\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{3},\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{3},0,P](y,t):=[\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;P)-\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;\bar{P}^{1})]\cdot\nabla U

(6.19)

\displaystyle+{\varepsilon_{j}^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{6}\over 1+|y|^{3}}\,E_{1,2}\sin\theta+{\varepsilon_{j}^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{6}\over 1+|y|^{2}}\,E_{2}[P,\dot{P}]+{\varepsilon_{j}^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{8}\over 1+|y|}\,E_{i\geq 0}[P,\dot{P}].

If we compare this error
E_{j}^{in}[\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{3},\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{3},0,P]
with the error

E_{j}^{in}[\phi_{j}^{1},\psi_{j}^{1},0,P]
in (
6.15
), we observe a crucial difference: even though their main term have size
\varepsilon^{3}
(multiplied by powers of
|\log\varepsilon|
) and are in Fourier mode-
1
, in
E_{j}^{in}[\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{3},\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{3},0,P]
Fourier mode-
1
enters only with a
\sin\theta
. We shall proceed as in the second improvement of the approximation, with adjusting the points
P^{1}
and solving the same elliptic linear problem, but this time the new error will not have Fourier mode-
0
of size
\varepsilon^{4}
. This subtle fact allows us to proceed with the construction.

6.5.

Fourth inner improvement.
Our next step is to eliminate the term
{\varepsilon_{j}^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{6}\over 1+|y|^{3}}\,E_{1,2}\sin\theta
in the error (
6.4
).
Take
\ell=2
in the decomposition (
6.16
) of
P^{1}(t)
and
for
\bar{P}^{2}=(\bar{P}^{2}_{1},\ldots,\bar{P}^{2}_{k})
,
\bar{P}^{2}_{j}=P_{0}+P_{j}^{0}+P_{j}^{11}+P_{j}^{12}
, we take

e_{4}(y,t):=\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;\bar{P}^{2})\cdot\nabla U+{\varepsilon_{j}^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{6}\over 1+|y|^{3}}\,E_{1,2}[\bar{P}^{2},(\dot{P}^{0}+\dot{P}^{11})]\sin\theta.

We have
\int_{0}^{2\pi}e_{4}(\rho e^{i\theta},t)\,d\theta=0
, and
e_{4}
satisfies the decay (
6.13
) with
m=3
. In fact, under the assumptions (
4.2
) on
P
, we have

(1+|y|^{3})(|e_{4}(y,t)|+|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}|{\partial}_{t}e_{4}(y,t)|)\lesssim\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{5},\quad|y|<3R_{j}.

The orthogonality conditions

\int_{B_{8R_{j}}}(1+|y|^{2})e_{4}(y,y)Z_{\ell}(y)\,dy=0,\quad\ell=1,2

become a system of ODEs for the point
P^{12}=(P_{1}^{12},\ldots,P_{k}^{12})
of the same form as (
6.17
).
Standard ODEs theory gives that, for all
\varepsilon>0
small enough there exists a unique solution
P^{12}
to (
6.17
) with initial condition
P^{12}(0)=0
, which satisfy the bounds

\|P^{12}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}[0,T)}+\|\dot{P}^{12}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}[0,T)}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{6}.

We denote by
\psi_{j}^{4}=\psi_{j}^{4}(y,t)
and
\phi_{j}^{4}(y,t)=-\Delta_{5,j}\psi_{j}^{4}
the solution to (
6.10
), with
g=e_{4}
,
whose existence and estimates are given by Lemma
6.1
. We have that
\int_{0}^{2\pi}\psi_{j}^{4}(\rho e^{i\theta},t)\,d\theta=0
and

|\psi^{4}_{j}(y,t)|\,+\,(1+|y|)\,|{\nabla}\psi_{j}^{4}(y,t)|\,\lesssim\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{7}\,(1+|y|),\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}B(0,3R_{j})\times[0,T].

We also have

\,(1+|y|^{2})\,|\nabla_{y}\phi_{j}^{4}(y,t)|+\,(1+|y|)\,|\phi_{j}^{4}(y,t)|\lesssim\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{7}\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}B(0,3R_{j})\times[0,T].

We can differentiate in time the equation, to also get

\,(1+|y|)\,|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}\,|{\partial}_{t}\phi_{j}^{4}(y,t)|\lesssim\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{7}\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}B(0,3R_{j})\times[0,T].

From Lemma
6.1
we infer that is that the Fourier decomposition of
\psi_{j}^{4}
contains Fourier mode-
1
terms, but only with
\cos\theta
. Hence the Fourier decomposition of the function
\phi_{j}^{4}(y,t)

has mode-
1
terms with
\cos\theta
of size
\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{7}
, terms of mode-
0
, mode-
2
or higher of size
\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{7}
or smaller.
Using these properties and the notations introduced in (
6.5
) we get

\displaystyle L_{1}(\phi_{j}^{4})={\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{7}\over 1+|y|}E_{i\geq 0},

\displaystyle\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma_{0}\cdot\nabla[{3\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}}{\partial}_{y_{1}}\psi^{4}_{j}]={\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{7}\over 1+|y|^{2}}E_{i\geq 2}+{\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{7}\over 1+|y|}E_{i\geq 0},

\displaystyle L_{2}[\phi_{j}^{4}]+L_{3}[\psi_{j}^{4}]-L[\psi_{j}^{4}]+Q(\psi_{j}^{4},\phi_{j}^{4})={\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{8}\over 1+|y|^{2}}E_{i\geq 2}+{\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{4}\over 1+|y|}E_{i\geq 0}

and

\displaystyle Q(\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{3},\phi_{j}^{4})+Q(\psi_{j}^{4},\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{3})={\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{8}\over 1+|y|}E_{i\geq 0}.

Define

\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{4}=\sum_{i=1}^{4}\phi_{j}^{i},\quad\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{4}=\sum_{i=1}^{4}\psi_{j}^{i}.

We get the
new error, for
y\in B(0,3R_{j})
,

\displaystyle E_{j}^{in}

\displaystyle[\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{4},\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{4},0,P](y,t):=[\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;P)-\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;\bar{P}^{2})]\cdot\nabla U

(6.20)

\displaystyle+{\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{8}\over 1+|y|^{2}}\,E_{i\geq 2}+{\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{8}\over 1+|y|}\,E_{i\geq 0}[P,\dot{P}].

The
0
-th Fourier mode of the new error comes with size
\varepsilon^{5}
(and powers of
|\log\varepsilon|
).

6.6.

Fifth inner improvement.
We now remove part of the Fourier mode-
2
or higher terms of size
\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{8}
in (
6.5
). As in the first inner improvement, we take just the parts that depend on
P
and on
\dot{P}^{0}
,
\dot{P}^{1}
, but not the ones depending on
\dot{\bf a}
, and we call it
e_{5}
.
We extend the function
e_{5}
in (
6.9
) to be equal to
0
outside
B_{j}=B(0,3R_{j})
. Under the assumptions (
4.2
) on
P
,
P^{0}
and
P^{1}
, we have that

(1+|y|^{2})(|e_{5}(y,t)|+|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}|{\partial}_{t}e_{5}(y,t)|)\lesssim\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{8}.

Moreover the function
e_{5}
satisfies authomatically the orthogonality conditions (
6.11
) and (
6.12
).
Let

\psi^{5}_{j}
and
\phi_{j}^{5}(y,t)=-\Delta_{5,j}\psi_{j}^{5}
be the solution to (
6.10
) when
g=e_{5}
, as predicted by Lemma
6.1
. We have

|\psi^{5}_{j}(y,t)|\,+\,(1+|y|)\,|{\nabla}\psi_{j}^{5}(y,t)|\,\lesssim\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{8}(1+|y|^{2}),\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}B(0,8R_{j})\times[0,T].

and

\,(1+|y|)\,|\nabla_{y}\phi_{j}^{5}(y,t)|+\,\,|\phi_{j}^{5}(y,t)|\lesssim\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{8}\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}B(0,8R_{j})\times[0,T].

We can differentiate in time equations (
6.10
), to also get

\,\,|{\partial}_{t}\phi_{j}^{5}(y,t)|\lesssim\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{8}\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}B(0,8R_{j})\times[0,T].

Define

\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{5}=\sum_{i=1}^{5}\phi_{j}^{i},\quad\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{5}=\sum_{i=1}^{5}\psi_{j}^{i}.

We get the
new error, for
y\in B(0,3R_{j})

(6.21)

\displaystyle E_{j}^{in}

\displaystyle[\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{5},\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{5},0,P](y,t):=[\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;P)-\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;\bar{P}^{2})]\cdot\nabla U+{\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{8}\over 1+|y|}\,E_{i\geq 0}.

6.7.

Sixth inner improvement.
Our next step is the elimination of the
\varepsilon_{j}^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{8}
-term in (
6.21
),
{\varepsilon_{j}^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{8}\over 1+|y|}\,E_{i\geq 0}
, to get faster decay in the
y
variable. To do so, rather than solving an elliptic problem or an ODEs,
we solve the transport-type equation

\displaystyle L_{1}(\phi_{j})+L_{2}(\phi_{j})

\displaystyle=|\log\varepsilon|\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}){\partial}_{t}\phi_{j}+|\log\varepsilon|B_{0}(\phi_{j})

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\perp}\left(\Gamma_{0}+\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}}\,y_{1}\,\Big(\Gamma_{0}(y)+\bar{A}+\Gamma(y)\Big)\,+{\mathcal{R}}^{0}_{j}(y,t;P)\right)\cdot\nabla\phi_{j}=E(y,t),

in
B_{3R_{j}}\times[0,T]
,
with initial condition
\phi(y,0)=0
in
B(0,3R_{j})
.
We write the operator
B_{0}
defined in (
4.21
) as follows

\displaystyle|\log\varepsilon|B_{0}(\phi)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{B}}_{j}^{0}(y,t;P)\cdot\nabla\phi

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{j}^{0}(y,t;P)

\displaystyle=-{|\log\varepsilon|\over 2}{d\over dt}(\varepsilon_{j}^{2})(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})\,y-|\log\varepsilon|{\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\dot{P}_{j}.

We will need uniform differentiability in
t
of the coefficients
{{\mathcal{R}}}_{j}^{0}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{j}^{0}
. Thus we consider the following slightly-modified transport equation

(6.22)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}){\partial}_{t}\phi&+\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0}(y)+\tilde{{\mathcal{R}}}_{j}(y,t;P))\cdot\nabla_{y}\phi+{\mathcal{B}}_{j}(y,t;P)\cdot\nabla_{y}\phi\\
&=E(y,t),\quad\text{in }B(0,3R_{j})\times[0,T]\\
\phi(y,0)&=0,\quad\text{in }B(0,3R_{j})\end{aligned}\right.

Here

\tilde{{\mathcal{R}}}_{j}(y,t)=\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}}\,y_{1}\,\Big(\Gamma_{0}(y)+\bar{A}+\Gamma(y)\Big)+{\mathcal{R}}^{1}_{j}(y,t;P)

where

{\mathcal{R}}^{0}_{j}(y,t;P)={\mathcal{R}}^{1}_{j}(y,t;P)+\varepsilon_{j}|\log\varepsilon|\,\dot{\bf a}_{j}\cdot y

see (
6.1
). In other words, we leave out the term involving
\dot{\bf a}
. We do the same to
{\mathcal{B}}_{j}^{0}
: using the fact that
{d\over dt}\varepsilon_{j}^{2}=-r_{0}\varepsilon^{2}{\dot{r}_{j}\over r_{j}^{2}}
, we write

{\mathcal{B}}_{j}^{0}(y,t;P)={\mathcal{B}}_{j}(y,t;P)+{|\log\varepsilon|\varepsilon^{2}r_{0}\over 2}{\dot{\bf a}_{j1}\over r_{j}^{2}}(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})y-|\log\varepsilon|{\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\dot{\bf a}_{j}.

It is straightforward to check that, under our assumptions (
4.2
), (
4.3
) and (
4.4
), for
y\in B(0,3R_{j})
we have

(6.23)

\displaystyle|{{\mathcal{R}}}^{1}_{j}(y,t)|+|{\partial}_{t}{{\mathcal{R}}}^{1}_{j}(y,t)|

\displaystyle\leq\ M\varepsilon^{2}|y|^{2},

\displaystyle|\nabla_{y}{{\mathcal{R}}}^{1}_{j}(y,t)|+|{\partial}_{t}\nabla_{y}{{\mathcal{R}}}^{1}_{j}(y,t)|

\displaystyle\leq\ M\varepsilon^{2}|y|,

\displaystyle|D^{2}_{y}{{\mathcal{R}}}^{1}_{j}(y,t)|

\displaystyle\leq\ M\varepsilon^{2},

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{B}}_{j}(y,t)|+|{\partial}_{t}{\mathcal{B}}_{j}(y,t)|

\displaystyle\leq\ M\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}|y|,

\displaystyle|\nabla_{y}{\mathcal{B}}_{j}(y,t)|

\displaystyle\leq\ M\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2},

for some positive constant
M
independent of
\varepsilon
.

We consider a smooth cut-off function
\eta_{4}(s)
as in (
2.2
), and take

(6.24)

\eta_{4\varepsilon}(y)=\eta_{4}(|\log\varepsilon|^{\zeta}\,\varepsilon\,|y|).

We will then have a solution to (
6.22
) by restricting to
B(0,3R_{j})
the solution of the Cauchy problem

(6.25)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|(1+\eta_{4\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}){\partial}_{t}\phi&+\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0}(y)+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\tilde{{\mathcal{R}}}_{j}(y,t;\xi))\cdot\nabla_{y}\phi+\eta_{4\varepsilon}{\mathcal{B}}_{j}(y,t;P)\cdot\nabla_{y}\phi\\
&=\eta_{4\varepsilon}E(y,t),\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]\\
\phi(y,0)&=0,\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\end{aligned}\right.

In §
7
we prove the following result

Lemma 6.2
.

Let us assume that
\tilde{{\mathcal{R}}}_{j}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{j}
satisfy
(\ref{papa-new})
.
Then there exist numbers
C,\delta>0
such that for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon
and any function
E(y,t)
that satisfies for some
C,\alpha\in\mathbb{R}

(1+|y|^{2})|D^{2}_{y}E(y,t)|+(1+|y|)\left(|{\nabla}_{y}E(y,t)|+|{\partial}_{t}{\nabla}_{y}E(y,t)|\right)+|E(y,t)|+|{\partial}_{t}E(y,t)|\ \leq\ C\,(1+|y|)^{\alpha},

the solution of
(\ref{pico1})
satisfies

(1+|y|)|{\nabla}_{y}\phi(y,t)|+|\phi(y,t)|\ \leq\ C\varepsilon^{-2}|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}C(1+|y|)^{\alpha}

for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, with
|y|<4\varepsilon^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|^{-\zeta}
,
t\in[0,T)
.

We want to apply Lemma
6.2
for the right hand side

E(y,t)=e_{6},\quad e_{6}={\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{8}\over 1+|y|}\,E_{i\geq 0}(\rho,\theta,t,\varepsilon)

where we freeze this term at
P_{j}=P_{0}+P_{j}^{0}+P_{j}^{1}
.
We define
\phi_{j}^{6}
to be the solution to problem (
6.22
), with
E
as above, predicted by Lemma
6.2
. It satisfies

(1+|y|)|{\nabla}_{y}\phi^{6}_{j}(y,t)|+|\phi^{6}_{j}(y,t)|\ \leq\ C{\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{7}\over 1+|y|}.

Let
\psi_{j}^{6}
be the solution to

-\Delta_{5,j}\psi_{j}^{6}=\phi_{j}^{6}\quad(y,t)\in B(0,4R_{j})\times[0,T),\quad\psi_{j}^{6}=0\quad(y,t)\in\partial B(0,4R_{j})\times[0,T).

Let
p(y,t)
, with
p=0
on
\partial B(0,4R_{j})
be the positive smooth radial solution to

\Delta_{y}p+{4\over 1+|y|}=0,\quad y\in B(0,4R_{j}).

Then
p(y)=16(R_{j}-|y|)-4\int_{|y|}^{4R_{j}}{\log(1+s)\over s}\,ds
, and
|p(y)|\lesssim(1+|y|)
and

\Delta_{y}p+{3\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}}{\partial}_{1}p+{2\over 1+|y|}\leq 0.

Take
\bar{\psi}(y,t)=M\,\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{7}\,p
, for some
M>0
. It is a super solution for

\Delta_{5,j}\psi+\phi_{j}^{6}\leq 0,

and gives that

(1+|y|^{2})|D^{2}_{y}\psi^{6}_{j}(y,t)|+(1+|y|)|{\nabla}_{y}\psi^{6}_{j}(y,t)|+|\psi^{6}_{j}(y,t)|\ \leq\ C\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{8}(1+|y|).

We use this information to compute the following terms

\displaystyle L_{3}(\psi_{j}^{6})+Q(\psi_{j}^{6},\phi_{j}^{6})={\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{8}\over 1+|y|^{5}}E_{i\geq 1},\quad Q(\psi_{j}^{6},\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{5})+Q(\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{5},\phi_{j}^{6})={\varepsilon_{j}^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{8}\over 1+|y|^{3}}E_{i\geq 0}

Define

\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{6}=\sum_{i=1}^{6}\phi_{j}^{i},\quad\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{6}=\sum_{i=1}^{6}\psi_{j}^{i}.

We get the
new error

\displaystyle E_{j}^{in}

\displaystyle[\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{6},\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{6},0,P](y,t):=[\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;P)-\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;\bar{P}^{2})]\cdot\nabla U

(6.26)

\displaystyle+{\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{8}\over 1+|y|^{5}}E_{i\geq 1}+{\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{8}\over 1+|y|^{3}}\,E_{i\geq 0}.

If we compare this error with the one in (
6.15
), we see that both their main terms have size
\varepsilon^{3}
multiplied by a power of
|\log\varepsilon|
, and have Fourier mode-
1
. The difference is in their decay in the
y
-variable: the error in (
6.7
) has a much faster decay, which will be crucial to make the final argument of our construction work. We explain this in Section
9
where the
inner-outer
scheme is described in detail.

At this point of our construction we choose
\zeta
in (
5.3
). Take

\zeta=3.

Then

W^{0}(x)+{\eta_{j1}\over r_{j}\varepsilon_{j}^{2}}\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{6}({x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}},t)={1\over r_{j}\varepsilon_{j}^{2}}U({x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}})(1+o(1)),\quad\mbox{as}\quad\varepsilon\to 0

for
|x-P_{j}|<|\log\varepsilon|^{-3}
.

6.8.

The outer improvement.
So far we have modified the approximate solution in the inner regions, namely at a small distance from the points
P_{j}
. We will now improve the outer error given by

\displaystyle E_{0}(x,t;P)

\displaystyle=E^{out}[0,0,\tilde{\phi}^{6},\tilde{\psi}^{6},P](x,t)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\left[r\,|\log\varepsilon|\,{\partial}_{t}\bar{\eta}_{j1}+\nabla_{x}^{\perp}(r^{2}(\Psi^{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\eta_{j2}\over r_{j}}\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{6}({x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}})-r_{0}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|))\nabla\bar{\eta}_{1j}\right]{\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{6}\over\varepsilon_{j}^{2}r_{j}}

\displaystyle+\left[\sum_{j=1}^{k}(\eta_{2j}-\eta_{1j})\nabla_{x}^{\perp}(r^{2}{\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{6}\over r_{j}})+{r^{2}\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{6}\over r_{j}}\nabla_{x}^{\perp}\eta_{2j}\right]\nabla_{x}W^{0}

\displaystyle+(1-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\eta_{j1})S_{1}(W^{0},\Psi^{0})\quad(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T)

We also want to reduce the size of
S_{2}[\tilde{\phi}^{6},\tilde{\psi}^{6},0,0,P]
.
We refer to (
2.3
) and also (
5.7
).
Observe that the function
\Psi^{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\eta_{j2}\over r_{j}}\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{6}({x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}})
satisfies the
conditions on the boundary and at infinity, see (
4.18
): for all
t\in[0,T]

{\partial\psi\over\partial r}(x,t)=0,\quad{\mbox{on}}\quad{\partial}\Sigma,\quad|\psi(x,t)|\to 0,\quad\quad\mbox{as}\quad|x|\to\infty.

To reduce the outer error, we first solve in
\phi^{out}
the
outer transport equation

(6.27)

\displaystyle|\log\varepsilon|\,r\,{\partial}_{t}\phi^{out}

\displaystyle+\nabla_{x}^{\perp}(r^{2}(\Psi^{0}-r_{0}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|))\nabla_{x}\phi^{out}=E_{0},\quad{\mbox{in}}\quad\Sigma\times[0,T)

\displaystyle\phi^{out}(x,0)

\displaystyle=0,\quad\quad{\mbox{in}}\quad\Sigma.

We define

(6.28)

\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{0}(x,t)={E_{0}(x,t)\over r},\quad|\log\varepsilon|B(x,t)={1\over r}\nabla_{x}^{\perp}(r^{2}(\Psi^{0}-r_{0}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|))

and re-write (
6.27
) as

(6.29)

\displaystyle|\log\varepsilon|\,{\partial}_{t}\phi^{out}+|\log\varepsilon|B\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi^{out}

\displaystyle=\tilde{E}_{0},\quad{\mbox{in}}\quad\Sigma\times[0,T)

\displaystyle\phi^{out}(x,0)

\displaystyle=0,\quad\quad{\mbox{in}}\quad\Sigma.

From the very definition of
\Psi^{0}

the following properties for
B(x,t)
follow: if
B(x,t)=(B_{1}(x,t),B_{2}(x,t))
, we have

(6.30)

\displaystyle B_{1}(x,t)

\displaystyle=0{\quad\hbox{in }}(x,t)\in{\partial}\Sigma\times[0,T]

and

(6.31)

\displaystyle B(x,t)

\displaystyle=O(1){\quad\hbox{in }}(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T],\quad\quad\mbox{as}\quad\varepsilon\to 0.

Since the function
B
is continuous and log-Lipschitz in
x
uniformly in
t
, for
(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T)
,
we can represent the solution to (
6.29
)
using the Duhamel’s representation formula

(6.32)

\phi^{out}(x,t)=|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{E}_{0}(\bar{x}(s;x,t),s)\,ds

where
\bar{x}(s;x,t)
are the characteristic curves defined as

(6.33)

\,{d\over ds}\bar{x}(s;x,t)=B(\bar{x}(s;x,t),s)\quad s\in(0,t),\quad\bar{x}(t;x,t)=x,

which exist and are unique, mainly thanks to (
6.30
) as we shall prove later.

The right-hand side of our equation (
6.27
) is supported away from the vortices
P_{j}(t)
at a distance proportional to
|\log\varepsilon|^{-3}
. We assume that for some fixed number
\delta^{\prime}
we have

(6.34)

\tilde{E}_{0},\,E_{0}\equiv 0{\quad\hbox{in }}\Big\{(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T]\ /\ x\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}B(P_{j}(t),\delta^{\prime}|\log\varepsilon|^{-3})\Big\}.

We have the validity of the following

Lemma 6.3
.

For
x\in\Sigma
the characteristic curves (
6.33
) satisfy

\bar{x}(s;t,x)\in\Sigma
for all
0\leq s\leq t
. The solution of
(\ref{transport-outer})
given by
(\ref{phi3})
satisfies for any
1\leq p\leq+\infty
,

\|\phi(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}t\,\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\|\tilde{E}_{0}(\cdot,s)\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}.

If
E_{0}
satisfies
(\ref{assE})
, there exists a number
\delta^{*}>0
independent of
\delta^{\prime}
and
\varepsilon
such that the solution of
(\ref{transport-outer})

satisfies

\phi\equiv 0{\quad\hbox{in }}\Big\{(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T]\ /\ x\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}B(P_{j}(t),\delta^{*}|\log\varepsilon|^{-3})\Big\}.

Moreover, if
\tilde{E}_{0}
satisfies
(\ref{assE})

and

(1+|x|)|{\nabla}_{x}\tilde{E}_{0}(x,t)|+|\tilde{E}_{0}(x,t)|\leq{A\over 1+|x|^{4}}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T],

then there exists
C>0
such that
the solution of
(\ref{transport-outer})
satisfies
the estimate

(6.35)

(1+|x|)|{\nabla}_{x}\phi(x,t)|+|{\partial}_{t}\phi(x,t)|+|\phi(x,t)|\ \leq\ C\,|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}{A\over 1+|x|^{4}}.

For the proof of Lemmas
6.3
see §
8
.

From (
4.34
), we explicitly compute

\displaystyle{(1-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\eta_{j1})S_{1}(W^{0},\Psi^{0})\over r}

\displaystyle=|\log\varepsilon|(1-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\eta_{j1})\,\eta\sum_{j=1}^{k}\biggl[{\partial}_{t}W^{0}_{j}+{r\over|\log\varepsilon|}{\nabla}^{\perp}(\Psi^{0}-r_{0}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|)\cdot{\nabla}W_{j}^{0}

\displaystyle-{2\over|\log\varepsilon|}(\Psi^{0}-r_{0}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|)\,{\bf e_{2}}\cdot{\nabla}W_{j}^{0}\Biggl].

This fact, together with (
6.8
) and the estimates on
\tilde{\phi}^{6}
,
\tilde{\psi}^{6}
give that
\tilde{E}_{0}(x,t)
has no singularity at
r=0
and satisfies

(6.36)

\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{0}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{k}(1-\eta_{j1}){O(\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|)\over(|\log\varepsilon|^{-3}+|x-P_{j}|)^{4}},

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\tilde{E}_{0}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{k}(1-\eta_{j1}){O(\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|)\over(|\log\varepsilon|^{-3}+|x-P_{j}|)^{5}}.

We define
\phi^{out}_{1}
to solve (
6.27
). Since (
6.36
), from Lemma
6.3
we obtain that

(1+|x|)|{\nabla}_{x}\phi^{out}_{1}(x,t)|+|{\partial}_{t}\phi^{out}_{1}(x,t)|+|\phi^{out}_{1}(x,t)|\ \leq\ C\,{\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+|x|^{4}}

where
b>0
denotes a number whose exact value will change from line to line and also within the same line. At the end of this construction, we will absorb any positive power of
|\log\varepsilon|
in an arbitrarily smaller power of
\varepsilon
.

We next introduce
\psi_{1}^{out}
to solve

\displaystyle-\Delta_{5}\psi^{out}_{1}

\displaystyle=\phi^{out}_{1}+S_{2}[\tilde{\phi}^{6},\tilde{\psi}^{6},0,0,P],\quad{\mbox{on}}\quad\Sigma\times[0,T),\quad

\displaystyle{\partial\psi^{out}_{1}\over\partial r}

\displaystyle=0\quad{\mbox{on}}\quad\partial\Sigma\times[0,T),\quad|\psi^{out}_{1}(x,t)|\to 0\quad\text{as }|x|\to\infty.

From the estimates on
\tilde{\phi}^{6}
and
\tilde{\psi}^{6}
we get

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle[\tilde{\phi}^{6},\tilde{\psi}^{6},0,0,P]=\sum_{j=1}^{k}(\eta_{j1}-\eta_{j2}){\Delta_{5,j}\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{6}\over r_{j}\varepsilon_{j}^{2}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{k}({\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{6}\over r_{j}}\Delta_{5}\eta_{j2}+2\nabla_{x}\eta_{j2}\nabla_{x}{\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{6}\over r_{j}})=O(\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}){\bf 1}_{\{|\log\varepsilon|^{-3}<|x-P_{j}|<4|\log\varepsilon|^{-3}\}}.

With the aid of Lemma
4.1
, we have

(6.37)

(1+|x|)|\nabla\psi_{1}^{out}(x,t)|+|\psi_{1}^{out}(x,t)|\lesssim{\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+|x|^{2}},

and hence

(6.38)

S_{2}[\tilde{\phi}^{6},\tilde{\psi}^{6},\phi_{1}^{out},\psi_{1}^{out},P]=0\quad(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T).

Having introduced the outer corrections
\phi_{1}^{out}
and
\psi_{1}^{out}
, we compute the new outer error
E^{out}[\phi^{out}_{1},\psi^{out}_{1},\tilde{\phi}^{6},\tilde{\psi}^{6},P]
and the new inner error
E_{j}^{in}[\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{6},\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{6},\psi_{1}^{out},P]
.

The new outer error is given by

\displaystyle E^{out}

\displaystyle[\phi^{out}_{1},\psi^{out}_{1},\tilde{\phi}^{6},\tilde{\psi}^{6},P](x,t):=\nabla_{x}^{\perp}(r^{2}(\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\eta_{j2}\over r_{j}}\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{6}+\psi^{out}_{1}))\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi^{out}_{1}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\nabla_{x}^{\perp}(r^{2}\psi^{out}_{1})\nabla\bar{\eta}_{1j}{\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{6}\over\varepsilon_{j}^{2}r_{j}}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}(\eta_{2j}-\eta_{1j})\nabla_{x}^{\perp}(r^{2}\psi^{out}_{1})\cdot\nabla_{x}W^{0}

\displaystyle+(1-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\eta_{2j})\nabla^{\perp}(r^{2}\psi^{out}_{1})\cdot\nabla W^{0}\quad(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T)

We see that

E^{out}[\phi^{out}_{1},\psi^{out}_{1},\tilde{\phi}^{6},\tilde{\psi}^{6},P](x,t)
is a regular function, which is
\equiv 0
in the region

\Big\{(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T]\ /\ x\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}B(P_{j}(t),\bar{\delta}|\log\varepsilon|^{-3})\Big\}

for some
\bar{\delta}>0
, and satisfies the bounds

(6.39)

|E^{out}[\phi^{out}_{1},\psi^{out}_{1},\tilde{\phi}^{6},\tilde{\psi}^{6},P](x,t)|\lesssim{\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+|x|^{4}}.

The new inner error is given by

\displaystyle E_{j}^{in}

\displaystyle[\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{6},\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{6},\psi_{1}^{out},P](y,t):=\nabla^{\perp}((1+{\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}\over r_{1}})^{2}r_{j}\psi^{out}_{1})\cdot\nabla(w^{0}_{j}+\sum_{i\not=j}w_{i}^{0})

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\perp}\left((1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})^{2}r_{j}\psi^{out}_{1}\right)\cdot\nabla\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{6}+E_{j}^{in}[\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{6},\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{6},0,P](y,t).

The biggest term in this expression comes from
\nabla^{\perp}((1+{\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}\over r_{1}})^{2}r_{j}\psi^{out}_{1})\cdot\nabla(w^{0}_{j}+\sum_{i\not=j}w_{i}^{0})
. Using (
6.37
) we see that the size of this term is
{\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+|y|^{5}}
. Moreover, since
w_{j}^{0}
satisfies (
4.13
), its Fourier modes-
0
come at order
\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}
or smaller. In combination with (
6.7
), we get

\displaystyle E_{j}^{in}

\displaystyle[\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{6},\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{6},\psi_{1}^{out},P](y,t):=[\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;P)-\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;\bar{P}^{2})]\cdot\nabla U

(6.40)

\displaystyle+{\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+|y|^{5}}E_{i\geq 1}+{\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+|y|^{4}}E_{0}+{\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+|y|^{3}}\,E_{i\geq 0}.

Recall that
b>0
denotes a number whose exact value will change from line to line and also within the sale line.

6.9.

Seventh inner improvement.
Our next step is to eliminate the terms in the error (
6.8
) given by

{\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{13}\over 1+|y|^{5}}E_{i\geq 1}
. We proceed as in the second and fourth improvements.
Take
\ell=3
in the decomposition (
6.16
) of
P^{1}(t)
and
for
\bar{P}^{3}=(\bar{P}_{1}^{3},\ldots,\bar{P}_{k}^{3})
,
\bar{P}^{3}_{j}=P_{0}+P_{j}^{0}+P_{j}^{11}+P_{j}^{12}+P_{j}^{13}
, we take

e_{7}(y,t):=\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;\bar{P}^{3})\cdot\nabla U+{\varepsilon_{j}^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+|y|^{5}}\,E_{i\geq 1}[\bar{P}^{2},\dot{P}^{0}+\dot{P}^{11}+\dot{P}^{12}].

We have
\int_{0}^{2\pi}e_{7}(\rho e^{i\theta},t)\,d\theta=0
, and
e_{7}
satisfies the decay (
6.13
) with
m=5
. In fact, under the assumptions (
4.2
) on
P
, we have

(1+|y|^{5})(|e_{7}(y,t)|+|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}|{\partial}_{t}e_{7}(y,t)|)\lesssim\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{b},\quad|y|<3R_{j}.

The orthogonality conditions

\int_{B_{8R_{j}}}(1+|y|^{2})e_{7}(y,y)Z_{\ell}(y)\,dy=0,\quad\ell=1,2

become a system of ODEs for the point
P^{13}=(P_{1}^{13},\ldots,P_{k}^{13})
of the same form as (
6.17
).
Standard ODEs theory gives that, for all
\varepsilon>0
small enough there exists a unique solution
P^{13}
to (
6.17
) with initial condition
P^{13}(0)=0
, which satisfy the bounds

\|P^{13}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}[0,T)}+\|\dot{P}^{13}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}[0,T)}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{c},

for some
c>0
.
We denote by
\psi_{j}^{7}=\psi_{j}^{7}(y,t)
and
\phi_{j}^{7}(y,t)=-\Delta_{5,j}\psi_{j}^{7}
the solution to (
6.10
), with
g=e_{7}
,
whose existence and estimates are given by Lemma
6.1
. We have that
\int_{0}^{2\pi}\psi_{j}^{7}(\rho e^{i\theta},t)\,d\theta=0
and

|\psi^{7}_{j}(y,t)|\,+\,(1+|y|)\,|{\nabla}\psi_{j}^{7}(y,t)|\,\lesssim\,{\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+|y|},\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}B(0,3R_{j})\times[0,T].

We also have

\,(1+|y|^{4})\,|\nabla_{y}\phi_{j}^{7}(y,t)|+\,(1+|y|^{3})\,|\phi_{j}^{7}(y,t)|\lesssim\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}B(0,3R_{j})\times[0,T].

We can differentiate in time the equation, to also get

\,(1+|y|^{3})\,|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}\,|{\partial}_{t}\phi_{j}^{7}(y,t)|\lesssim\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}B(0,3R_{j})\times[0,T].

Define

\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{7}=\sum_{i=1}^{7}\phi_{j}^{i},\quad\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{7}=\sum_{i=1}^{7}\psi_{j}^{i}.

Arguing as in the second improvement we get

\displaystyle E_{j}^{in}
