\displaystyle[\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{7},\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{7},\psi_{1}^{out},P](y,t):=[\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;P)-\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;\bar{P}^{3})]\cdot\nabla U

(6.41)

\displaystyle+{\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+|y|^{4}}\,E_{i\geq 0}+{\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{28}\over 1+|y|^{3}}\,E_{i\geq 0}[P,\dot{P}].

6.10.

Eighth inner improvement.
Our next step is the elimination of the Fourier mode-
0
term of size
\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{26}
in formula (
6.9
). We proceed as in the third inner improvement and define
\phi_{j}^{8}
as follows

\phi_{j}^{8}(y,t)=-{\varepsilon^{4}\,|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+|y|^{4}}\,\int_{0}^{t}\varepsilon_{j}^{-2}(s)E_{0}(\rho,\theta,s,\varepsilon)\,ds.

It solves

\displaystyle|\log\varepsilon|\varepsilon_{j}^{2}{\partial}_{t}\phi_{j}^{8}

\displaystyle+{\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+|y|^{4}}\,E_{0}=0\quad(y,t)\in B(0,3R_{j})\times[0,T)

\displaystyle\phi_{j}^{8}(y,0)

\displaystyle=0{\quad\hbox{in }}B(0,3R_{j}),

it satisfies

(1+|y|)|\nabla_{y}\phi_{j}^{8}(y,t)|+|\phi_{j}^{8}(y,t)|\lesssim{\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+|y|^{4}}\,,\quad

and its Fourier decomposition only has mode
0
.
Let
\psi_{j}^{8}
be the solution to

-\Delta_{5,j}\psi_{j}^{8}=\phi_{j}^{8}\quad(y,t)\in B_{4R_{j}}\times[0,T),\quad\psi_{j}^{8}=0\quad(y,t)\in\partial B_{4R_{j}}\times[0,T).

Thanks to the space decay in
\phi_{j}^{8}
we get

|\psi_{j}^{8}(y,t)|+|(1+|y|)\nabla\psi_{j}^{8}(y,t)|\lesssim{\varepsilon^{2}\,|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+|y|^{2}}.

As in the third inner improvement we get, for

\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{8}=\sum_{i=1}^{8}\phi_{j}^{i},\quad\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{8}=\sum_{i=1}^{8}\psi_{j}^{i},

\displaystyle E_{j}^{in}

\displaystyle[\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{8},\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{8},\psi_{1}^{out},P](y,t):=[\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;P)-\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;\bar{P}^{3})]\cdot\nabla U

(6.42)

\displaystyle+{\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+|y|^{5}}\,E_{1,1}\sin\theta+{\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+|y|^{4}}\,E_{2}[P,\dot{P}]+{\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+|y|^{3}}\,E_{i\geq 0}[P,\dot{P}].

In comparison with (
6.9
), we loose one power of
\varepsilon
and produce a new error in mode
1
, in the form of
\sin\theta
. We have already seen how to deal with such a situation in the fourth inner improvement and in this way we proceed next.

6.11.

Ninth inner improvement.
We eliminate the terms in the error (
6.10
)

{\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+|y|^{5}}\,E_{1,1}\sin\theta
.
Take
\ell=4
in the decomposition (
6.16
) of
P^{1}(t)
and
for
\bar{P}^{4}=(\bar{P}_{1}^{4},\ldots,\bar{P}_{k}^{4})
,
\bar{P}^{4}_{j}=P_{0}+P_{j}^{0}+P_{j}^{11}+P_{j}^{12}+P_{j}^{13}+P^{14}_{j}
, we take

e_{9}(y,t):=\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;\bar{P}^{4})\cdot\nabla U+{\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+|y|^{5}}\,E_{1,1}[\bar{P}^{4},\dot{P}^{4}-\dot{P}^{14}]\sin\theta.

We have
\int_{0}^{2\pi}e_{9}(\rho e^{i\theta},t)\,d\theta=0
, and
e_{9}
satisfies the decay (
6.13
) with
m=5
. In fact, under the assumptions (
4.2
) on
P
, we have

(1+|y|^{5})(|e_{9}(y,t)|+|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}|{\partial}_{t}e_{9}(y,t)|)\lesssim\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{b},\quad|y|<3R_{j}.

The orthogonality conditions

\int_{B_{8R_{j}}}(1+|y|^{2})e_{8}(y,y)Z_{\ell}(y)\,dy=0,\quad\ell=1,2

become a system of ODEs for the point
P^{14}
of the same form as (
6.17
).
Standard ODEs theory gives that, for all
\varepsilon>0
small enough there exists a unique solution
P^{14}
to (
6.17
) with initial condition
P^{14}(0)=0
, which satisfy the bounds

\|P^{14}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}[0,T)}+\|\dot{P}^{14}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}[0,T)}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{c}

for some
c>0
.
We denote by
\psi_{j}^{9}=\psi_{j}^{9}(y,t)
and
\phi_{j}^{9}(y,t)=-\Delta_{5,j}\psi_{j}^{9}
the solution to (
6.10
), with
g=e_{9}
,
whose existence and estimates are given by Lemma
6.1
. We have that
\int_{0}^{2\pi}\psi_{j}^{9}(\rho e^{i\theta},t)\,d\theta=0
and

|\psi^{9}_{j}(y,t)|\,+\,(1+|y|)\,|{\nabla}\psi_{j}^{9}(y,t)|\,\lesssim{\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+|y|},\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}B(0,3R_{j})\times[0,T].

We also have

\,(1+|y|^{4})\,|\nabla_{y}\phi_{j}^{9}(y,t)|+\,(1+|y|^{3})\,|\phi_{j}^{9}(y,t)|\lesssim\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}B(0,3R_{j})\times[0,T].

We can differentiate in time the equation, to also get

\,(1+|y|^{3})\,|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}\,|{\partial}_{t}\phi_{j}^{9}(y,t)|\lesssim\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{27}\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}B(0,3R_{j})\times[0,T].

Arguing as in the fourth improvement we get, for

\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{9}=\sum_{i=1}^{9}\phi_{j}^{i},\quad\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{9}=\sum_{i=1}^{9}\psi_{j}^{i}.

We get the
new error

\displaystyle E_{j}^{in}

\displaystyle[\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{9},\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{9},\psi_{1}^{out},P](y,t):=[\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;P)-\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;\bar{P}^{4})]\cdot\nabla U

\displaystyle+{\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{50}\over 1+|y|^{4}}\,E_{i\geq 2}+{\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{52}\over 1+|y|^{3}}\,E_{i\geq 0}[P,\dot{P}].

6.12.

Tenth inner improvement.
Our last step consists in removing the term
{\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+|y|^{4}}\,E_{i\geq 2}
and we argue exactly as in the first inner improvement, but with two more power of
\varepsilon
. We find
\psi_{j}^{10}
,
\phi_{j}^{10}
satisfying

|\psi^{10}_{j}(y,t)|\,+\,(1+|y|)\,|{\nabla}\psi_{j}^{10}(y,t)|\,\lesssim\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{b},\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}B(0,8R_{j})\times[0,T].

and

\,(1+|y|^{3})\,|\nabla_{y}\phi_{j}^{10}(y,t)|+\,(1+|y|^{2})\,|\phi_{j}^{10}(y,t)|\lesssim\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}B(0,8R_{j})\times[0,T],

such that, for

\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{10}=\sum_{i=1}^{10}\phi_{j}^{i},\quad\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{10}=\sum_{i=1}^{10}\psi_{j}^{i}.

we get

\displaystyle E_{j}^{in}

\displaystyle[\phi_{j}^{10},\psi_{j}^{10},\psi_{1}^{out},P](y,t):=[\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;P)-\nabla^{\perp}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;\bar{P}^{4})]\cdot\nabla U+{\varepsilon^{5}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+|y|^{3}}\,E_{i\geq 0}[P,\dot{P}]

as
\varepsilon\to 0
, where
the functions
E_{j}(\rho,\theta,t,\varepsilon)
have the form described in (
4.32
). Using the definition of
{\mathcal{R}}^{00}_{j}
in (
6.7
), we check that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;P)

\displaystyle-{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{00}(y,t;\bar{P}^{4})=\varepsilon_{j}|\log\varepsilon|\dot{\bf a}_{j}\cdot y

\displaystyle+\varepsilon_{j}[{\nabla}_{x}\varphi_{j}(P_{j};P)-{\nabla}_{x}\varphi_{j}(\bar{P}_{j}^{4};\bar{P}^{4})]\cdot y

\displaystyle+\,\varepsilon_{j}^{3}D_{x}\theta_{j\varepsilon_{j}}(P_{j};P)[y]-\varepsilon_{j}^{3}D_{x}\theta_{j\varepsilon_{j}}(\bar{P}^{4}_{j};\bar{P}^{4})[y].

We take

\phi_{j}^{*}=\bar{\phi}_{j}^{10},\quad\psi_{j}^{*}=\bar{\psi}_{j}^{10},\quad\psi^{*,out}=\psi_{1}^{out},\quad\phi^{*,out}=\phi_{1}^{out}.

Recall from (
6.38
) that

S_{2}[\tilde{\phi}^{6},\tilde{\psi}^{6},\phi_{1}^{out},\psi_{1}^{out},P]=0\quad(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T).

Hence

|S_{2}[\phi^{*,in},\psi^{*,in},\phi^{*,out},\psi^{*,out},P]|\ \leq\ C\varepsilon^{4-\sigma}\eta_{1}\left({4|x-(r_{0},0)|\over r_{0}}\right).

Besides, from (
6.39
) we get

\left|E^{out}[\phi^{*,out},\psi^{*,out},\phi^{*,in},\psi^{*,in},{\bf a}](x,t)\right|\lesssim{\varepsilon^{4}|\log\varepsilon|^{b}\over 1+|x|^{4}},\quad(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T].

This concludes the proof of Proposition
5.1
.

7.
The inner modified transport equation

This section is devoted to discuss the result of Lemma
6.2
, on the solution of the Cauchy Problem (
6.25
). We write it as

(7.1)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|{\partial}_{t}\phi&+{\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0}(y)+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\tilde{{\mathcal{R}}}_{j}(y,t))+\eta_{4\varepsilon}{\mathcal{B}}_{j}(y,t)\over(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}\eta_{4\varepsilon}y_{1})}\cdot\nabla_{y}\phi={\eta_{4\varepsilon}E(y,t)\over(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}\eta_{4\varepsilon}y_{1})},\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T],\\
\phi(y,0)&=0,\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}.\end{aligned}

We refer to (
6.24
) for the definition of
\eta_{4\varepsilon}
. Problem (
7.1
) is a transport equation. It is convenient to introduce the change of variable in time and let

t=t(\tau)
,

t=|\log\varepsilon|\int_{0}^{\tau}\varepsilon_{j}^{2}(s)\,ds,\quad T=|\log\varepsilon|\int_{0}^{\tau_{T}}\varepsilon_{j}^{2}(s)\,ds.

Problem (
7.1
) gets transformed into a problem for
\phi=\phi(y,t(\tau))=\phi(y,\tau)
as

(7.2)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \partial_{\tau}\phi&+{\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0}(y)+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\tilde{{\mathcal{R}}}_{j}(y,t))+\eta_{4\varepsilon}{\mathcal{B}}_{j}(y,t)\over(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}\eta_{4\varepsilon}y_{1})}\cdot\nabla_{y}\phi={\eta_{4\varepsilon}E(y,t)\over(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}\eta_{4\varepsilon}y_{1})},\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\tau_{T}],\\
\phi(y,0)&=0,\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\end{aligned}

We solve (
7.2
) by the method of characteristics.

The characteristic curve
\bar{y}(s;\tau,y)
is by definition the solution

\bar{y}(s)
of the ODE system

(7.3)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{d\bar{y}}{ds}(s)&={\mathcal{B}}(\bar{y}(s),t(s))\\
\bar{y}(\tau)&=y.\end{aligned}\right.

where

{\mathcal{B}}(y,t(\tau))={\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0}(y)+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\tilde{{\mathcal{R}}}_{j}(y,t))+\eta_{4\varepsilon}{\mathcal{B}}_{j}(y,t)\over(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}\eta_{4\varepsilon}y_{1})}.

Observe that
(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}\eta_{4\varepsilon}y_{1})=1+O(|\log\varepsilon|^{-3})
, uniformly in
\varepsilon
and that
{\mathcal{B}}
is
log-Lipschitz in
y
uniformly in
t
, that is, for some
L>0
,

|{\mathcal{B}}(y_{1},t)-{\mathcal{B}}(y_{2},t)|\leq L|y_{1}-y_{2}|(1+|\log|y_{1}-y_{2}|\,|)

and continuous in its two variables. Hence system (
7.3
) has a unique solution.
For a locally bounded function
E
, the unique solution of (
7.2
) is then represented by the formula

(7.4)

\displaystyle\phi(y,\tau)=\int_{0}^{\tau}{\mathcal{E}}(\bar{y}(s;\tau,y),t(s))\,ds,\quad{\mathcal{E}}={\eta_{4\varepsilon}E(y,t)\over(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}\eta_{4\varepsilon}y_{1})}.

Under the assumptions in Lemma
6.2
, we get that

(7.5)

(1+|y|^{2})|D_{y}^{2}\mathcal{E}(y,t)|+(1+|y|)|{\nabla}_{y}\mathcal{{\partial}}_{t}\mathcal{E}(y,t)|+(1+|y|)|{\nabla}_{y}\mathcal{E}(y,t)|+|\mathcal{E}(y,t)|+|{\partial}_{t}\mathcal{E}(y,t)|\ \leq\ C\,(1+|y|)^{\alpha}.

Our first result is

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
1\leq p\leq+\infty
and
m\in\mathbb{R}
. There exists a number
C>0
such that
for any function
E(y,t)
that satisfies

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|(1+|\cdot|)^{-m}E(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\ <\ +\infty

we have that for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon
, the solution of
(\ref{pico1-new})
satisfies

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|(1+|\cdot|)^{-m}\phi(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\ \leq\ C\varepsilon^{-2}|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|(1+|\cdot|)^{-m}E(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Proof.

Let

H(y,s)=\Gamma_{0}(y)+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\tilde{{\mathcal{R}}}_{j}(y,t(s)).

We use the explicit expression for
{{\mathcal{R}}}_{j}(y,t(s))
to get that along the characteristic curves defined in (
7.3
) one has

\displaystyle{d\over ds}

\displaystyle H(\bar{y}(s),t(s))=\nabla_{y}(\Gamma_{0}(\bar{y})+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\tilde{{\mathcal{R}}}_{j}(\bar{y},t(s)))\cdot\frac{d\bar{y}}{ds}(s)+{\partial}_{s}(\eta_{4\varepsilon}\tilde{{\mathcal{R}}}_{j}(\bar{y},t(s)))

\displaystyle=\nabla_{y}(\Gamma_{0}(\bar{y})+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\tilde{{\mathcal{R}}}_{j}(\bar{y},t(s)))\cdot{\eta_{4\varepsilon}{\mathcal{B}}_{j}(\bar{y},t(s))\over(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}\eta_{4\varepsilon}y_{1})}+{\partial}_{s}(\eta_{4\varepsilon}\tilde{{\mathcal{R}}}_{j}(\bar{y},t(s))).

Observe that

\displaystyle{\partial}_{s}(\eta_{4\varepsilon}\tilde{{\mathcal{R}}}_{j}(\bar{y},t(s)))

\displaystyle=\eta_{4\varepsilon}{d\over dt}(\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}}){dt\over ds}\,\bar{y}_{1}\,\Big(\Gamma_{0}(\bar{y})+\bar{A}+\Gamma(\bar{y})\Big)+\eta_{4\varepsilon}{\partial}_{t}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1}_{j}(\bar{y},t;P){dt\over ds}

\displaystyle=\eta_{4\varepsilon}{d\over dt}(\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}})\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\,\bar{y}_{1}\,\Big(\Gamma_{0}(\bar{y})+\bar{A}+\Gamma(\bar{y})\Big)+\eta_{4\varepsilon}{\partial}_{t}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1}_{j}(\bar{y},t;P)\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|,

as
{dt\over ds}=\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|
.
We integrate both sides from
\tau
to
s
and get

\displaystyle H(\bar{y}(s),t(s))

\displaystyle-H(y,t(\tau))=\int_{\tau}^{s}\nabla_{y}(\Gamma_{0}(\bar{y}(\xi))+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\tilde{{\mathcal{R}}}_{j}(\bar{y}(\xi),t(\xi)))\cdot{\eta_{4\varepsilon}{\mathcal{B}}_{j}(\bar{y}(\xi),t(\xi))\over(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}\eta_{4\varepsilon}\bar{y}_{1}(\xi))}

\displaystyle+|\log\varepsilon|\int_{\tau}^{s}\eta_{4\varepsilon}{d\over dt}(\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}})\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,\bar{y}_{1}(\xi)\,\Big(\Gamma_{0}(\bar{y}(\xi))+\bar{A}+\Gamma(\bar{y}(\xi))\Big)\,d\xi

\displaystyle+|\log\varepsilon|\int_{\tau}^{s}\eta_{4\varepsilon}{\partial}_{t}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1}_{j}(\bar{y}(\xi),t;P)\varepsilon_{j}^{2}(t(\xi))\,d\xi.

By assumptions (
4.2
) and (
4.7
), we have that
{d\over dt}(\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}})=O(\varepsilon|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}})
. Furthermore,
s\in[0,\tau)
,
\tau\in[0,\varepsilon_{j}^{-2}|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}]
, and using (
6.23
) we get

H(\bar{y}(s),t(s))-H(y,t(\tau))=|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}}g(s)

for
g(s)=O(1)
as
s\in[0,\tau)
, uniformly as
\varepsilon\to 0
, such that
g(\tau)=0
.
This relation is equivalent to

\displaystyle(1+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}}\bar{y}_{1}(s))\Gamma_{0}(\bar{y}(s))+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}}\,\bar{y}_{1}(s)\,\Big(\bar{A}+\Gamma(\bar{y}(s))\Big)+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1}_{j}(\bar{y}(s),t(s);P)

\displaystyle=(1+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}}y_{1})\Gamma_{0}(y)+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}}\,y_{1}\,\Big(\bar{A}+\Gamma(y)\Big)+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1}_{j}(y,t(\tau);P)+O(|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}}).

A closer look at this identity gives

\displaystyle\log(1+|\bar{y}(s)|^{2})=(1+O(\eta_{4\varepsilon}|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}))\log(1+|y|^{2})+O(|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}}),

and hence, taking the exponential on both sides and using the fact that
(\varepsilon|\log\varepsilon|)^{-|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}}=O(1)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
,

\displaystyle(1+|\bar{y}(s)|^{2})

\displaystyle=(1+|y|^{2})\,[2+O({\log|\log\varepsilon|\over|\log\varepsilon|})+O(|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}})]

\displaystyle=(1+|y|^{2})\,[2+O(|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}})].

Therefore there are positive constants
a,b
independent of
\tau\in(0,\tau_{T})
and
\varepsilon
such that

(7.6)

a(1+|y(s)|^{2})\leq 1+|\bar{y}(s)|^{2}\leq b(1+|y(s)|^{2})\quad\forall s\in[0,\tau_{T}).

We recall the representation formula (
7.4
)

\phi(y,\tau)=\int_{0}^{\tau}{\mathcal{E}}(\bar{y}(s;\tau,y),t(s))\,ds,\quad{\mathcal{E}}={\eta_{4\varepsilon}E(y,t)\over(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}\eta_{4\varepsilon}y_{1})}

Using (
7.6
) we readily get

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|(1+|\cdot|)^{-m}\phi(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\ \leq\ C\varepsilon^{-2}|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|(1+|\cdot|)^{-m}E(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

for any
1\leq p\leq+\infty
, as desired.
∎

A consequence of the bounds (
7.6
) on the characteristic curves is a control on the spacial support of
\phi
if the spacial support of the function
E
stays
at a uniform large distance of the origin then so does the solution of (
7.1
).

Lemma 7.2
.

There exist numbers
\varepsilon_{0}>0
,
\beta>0
such that for any sufficiently small
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
and any locally bounded function
E
such that

E(y,t)=0\quad\mbox{for all}\quad(y,t)\in B_{\varepsilon^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|^{-\zeta}}(0)\times[0,T]

we have that the solution of
(\ref{pico1-new})
satisfies

\phi(y,t)=0\quad\mbox{for all}\quad(y,t)\in B_{\beta\varepsilon^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|^{-\zeta}}(0)\times[0,T].

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of estimates (
7.6
) for the characteristics and the representation formula (
7.4
).
∎

We can now prove Lemma
6.2
.

Proof of Lemma
6.2
.

The bound on the function
|\phi(y,t)|
follows directly from the control on the characteristic curves we obtained in (
7.6
) and the representation formula (
7.4
).

To estimate
{\partial}_{y_{\ell}}\phi(y,\tau)
, we formally differentiate (
7.4
) with respect to
y_{\ell}
,
\ell=1,2
and obtain

(7.7)

\displaystyle{\partial}_{y_{\ell}}\phi(y,\tau)=\int_{0}^{\tau}{\nabla}_{\bar{y}}{\mathcal{E}}(\bar{y}(s;\tau,y),t(s))\cdot\bar{y}_{y_{\ell}}(s;\tau,y)\,ds.

Recall that

\displaystyle\left(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}\eta_{4\varepsilon}(\bar{y})\bar{y}_{1}\right)\,\frac{d\bar{y}}{ds}(s)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0}(\bar{y})+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\tilde{{\mathcal{R}}}_{j}(\bar{y},t(s)))+\eta_{4\varepsilon}{\mathcal{B}}_{j}(\bar{y},t),\quad\bar{y}(\tau)=y,

(see (
7.3
)). Under assumptions (
6.23
), the above system has the form

\displaystyle\left(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}\eta_{4\varepsilon}(\bar{y})\bar{y}_{1}\right)\,\frac{d\bar{y}}{ds}(s)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{y}^{\perp}\left(\Gamma_{0}(\bar{y})+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}}\,y_{1}\,(\Gamma_{0}(y)+\bar{A}+\Gamma(y))\right)+\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}\,\eta_{4\varepsilon}\,\mathcal{A}(\bar{y},s),\quad\bar{y}(\tau)=y,

where
\mathcal{A}
satisfies

(7.8)

\left|{\partial}_{s}\mathcal{A}(y,s)\right|+\left|\mathcal{A}(y,s)\right|\leq M|y|,\quad\left|\nabla_{y}\mathcal{A}(y,s)\right|\leq M

for some constant
M
independent of
\varepsilon
. Consider the point
a=(a_{1},a_{2})
, close to
y=0
, such that

\Big(\nabla^{\perp}\left(\Gamma_{0}(y)+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}}\,y_{1}\,(\Gamma_{0}(y)+\bar{A}+\Gamma(y))\right)+\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}\,\eta_{4\varepsilon}\,\mathcal{A}(y,s)\Big)_{y=a}=0.

A direct inspection gives

a_{1}=-{\varepsilon_{j}\over 8r_{j}}(\bar{A}+\Gamma(0))+a_{1\varepsilon}(s),\quad a_{2}=a_{2\varepsilon}(s),\quad{\mbox{with}}\quad|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}|{d\over ds}a_{i\varepsilon}|+|a_{i\varepsilon}|\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2},\quad i=1,2.

Introducing the change of variable
\bar{z}=\bar{y}-a
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}\eta_{4\varepsilon}(\bar{z}_{1}+a_{1})\right)\,\frac{d\bar{z}}{ds}(s)

\displaystyle=B_{a}(\bar{z})+\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}\,\eta_{4\varepsilon}\,\mathcal{A}(\bar{z}+a,s),\quad\bar{z}(\tau)=y-a(\tau),

where
\mathcal{A}
stands for a function satisfying (
7.8
) and

\displaystyle B_{a}(z)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{z}^{\perp}\left(\Gamma_{0}(z+a)+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}}\,(z_{1}+a_{1})\,(\Gamma_{0}(z+a)+\bar{A}+\Gamma(z+a))\right)

\displaystyle={(z+a)^{\perp}\over|z+a|}\left(\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}(|z+a|)+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}}\,(z_{1}+a_{1})\,(\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}(|z+a|)+\Gamma^{\prime}(|z+a|))\right)

\displaystyle+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}}\,(\Gamma_{0}(z+a)+\bar{A}+\Gamma(z+a))\,{\bf e}_{2}+\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}\,\eta_{4\varepsilon}\,\mathcal{A}_{1}(z+a,s)

where
\mathcal{A}_{1}
also satisfies (
7.8
).
The vector field
B_{a}(z)
is smooth and the choice of
a
gives

(7.9)

B_{a}(0)+\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}\,\eta_{4\varepsilon}\,\mathcal{A}(a,s)=0,\quad\forall s.

Let us introduce polar coordinates around
a
:

\bar{z}=\bar{y}-a=\bar{\rho}e^{i\bar{\theta}}.

Hence

{d\bar{z}\over ds}=\bar{\rho}_{s}\hat{\rho}+\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}_{s}\hat{\theta},\quad\hat{\rho}=e^{i\bar{\theta}},\quad\hat{\theta}=ie^{i\bar{\theta}}.

We have that

\displaystyle B_{a}(z)\cdot\hat{\rho}

\displaystyle={a^{\perp}\cdot\hat{\rho}\over|z+a|}\left(\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}(|z+a|)+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}}\,(z_{1}+a_{1})\,(\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}(|z+a|)+\Gamma^{\prime}(|z+a|))\right)

\displaystyle+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}}\,(\Gamma_{0}(z+a)+\bar{A}+\Gamma(z+a))\,\sin\theta+\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}\,\eta_{4\varepsilon}\,\mathcal{A}_{1}(z+a,s)\cdot\hat{\rho}\,

and

\displaystyle B_{a}(z)\cdot\hat{\theta}

\displaystyle={z^{\perp}\cdot\hat{\theta}\over|z+a|}\left(\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}(|z+a|)+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}}\,(z_{1}+a_{1})\,(\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}(|z+a|)+\Gamma^{\prime}(|z+a|))\right)

\displaystyle+{a^{\perp}\cdot\hat{\theta}\over|z+a|}\left(\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}(|z+a|)+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}}\,(z_{1}+a_{1})\,(\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}(|z+a|)+\Gamma^{\prime}(|z+a|))\right)

\displaystyle+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}}\,(\Gamma_{0}(z+a)+\bar{A}+\Gamma(z+a))\,\cos\theta+\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}\,\eta_{4\varepsilon}\,\mathcal{A}_{1}(z+a,s)\cdot\hat{\theta}.

Set

\displaystyle B_{a1}(z,s)

\displaystyle={a_{1}\over|z+a|}\left(\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}(|z+a|)+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}}\,(z_{1}+a_{1})\,(\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}(|z+a|)+\Gamma^{\prime}(|z+a|))\right)

\displaystyle+\,\eta_{4\varepsilon}\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}}\,(\Gamma_{0}(z+a)+\bar{A}+\Gamma(z+a)).

In the region we are considering

B_{a1}(0,s)=O(\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}),\quad|B_{a1}(z,s)|=O(\varepsilon|\log\varepsilon|),\quad|{\partial}_{s}B_{a1}(z,s)|=|{\partial}_{t}B_{a1}|\,\varepsilon^{2}\,|\log\varepsilon|=O(\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}}).

With the definition of
B_{a1}
, we can write

\displaystyle B_{a}(z)\cdot\hat{\rho}

\displaystyle=B_{a1}\,\sin\theta+\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}\,\eta_{4\varepsilon}\,\mathcal{A}_{1}(z+a,s)\cdot\hat{\rho}

\displaystyle B_{a}(z)\cdot\hat{\theta}

\displaystyle={\rho\over|z+a|}\left(\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}(|z+a|)+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}}\,(z_{1}+a_{1})\,(\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}(|z+a|)+\Gamma^{\prime}(|z+a|))\right)

\displaystyle+B_{a1}\,\cos\theta+\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}\,\eta_{4\varepsilon}\,\mathcal{A}_{1}(z+a,s)\cdot\hat{\theta}.

Inserting these computations in the ODEs system for
{d\bar{z}\over ds}
we get

(7.10)

\displaystyle\left(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}\eta_{4\varepsilon}(\bar{z}_{1}+a_{1})\right)\,\bar{\rho}_{s}

\displaystyle=B_{a1}\,\sin\bar{\theta}+\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}\,\eta_{4\varepsilon}\,\mathcal{A}(\bar{z}+a,s)\cdot\hat{\rho},

\displaystyle\left(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}\eta_{4\varepsilon}(\bar{z}_{1}+a_{1})\right)\,\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}_{s}

\displaystyle={\bar{\rho}\over|\bar{z}+a|}\left(\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}(|\bar{z}+a|)+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{2r_{j}}\,(\bar{z}_{1}+a_{1})\,(\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}(|\bar{z}+a|)+\Gamma^{\prime}(|\bar{z}+a|))\right)

\displaystyle+B_{a1}\,\cos\bar{\theta}+\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}\,\eta_{4\varepsilon}\,\mathcal{A}(\bar{z}+a,s)\cdot\hat{\theta}.

Let us analyse the second equation in (
7.10
). Using the control on the characteristics in the proof of Lemma
7.1
, we have the bound (
7.6
) also for
\bar{z}(s)
. Thanks to (
7.9
), we can divide the second equation in (
7.10
) by
\bar{\rho}
and we write it as

\displaystyle\bar{\theta}_{s}

\displaystyle=-{4\over 1+\bar{\rho}^{2}}\left(1+g_{\varepsilon}(s,\bar{\rho})\right)\,\,+\tilde{B}_{a1}\,\cos\bar{\theta}\,\,+\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}\,\eta_{4\varepsilon}\,\mathcal{A}(z+a,s)\cdot\hat{\theta},

where

(1+|z|)\,|\tilde{B}_{a1}(z,s)|=O(\varepsilon|\log\varepsilon|),\quad(1+|z|)\,|{\partial}_{s}\tilde{B}_{a1}(z,s)|=O(\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|).

Here
|g_{\varepsilon}(s,\bar{\rho})|
is a function such that
|g_{\varepsilon}(s,\bar{\rho})|=O(|\log\varepsilon|^{-\zeta+1})
, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, where
\zeta>2
is the number introduced in the definition of
\eta_{4\varepsilon}
as in (
6.24
). Let
\bar{\theta}_{0}
be

\bar{\theta}_{0}(s)=-\int_{\tau}^{s}{4\over 1+\bar{\rho}^{2}(\eta)}\,d\eta

and let
g

to solve
{\partial}_{s}\left(\bar{\theta}_{0}(1+g)\right)=-{4\over 1+\bar{\rho}^{2}}\left(1+g_{\varepsilon}(s,\bar{\rho})\right)
. This is possible for

\bar{\theta}_{0}g=-\int_{\tau}^{s}{4\over 1+\bar{\rho}^{2}(\eta)}g_{\varepsilon}(\eta,\bar{\rho})d\eta.

Let
\rho=\bar{\rho}(\tau)
. Following the proof of Lemma
7.1
, we have that
\bar{\rho}(s)^{2}=\rho^{2}(1+o(1))
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, we get

(1+g)\bar{\theta}_{0}=-{4(s-\tau)\over 1+\rho^{2}}\,\left(1+O\left(|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\right)\right).

Let
\bar{\theta}_{1}
solve at main order

{\partial}_{s}\bar{\theta}_{1}=\tilde{B}_{a1}\,\cos\left(\bar{\theta}_{0}\right).

An integration by parts gives, for
z=\bar{\theta}_{0}(s)

\displaystyle\bar{\theta}_{1}

\displaystyle=-\int\tilde{B}_{a1}{1+\bar{\rho}^{2}\over 4}\cos z\,dz=\tilde{B}_{a1}{1+\bar{\rho}^{2}\over 4}\sin\left(\bar{\theta}_{0}\right)-\int_{s}^{\tau}{d\over ds}(\tilde{B}_{a1})\,\,{1+\bar{\rho}^{2}\over 4}\sin\left(\bar{\theta}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\tilde{B}_{a1}{1+\bar{\rho}^{2}\over 4}\sin\left(\bar{\theta}_{0}\right)\left(1+O(|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}})\right).

We write
\bar{\theta}=\bar{\theta}_{0}+\bar{\theta}_{1}+\theta_{2}
. We get

\theta_{2}=O(|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}})
and
{\partial}_{s}\theta_{2}=O(\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2})
and
we conclude that

(7.11)

\displaystyle\bar{\theta}

\displaystyle=-{4(s-\tau)\over 1+\bar{\rho}^{2}}\,\left(1+O\left(|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\right)\right)+\tilde{B}_{a1}{1+\bar{\rho}^{2}\over 4}\sin\left(\bar{\theta}_{0}\right)\left(1+O(|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}})\right)+O(|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}})

Inserting this result into the first equation in (
7.10
), we get

(7.12)

\bar{\rho}(s)=\rho+\rho O(\varepsilon\rho\eta_{4\varepsilon})\cos({4(s-\tau)\over 1+\rho^{2}}).

Since
a=a(s)
, we have that

{d\bar{y}\over dy_{\ell}}={d\bar{z}\over dy_{\ell}}=\bar{\rho}_{y_{\ell}}\hat{\rho}+\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}_{y_{\ell}}\hat{\theta},

and using (
7.7
) we write

\displaystyle{\partial}_{y_{\ell}}\phi(y,\tau)=\int_{0}^{\tau}{\nabla}_{\bar{y}}{\mathcal{E}}(\bar{y},t(s))\cdot\left(\bar{\rho}_{y_{\ell}}\hat{\rho}+\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}_{y_{\ell}}\hat{\theta}\right)\,ds.

First we estimate
\int_{0}^{\tau}{\nabla}_{\bar{y}}{\mathcal{E}}(\bar{y},t(s))\cdot\bar{\rho}_{y_{\ell}}\hat{\rho}\,ds
. From (
7.12
) we obtain

\bar{\rho}_{y_{\ell}}=\left(1+O(\varepsilon\rho\eta_{4\varepsilon})\cos{4(s-\tau)\over 1+\rho^{2}}\right)\,+O(\varepsilon\rho\eta_{4\varepsilon})\,{s-\tau\over 1+\rho^{2}}\sin{4(s-\tau)\over 1+\rho^{2}}\,.

Set
g(\bar{y},s):=\nabla_{\bar{y}}{\mathcal{E}}(\bar{y}(s),t(s))\cdot\hat{\rho}
,

(7.13)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau}{\nabla}_{\bar{y}}{\mathcal{E}}(\bar{y},t(s))\cdot\bar{\rho}_{y_{\ell}}\hat{\rho}\,ds

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\tau}g\left(1+O(\varepsilon\rho\eta_{4\varepsilon})\cos{4(s-\tau)\over 1+\rho^{2}}\right)\,\,ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\tau}g\,O(\varepsilon\rho\eta_{4\varepsilon})\,{s-\tau\over 1+\rho^{2}}\sin{4(s-\tau)\over 1+\rho^{2}}\,\,ds=A+B.

From (
7.5
) we readily get

|A|\lesssim\varepsilon^{-2}|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}(1+|y|)^{\alpha-1}.

To estimate
B
, we integrate by parts, we use that
\int x\sin x\,dx=-x\cos x+\sin x+C
and obtain

\displaystyle|B|

\displaystyle\leq O(\varepsilon\rho\eta_{4\varepsilon}))\left(|g(\bar{y}(\tau),t(\tau))|\,\tau\,|\cos{4\tau\over 1+\rho^{2}}|+|g|\,(1+\rho^{2})\,|\sin{4\tau\over 1+\rho^{2}}|\right)

\displaystyle+O(\varepsilon\rho\eta_{4\varepsilon}))\,\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\,\left(C\,(1+|y|^{\alpha-1})\,\int_{0}^{\tau}s\cos{4s\over 1+\rho^{2}}+\sin{4s\over 1+\rho^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim O(\varepsilon\rho\eta_{4\varepsilon}))\,\varepsilon^{-2}|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\,(1+|y|^{\alpha-1}).

These estimates allow us conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau}{\nabla}_{\bar{y}}{\mathcal{E}}(\bar{y},t(s))\cdot\bar{\rho}_{y_{\ell}}\hat{\rho}\,ds\lesssim\varepsilon^{-2}|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}(1+|y|)^{\alpha-1}.

Next we treat the term we estimate
\int_{0}^{\tau}{\nabla}_{\bar{y}}{\mathcal{E}}(\bar{y},t(s))\cdot\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}_{y_{\ell}}\hat{\theta}\,ds
.
To this purpose we observe that

{d\over ds}\mathcal{E}=\nabla_{\bar{y}}\mathcal{E}{d\bar{y}\over ds}+\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\,{d\over dt}\mathcal{E}.

Since
{d\bar{y}\over ds}=\bar{\rho}_{s}\hat{\rho}+\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}_{s}\hat{\theta}
, letting
\gamma={\bar{\theta}_{y_{\ell}}\over\bar{\theta}_{s}}
we get

{\nabla}_{\bar{y}}{\mathcal{E}}(\bar{y},t(s))\cdot\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}_{y_{\ell}}\hat{\theta}=\gamma{d\over ds}\mathcal{E}-\gamma\nabla_{\bar{y}}\mathcal{E}\bar{\rho}_{s}\hat{\rho}-\gamma\,\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\,{d\over dt}\mathcal{E}.

We evaluate separately the following integrals

I=\int_{0}^{\tau}\gamma{d\over ds}\mathcal{E}\,ds,\quad II=\int_{0}^{\tau}\gamma\nabla_{\bar{y}}\mathcal{E}\bar{\rho}_{s}\hat{\rho}\,ds,\quad III=\int_{0}^{\tau}\gamma\,\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\,{d\over dt}\mathcal{E}\,ds.

From (
7.11
) it follows that

\displaystyle{\partial}_{y_{\ell}}\bar{\theta}

\displaystyle={8(s-\tau)\bar{\rho}\bar{\rho}_{y_{\ell}}\over(1+\bar{\rho}^{2})^{2}}\,\left(1+O\left(|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\left(({\partial}_{y_{\ell}}\tilde{B}_{a1}{1+\bar{\rho}^{2}\over 4}+\tilde{B}_{a1}{\bar{\rho}{\partial}_{y_{\ell}}\bar{\rho}\over 2})\sin\bar{\theta}_{0}+\tilde{B}_{a1}{1+\bar{\rho}^{2}\over 4}\cos\left(\bar{\theta}_{0}\right){\partial}_{y_{\ell}}\bar{\theta}_{0}\right)\left(1+O(|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}})\right))

\displaystyle+O(|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}}).

Under the assumptions on
\tilde{B}_{a1}
, we get

\left|({\partial}_{y_{\ell}}\tilde{B}_{a1}{1+\bar{\rho}^{2}\over 4}+\tilde{B}_{a1}{\bar{\rho}{\partial}_{y_{\ell}}\bar{\rho}\over 2})\sin\bar{\theta}_{0}\right|\lesssim\varepsilon|\sin\bar{\theta}_{0}|.

We thus have

\displaystyle{\partial}_{y_{\ell}}\bar{\theta}

\displaystyle={8(s-\tau)\bar{\rho}\bar{\rho}_{y_{\ell}}\over(1+\bar{\rho}^{2})^{2}}\,\left(1+O\left(|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\right)+O(\varepsilon\bar{\rho}\eta_{4\varepsilon})\right)+O(|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}}).

Hence

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle:={\bar{\theta}_{y_{\ell}}\over\bar{\theta}_{s}}={2(s-\tau)\bar{\rho}\bar{\rho}_{y_{\ell}}\over(1+\bar{\rho}^{2})}\,\left(1+O\left(|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\right)+O(\varepsilon\bar{\rho}\eta_{4\varepsilon})\right)+O(|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}}),

and

{d\gamma\over ds}=O\left({\bar{\rho}\bar{\rho}_{y_{\ell}}\over(1+\bar{\rho}^{2})}\right).

Since

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\tau}{d\over ds}(\gamma\mathcal{E})\,ds-\int_{0}^{\tau}\mathcal{E}{d\gamma\over ds}\,ds,

we easily get

|\int_{0}^{\tau}{d\over ds}(\gamma\mathcal{E})\,ds|\lesssim|\mathcal{E}(y,\tau)|\,{\tau\over 1+\rho}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-2}\,|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\,(1+|y|)^{\alpha-1},

and

\left|\int_{0}^{\tau}\mathcal{E}{d\gamma\over ds}\,ds\right|\lesssim\varepsilon^{-2}\,|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\,(1+|y|)^{\alpha-1},

as expected. We now treat integral
II:=\int_{0}^{\tau}\gamma\nabla_{\bar{y}}\mathcal{E}\bar{\rho}_{s}\hat{\rho}\,ds
. From the first equation in (
7.10
) and (
7.11
), we get

\displaystyle II

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\tau}\,B_{a1}\,(\nabla_{\bar{y}}\mathcal{E}\cdot\hat{\rho})\,\bar{\rho}\,\bar{\rho}_{y_{\ell}}{2(s-\tau)\over 1+\rho^{2}}\,\sin{2(s-\tau)\over 1+\rho^{2}}\,(1+o(1))\,ds+O\left(\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}\,\int_{0}^{\tau}\,\gamma(\nabla_{\bar{y}}\mathcal{E}\cdot\hat{\rho})\,\right).

To estimate the first integral in the above formula we proceed as in the estimate of the term
B
in formula (
7.13
). The second integral can be bounded as follows

\left|\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}\,\int_{0}^{\tau}\,\gamma(\nabla_{\bar{y}}\mathcal{E}\cdot\hat{\rho})\right|\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}\,(1+|y|)^{\alpha-1}\,\int_{0}^{\tau}(s-\tau)\,ds\lesssim\varepsilon^{-2}|\log\varepsilon|^{-{3\over 2}}\,(1+|y|)^{\alpha-1}.

We thus get

\left|II\right|\lesssim\varepsilon^{-2}\,|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\,(1+|y|)^{\alpha-1}.

In an analogous way we can treat the last integral
III=\int_{0}^{\tau}\gamma\,\varepsilon^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\,{d\over dt}\mathcal{E}\,ds
and we obtain

\left|III\right|\lesssim\varepsilon^{-2}\,|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\,(1+|y|)^{\alpha-1}.

Collecting all the above estimates, we arrive to the desired bound

\left|{\partial}_{y_{\ell}}\phi(y,\tau)\right|\lesssim\varepsilon^{-2}\,|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\,(1+|y|)^{\alpha-1}.

This concludes the proof of Lemma
6.2
.

8.
The outer modified transport equation

Proof of Lemma
6.3
.

Let
x\in\Sigma
. We shall prove that
\bar{x}(s;t,x)\in\Sigma
, for all
s\in(0,t)
. For the definition of
\bar{x}
we refer to (
6.33
). By contradiction, assume there exists
s_{0}\in(0,t)
such that
\bar{x}(s;t,x)\in\Sigma
, for all
s\in(s_{0},t)
and
\bar{x}(s_{0};t,x)\in\partial\Sigma
. Setting
\bar{x}(s_{0};t,x)=(\bar{r}(s_{0};t,x),\bar{z}(s_{0};t,x))
, it holds

\bar{r}(s_{0};t,x)=0
. Setting
B(x,t)=(B_{1}(x,t),B_{2}(x,t))
, we get from (
6.28
) that

B_{1}(\bar{x}(s_{0};t,x),s)=0
. See (
6.30
). Then the ODEs

{d\over ds}\bar{x}(s;x,t)\cdot{\bf e_{1}}=B_{1}(\bar{x}(s;t,x),s),\quad\bar{x}(s_{0};x,t)\cdot{\bf e_{1}}=0

has the trivial solution
\bar{x}(s;x,t)\cdot{\bf e_{1}}\equiv 0
, which contradicts uniqueness of solutions for ODEs. Formula (
6.32
) is then well-defined.

In order to get estimates on the solution, we write the solution using
Duhamel’s representation formula

\phi^{out}(x,t)=|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{E}_{0}(\bar{x}(s;x,t),s)\,ds

where
\bar{x}(s;x,t)
are the characteristic curves defined as

\,{d\over ds}\bar{x}(s;x,t)=B(\bar{x}(s;x,t),s)\quad s\in(0,t),\quad\bar{x}(t;x,t)=x.

Using the estimates on
B(x,t)
described in (
6.30
) we get

|\phi(x,t)|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}t\|E\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma\times[0,t])}.

We also have that for
1\leq p<+\infty
,

\int_{\Sigma}|E(\bar{x}(s;t,x),s)|^{p}\,dx\leq C\int_{\Sigma}|E(x;t,s)|^{p}\,dx

for some
C>0
,
since the differential of area for the change of variable
x\mapsto\bar{x}(s;t,x)
is a bounded function. From this we readily get

\|\phi(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(\Sigma)}\ \leq|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\ t\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\|E(\cdot,s)\|_{L^{p}(\Sigma)}.

With the change of variables
y=\frac{x-P_{j}(t)}{\varepsilon}
an equation of the type (
7.1
)
is satisfied. The result then follows from
Lemma
7.2
: since
E_{0}
and
\tilde{E}_{0}
are zero in the spacial region
\sum_{j=1}^{k}B(P_{j},2|\log\varepsilon|^{-3})
for all
t\in[0,T)
, there exists
\beta>0
such that the solution
\phi
to (
6.27
) is zero in
\sum_{j=1}^{k}B(P_{j},\beta|\log\varepsilon|^{-3})
, for all
t\in[0,T)
. An alternative way to see this is to estimate the characteristics for points
x\in B(P_{j},\delta|\log\varepsilon|^{-3})
and use (
6.31
).

This fact allows us to say that
\phi
satisfies an equation of the form

|\log\varepsilon|{\partial}_{t}\phi+{\nabla}_{x}^{\perp}H\cdot{\nabla}\phi=E(x,t)\quad\text{in }\Sigma\times[0,T],\quad\phi(x,0)=0\quad\text{in }\Sigma,

where

H(x,t)=|\log\varepsilon|\Big(1-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\eta_{j}(x,t)\Big)B

and

\eta_{j}
is a smooth function with
\eta_{j}(x,t)=1
whenever
|x-P_{j}(t)|<\beta|\log\varepsilon|^{-3}
and
=0
if
|x-P_{j}(t)|>2\beta|\log\varepsilon|^{-3}
.
Using the explicit expression of the coefficient
B
given in (
6.28
)-(
6.31
), we see that

H(x,t)=\Big(1-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\eta_{j}(x,t)\Big)O(|\log\varepsilon|),\quad\text{in }\Sigma\times[0,T],

uniformly as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Hence

|\bar{x}(s;t,x)|=|x|+O(1),\quad\quad\mbox{as}\quad\quad\varepsilon\to 0,

and bounds (
6.35
) readily follow.
∎

8.1.
Uniform continuity

Let us consider an equation of the form

(8.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} |\log\varepsilon|\,r\,{\partial}_{t}\phi+{\nabla}_{x}^{\perp}(r^{2}(\Psi^{0}-\alpha_{0}|\log\varepsilon|+e))\cdot{\nabla}\phi=&E(x,t)\quad\text{in }\Sigma\times[0,T],\\
\phi(x,0)&=0,\quad\text{in }\Sigma.\end{aligned}\right.

Let

|\log\varepsilon|{d\over ds}{\bar{x}}(s;t,x)=\bar{r}\nabla^{\perp}H\left(\bar{x}(s;t,x)\right)+2H\left(\bar{x}(s;t,x)\right){\bf e}_{2},\quad\quad\bar{x}(t;t,x)=x

where

H(x,t):=\Psi^{0}(x,t)-\alpha_{0}|\log\varepsilon|+e(x,t),\quad x=(r,z).

We know that the characteristics
\bar{x}=\bar{x}(s;t,x)
are well defined in
\Sigma\times[0,T]
if
\nabla e
,
r\Delta e\in L^{\infty}(\Sigma\times[0,T])
. Besides the formula

\phi(x,t)=\int_{0}^{t}E(\bar{x}(s;t,x),s)\,ds\

gives the solution of (
8.1
).
We have

Lemma 8.1
.

For all
\varrho>0
there exists a positive number

\delta=\delta(\varrho,\|\nabla e\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma\times[0,T])},\|r\Delta e\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma\times[0,T])},T,\Sigma)

such that
for all
(x_{1},t_{1}),\,(x_{2},t_{2})\in\bar{\Sigma}\times[0,T]
we have

|t_{1}-t_{2}|+|x_{1}-x_{2}|<\delta\implies\left|\bar{x}(s;t_{1},x_{1})-\bar{x}(s;t_{2},x_{2})\right|<\varrho.

If
E(x,t)
is a bounded function that satisfies

\sup_{t\in[0,T],\ |x_{1}-x_{2}|<\mu}|E(x_{1},t)-E(x_{2},t)|\leq\Theta(\mu)

for a certain function
\Theta
with
\Theta(\mu)\to 0
as
\mu\to 0
, then the solution
\phi(x,t)
of (
8.1
) satisfies that
for all
(x_{1},t_{1}),\,(x_{2},t_{2})\in\bar{\Sigma}\times[0,T]
we have

|t_{1}-t_{2}|+|x_{1}-x_{2}|<\delta\implies\left|\phi(x_{1},t_{1})-\phi(x_{2},t_{2})\right|<\varrho.

Proof.

By definition

\displaystyle|\log\varepsilon|\,{d\over ds}{\bar{x}}(s;t_{i},x_{i})

\displaystyle=\bar{r}(s;t_{i},x_{i})\nabla^{\perp}H\left(\bar{x}(s;t_{i},x_{i})\right)+2H\left(\bar{x}(s;t_{i},x_{i})\right){\bf e}_{2},\quad

\displaystyle\bar{x}(t_{i};t_{i},x_{i})

\displaystyle=x_{i}

for
i=1,2
. Let
h(s)=\bar{x}(s;t_{1},x_{1})-\bar{x}(s;t_{2},x_{2})
. Then

|{d\over ds}h(s)|\leq C\,A\,|h(s)|\,|\log(|h(s)|)|,

where
A=\left(\|\nabla e\|_{\infty}+\|r\Delta e\|_{\infty}\right)
.
Setting
\beta(s):=|h(s)|^{2}
, we can assume that
0<\beta(s)<1
. Then we get

\left|{d\over ds}\left(\log\left(\log{1\over\beta(s)}\right)\right)\right|\leq C\,A.

Integrating, we obtain

(8.2)

e^{-C\,A\,T}\,\log{1\over\beta(t_{1})}\leq\log{1\over\beta(s)}\leq e^{C\,A\,T}\log{1\over\beta(t_{1})}.

Observe now that

(8.3)

|h(t_{1})|=|x_{1}-x_{2}|+|x_{2}-\bar{x}(t_{1};t_{2},x_{2})|\leq C\,A\,\left(|x_{1}-x_{2}|+|t_{1}-t_{2}|\right).

Combining (
8.2
) and (
8.3
), we obtain the first statement of the Lemma.

The second statement of the Lemma is a direct consequence of the representation formula

\phi(x,t)=\int_{0}^{t}E(\bar{x}(s;t,x),s)\,ds\

for the solution of (
8.1
).
∎

Let us now consider

(8.4)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{\tau}\phi+{\nabla}_{x}^{\perp}\left(\Gamma_{0}(y)+b(y,t)\right)\cdot{\nabla}\phi=&E(y,t)\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\tau_{T}],\\
\phi(y,0)&=0,\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2},\end{aligned}\right.

with
b(y,t)=0=E(y,t)
for
|y|>R
and

t=|\log\varepsilon|\int_{0}^{\tau}\varepsilon_{j}^{2}(s)\,ds,\quad T=|\log\varepsilon|\int_{0}^{\tau_{T}}\varepsilon_{j}^{2}(s)\,ds.

This problem has the same form as the one in (
7.2
), and under our assumption

\tau_{T}\sim\varepsilon^{-2}|\log\varepsilon|^{-1},\quad R\to\infty\quad\mbox{as}\quad\varepsilon\to 0.

As in the previous problem, the modulus of continuity for the characteristics

\bar{y}=\bar{y}(s;\tau,y)
for (
8.4
) depends only on
\|\Delta_{y}b\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T])}
, and that of the solution
only on a uniform bound for
E
and for its modulus of continuity. Arguing as in the proof of Lemma
8.1
, we can show that

Lemma 8.2
.

Assume that

\sup_{\tau\in[0,\tau_{T}],\ |y_{1}-y_{2}|<\mu}|E(y_{1},\tau)-E(y_{2},\tau)|\leq\Theta(\mu)

for a certain function
\Theta
with
\Theta(\mu)\to 0
as
\mu\to 0
. Then
for each
\varrho>0
there exists a positive number

\delta=\delta(\varrho,\|\Delta b\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\tau_{T}])},\|E\|_{\infty},\Theta,T,\varepsilon)

such
that the solution
\phi(y,\tau)
of (
8.4
) satisfies that
for all
(y_{1},\tau_{1}),\,(y_{2},\tau_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\tau_{T}]
we have

|\tau_{1}-\tau_{2}|+|y_{1}-y_{2}|<\delta\implies\left|\phi(y_{1},\tau_{1})-\phi(y_{2},\tau_{2})\right|<\varrho.

These results will be useful in Section
11
for the final argument in our construction.

9.
The inner-outer gluing procedure

In the previous sections
we proved the existence of points
P_{1}^{1},\ldots,P_{k}^{1}
in (
4.2
) such that, for any choice of points
{\bf a}_{1},\ldots,{\bf a}_{k}
in (
4.2
) satisfying (
4.5
), there exists a good approximate leapfrogging of vortex rings
(W^{*},\Psi^{*})
with the form (
5.1
)-(
5.2
), as described in Proposition
5.1
.

We now set up the inner-outer gluing procedure which will lead us to find an exact solution
(\Psi,W)
to (
4.1
) close to the approximation
(\Psi^{*},W^{*})
.

The solution
(\Psi,W)
will have the form

(9.1)

\displaystyle\Psi

\displaystyle=\Psi^{*}(x,t)+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\bar{\eta}_{j2}{1\over r_{j}}\psi_{j}({x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}},t)+\psi^{out}(x,t)

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=W^{*}(x,t)+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\bar{\eta}_{j1}{1\over r_{j}\varepsilon_{j}^{2}}\phi_{j}({x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}},t)+\phi^{out}(x,t)

where

\bar{\eta}_{jN}(x)=\eta_{N}({|\log\varepsilon|^{5}|x-P_{j}|\over\delta}).

Here
\eta_{N}
is the cut-off function introduced in (
2.2
). This ansatz has the same form as the one used in (
5.1
) and (
5.2
) for the construction of the improved approximate leapfrogging of vortex rings
(W^{*},\Psi^{*})
. We notice though that here we are taking cut-offs slightly shorter than the ones in (
5.3
).

Let
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
be the Euler operators introduced in (
2.3
).
Then the operator
S_{1}
evaluated at
(W,\Psi)
becomes

(9.2)

\displaystyle S_{1}(W,\Psi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\bar{\eta}_{1j}\over\varepsilon_{j}^{4}}E_{j}[\phi_{j},\psi_{j}\,\psi^{out},P]+E^{out}[\phi^{out},\Psi^{out},\phi^{in},\psi^{in},P]

where

\displaystyle\phi^{in}(y,t)=(\phi_{1}(y,t),\ldots,\phi_{k}(y,t)),\quad\psi^{in}(y,t)=(\psi_{1}(y,t),\ldots,\psi_{k}(y,t)).

In (
9.2
)
E_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,k
, and
E^{out}
are defined respectively

\displaystyle E_{j}^{in}

\displaystyle[\phi_{j},\psi_{j}\,\psi^{out},{\bf a}](y,t):=|\log\varepsilon|\varepsilon_{j}^{2}(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}){\partial}_{t}\phi_{j}+|\log\varepsilon|B_{0}(\phi_{j})

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\perp}\left((1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})^{2}(\psi_{j}^{0}-r_{j}\alpha_{j}|\log\varepsilon_{j}|+\psi_{j}^{*}+\psi_{j}+r_{j}\psi^{out})+{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(y,t;P)\right)\cdot\nabla\phi_{j}

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\perp}\left((1+{\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}\over r_{j}})^{2}(\psi_{j}+r_{j}\psi^{out})\right)\nabla(\varepsilon_{j}^{2}r_{j}W^{*})

(9.3)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon_{j}^{4}S_{1}(W^{*},\Psi^{*})(\varepsilon_{j}y+P_{j}),\quad|y|<3R,\quad R:={1\over\varepsilon|\log\varepsilon|^{5}}

with
y={x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}}
, where
B_{0}
is defined in (
4.21
), and

(9.4)

\displaystyle E^{out}

\displaystyle[\phi^{out},\Psi^{out},\phi^{in},\psi^{in},{\bf a}](x,t):=|\log\varepsilon|\,r\,\partial_{t}\phi^{out}

\displaystyle+\nabla_{x}^{\perp}(r^{2}(\Psi^{*}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\bar{\eta}_{j2}\over r_{j}}\psi_{j}({x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}})+\psi^{out}-r_{0}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|))\nabla_{x}\phi^{out}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\left[r\,|\log\varepsilon|\,{\partial}_{t}\bar{\eta}_{j1}+\nabla_{x}^{\perp}(r^{2}(\Psi^{*}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\bar{\eta}_{j2}\over r_{j}}\psi_{j}({x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}})+\psi^{out}-r_{0}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|))\nabla\bar{\eta}_{1j}\right]{\phi_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}^{2}r_{j}}

\displaystyle+\left[\sum_{j=1}^{k}(\bar{\eta}_{2j}-\bar{\eta}_{1j})\nabla_{x}^{\perp}(r^{2}({\psi_{j}\over r_{j}}+\psi^{out}))+{r^{2}\psi_{j}\over r_{j}}\nabla_{x}^{\perp}\bar{\eta}_{2j}\right]\nabla_{x}W^{*}

\displaystyle+(1-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\bar{\eta}_{2j})\nabla^{\perp}(r^{2}\psi^{out})\cdot\nabla W^{*}+(1-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\bar{\eta}_{j1})S_{1}(W^{*},\Psi^{*})=0\quad(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T)

It is straightforward to check that a pair
(W,\Psi)
of the form (
9.1
) is a solution to (
4.1
) if
(\phi^{in},\psi^{in},\phi^{out},\psi^{out})
solve
the
inner-outer gluing
system given by the inner problem

(9.5)

\displaystyle E_{j}^{in}

\displaystyle[\phi_{j},\psi_{j}\,\psi^{out},{\bf a}](y,t)=0,\quad(y,t)\in B(0;3R)\times[0,T)

\displaystyle-\Delta_{5,j}\psi_{j}

\displaystyle=\phi_{j},\quad(y,t)\in B(0;3R)\times[0,T)

for all
j=1,\ldots,k
, coupled with the outer problem

(9.6)

\displaystyle E^{out}

\displaystyle[\phi^{out},\psi^{out},\phi^{in},\psi^{in},{\bf a}](x,t)=0,\quad(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T)

\displaystyle E_{1}^{out}

\displaystyle[\phi^{out},\psi^{out},\phi^{in},\psi^{in},{\bf a}](x,t)=0,\quad(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T)

where

\displaystyle E_{1}^{out}

\displaystyle:=\Delta_{5}\psi^{out}+\phi^{out}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}(\bar{\eta}_{j1}-\bar{\eta}_{j2}){\phi_{j}\over r_{j}\varepsilon_{j}^{2}}

(9.7)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{k}({\psi_{j}\over r_{j}}\Delta_{5}\bar{\eta}_{j2}+2\nabla_{x}\bar{\eta}_{j2}\nabla_{x}{\psi_{j}\over r_{j}}),\quad(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T)

with boundary and decay conditions on
\psi^{out}

(9.8)

{{\partial}\over{\partial}r}\psi^{out}(x,t)=0,\quad(x,t)\in{\partial}\Sigma\times[0,T],\quad|\psi^{out}(x,t)|\to 0,\quad\quad\mbox{as}\quad|x|\to\infty.

The rest of the paper is devoted to solve the inner-outer gluing system (
9.5
)-(
9.6
)-(
9.8
).

9.1.
Setting the inner problems in the whole
\mathbb{R}^{2}

The inner problems in (
9.5
) can be extended to the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with the use of cut-off functions. We will use two types of cut-off functions: one with large support much beyond the region
B(0,3R)
, the other with support bigger that
B(0,3R)
but of comparable size.

Using the notation introduce in (
2.2
), we define

(9.9)

\chi(y_{1})=\eta_{10}\left({|y_{1}|\over\varepsilon\,|\log\varepsilon|^{3}}\right),\quad\eta_{4\varepsilon}(y)=\eta_{4}\left({|y|\over R}\right),\quad{\mbox{where}}\quad R:={1\over\varepsilon|\log\varepsilon|^{5}}.

We use
\chi
to extend the operator
\Delta_{5,j}
to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The functions
\phi_{j}
and
\psi_{j}
are related via the operator
\Delta_{5,j}
as described in (
9.5
). This operator can be written as

-\mathop{\rm div}\left((1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})^{3}\nabla\psi_{j}\right)=(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})^{3}\,\phi_{j},\quad(y,t)\in B(0,3R)\times[0,T).

Letting
\mathcal{P}(y_{1},t)
be defined as

(9.10)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}(y_{1},t)

\displaystyle=p(y_{1},t)^{3},\quad\quad{\mbox{where}}\quad p(y_{1},t)=\left(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\chi\right),

it is clear that, if
(\psi_{j},\phi_{j})
are such that

-\mathop{\rm div}\left(\mathcal{P}\nabla\psi_{j}\right)=\mathcal{P}\,\phi_{j},\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}\quad\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]

then their restriction to
B(0,3R)
satisfy the second relation in (
9.5
).

Let us introduce the functions

(9.11)

\displaystyle\psi_{0j}(y,t)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{0}(y)+\frac{\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}}{2r_{j}}\,\chi\,\Big(-3\Gamma_{0}(y)+A_{\varepsilon}-4K(P_{j},P_{j})+\Gamma(y)\Big)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{0j}(y,t)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{0}(y)+\frac{\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}}{2r_{j}}\,\chi\,\Big(\Gamma_{0}(y)+\bar{A}+\Gamma(y)\Big).

For
y\in B(0,3R)
(and beyond) these functions coincide with part of the expansion of
\psi_{j}^{0}+\psi_{j}^{*}
and
(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})^{2}(\psi_{j}^{0}-r_{j}\alpha_{j}|\log\varepsilon_{j}|+\psi_{j}^{*})
respectively, as given in (
5.9
):

(9.12)

\displaystyle\psi_{j}^{0}+\psi_{j}^{*}

\displaystyle=\psi_{0j}-4\log\varepsilon_{j}-\log 8+K(P_{j};P_{j})+c_{j}^{*}(y,t)+c_{j}^{**}(y,t)

\displaystyle(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})^{2}(\psi_{j}^{0}-r_{j}\alpha_{j}|\log\varepsilon_{j}|+\psi_{j}^{*})

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{0j}(y)-(4-\alpha_{j}r_{j})\log\varepsilon_{j}-\log 8+K(P_{j};P_{j})

\displaystyle+b_{j}^{*}(y,t)+b_{j}^{**}(y,t)

where

(9.13)

\displaystyle|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}

\displaystyle|{\partial}_{t}c_{j}^{*}(y,t)|+(1+|y|)|\,{\nabla}_{y}c_{j}^{*}(y,t)|+|c_{j}^{*}(y,t)|\ \leq\ C\varepsilon^{2}\,(1+|y|^{2})\,\log(1+|y|)

\displaystyle(1+|y|)|\,{\nabla}_{y}c_{j}^{**}(y,t)|+|c_{j}^{**}(y,t)|\ \leq\ C\varepsilon^{2+\bar{\sigma}}\,(1+|y|^{2})\,

for
y={x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}}
,
|y|<|\log\varepsilon|^{-3}
, for some fixed
\bar{\sigma}>0
. Similar estimates hold true for
b_{j}^{*}
and
b_{j}^{**}
. We refer to (
5.9
).
Besides, from (
5.8
) we get that

\varepsilon_{j}^{2}r_{j}W^{*}=f\left((1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})^{2}(\psi_{j}^{0}-r_{j}\alpha_{j}|\log\varepsilon_{j}|+\psi_{j}^{*})\right)(1+O(\varepsilon^{2}))\sim f_{0}\left(\Gamma_{0j}(y,t)+b_{j}^{*}+b_{j}^{**}\right),\quad{\mbox{with}}\quad f_{0}(s)=e^{s}.

Let

B_{0}(\phi)=\mathcal{B}_{j}^{0}\cdot\nabla\phi,\quad\mathcal{B}_{j}^{0}:=-\varepsilon_{j}{\partial}_{t}\varepsilon_{j}(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1})y-{\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\over r_{j}}y_{1}{\partial}_{t}P_{j}.

With abuse of notation, we can write

(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\chi)^{2}(\psi_{j}^{0}-r_{j}\alpha_{j}|\log\varepsilon_{j}|+\psi_{j}^{*})+|\log\varepsilon|\,\eta_{4\varepsilon}\,\mathcal{B}_{j}^{0}+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(y,t;P)

to be equal to

\Gamma_{0j}+\eta_{4\varepsilon}\,b_{j}^{*}+\eta_{4\varepsilon}b_{j}^{**}

for some new
b_{j}^{*}
,
b_{j}^{**}
satisfying (
9.13
). Let us also introduce

(9.14)

\displaystyle b_{j}(\psi^{out},{\bf a})

\displaystyle=(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\chi)^{2}\,\eta_{4\varepsilon}\,(\hat{\psi}_{j}+r_{j}\psi^{out})

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}^{2}r_{j}W^{*}

\displaystyle=f_{0}\left(\Gamma_{0j}+b_{j}^{*}\right)+U^{*},\quad f_{0}(s)=e^{s}.

The inner problem (
9
)-(
9.5
) are the restriction to
B(0,3R)
of the following equations

(9.15)

\displaystyle E_{j}[\phi_{j},\psi_{j}\,\psi^{out},{\bf a}](y,t)

\displaystyle:=|\log\varepsilon|\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\chi)\,{\partial}_{t}\phi_{j}

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\perp}\left(\Gamma_{0j}+\eta_{4\varepsilon}b_{j}^{*}+\eta_{4\varepsilon}b_{j}^{**}+b_{j}(\psi^{out},{\bf a})\right)\cdot\nabla\phi_{j}

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\perp}((1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\chi)^{2}\psi_{j})\cdot\nabla\left(f_{0}\left(\Gamma_{0j}+b_{j}^{*}\right)+\eta_{4\varepsilon}U^{*}\right)

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\perp}\left(\eta_{4\varepsilon}(1+{\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}\over r_{1}}\chi)^{2}r_{j}\psi^{out}\right)\nabla\left(f_{0}\left(\Gamma_{0j}+b_{j}^{*}\right)+\eta_{4\varepsilon}U^{*}\right)

\displaystyle+{\mathcal{E}}_{j}[\phi_{j}^{*},\psi_{j}^{*}\,\psi^{*,out},{\bf a}](y,t)\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T],

\displaystyle-\mathop{\rm div}\left(\mathcal{P}\nabla\psi_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}\,\phi_{j},\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}\quad\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]

where
{\mathcal{E}}_{j}[\phi_{j}^{*},\psi_{j}^{*}\,\psi^{*,out},P]
is defined and estimated in Proposition
5.1
.

9.2.
Decomposition of
\phi_{j}
and
\psi_{j}

For a function
h:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
, consider the problem

(9.16)

-\mathop{\rm div}\left({\mathcal{P}}\nabla\psi\right)=h\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}\quad\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T].

A necessary condition for solvability of (
9.16
) is that

(9.17)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h(y,t)\,dy=0,\quad\forall t\in[0,T).

We will make use of the following lemma

Lemma 9.1
.

Assume
h:\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies (
9.17
) and

(9.18)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U^{-1}(y)h^{2}(y,t)\,dy<\infty,\quad\forall t\in[0,T],\quad{\mbox{where}}\quad U(y)={8\over(1+|y|^{2})^{2}}.

Then there exists a unique solution
\psi
to (
9.16
) such that, for all
t\in[0,T]
,
\psi(\cdot,t)\in C^{0,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and

|\psi(y,t)|\lesssim{\|U^{-{1\over 2}}h\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\over 1+|y|^{1-\sigma}}

for some
\sigma\in(0,1)
, and

\|U^{-{1\over 2}+{1\over p}}\nabla\psi\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim\|U^{-{1\over 2}}h\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

for any
p>2
.
If
\|U^{{1\over q}-1}h\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}<\infty
for some
q>2
, then

|\psi(y,t)|+(1+|y|)|\nabla\psi(y,t)|+(1+|y|)^{1+\sigma}[\nabla\psi]_{\sigma}\lesssim{\|U^{{1\over q}-1}h\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\over 1+|y|},

where we denote

[{\nabla}\psi]_{\sigma}(y)=\sup_{y_{1},y_{2}\in B_{1}(y)}\frac{|{\nabla}\psi(y_{1})-{\nabla}\psi(y_{2})|}{|y_{1}-y_{2}|^{\sigma}}

and
\sigma\in(0,1)
.

Proof.

It is convenient to pull equation (
9.16
) into the sphere
S^{2}
by means of the stereographic projection

\pi\,:\,S^{2}-\{(0,0,1)\}:\to\mathbb{R}^{2}

(9.19)

\pi(z)=(\frac{z_{1}}{1-z_{3}},\frac{z_{2}}{1-z_{3}}),\quad z\in S^{2}-\{(0,0,1)\},

whose inverse is given by
\pi^{-1}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to S^{2}-\{(0,0,1)\}

\pi^{-1}(y)=(\frac{2y_{1}}{1+|y|^{2}},\frac{2y_{2}}{1+|y|^{2}},\frac{|y|^{2}-1}{1+|y|^{2}}),\quad y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

For a function
h(y):\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
we denote by
h(z)
the function
h(\pi(z))
defined on
S^{2}
. Then the differential of volume is given by
d\pi(z)={4\over(1+|y|^{2})^{2}}\,dy={U(y)\over 2}\,dy
, where the function
U
is the one in (
2.1
). Thus we get

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h(y)={U^{1\over 2}\over\sqrt{2}}(\nabla_{S^{2}}h)(z),\quad\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h(y)={U\over 2}(\Delta_{S^{2}}h)(z),\quad y=\pi(z).

and

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h(y)\,dy\ =\ 2\int_{S^{2}}h(z)\,U^{-1}(z)\,d\pi(z).

For a vector-valued function
F(y):\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
we have

{\mathop{\rm div}}_{S^{2}}F(z)=\sqrt{2}U^{-1}{\mathop{\rm div}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(U^{1\over 2}F)(y).

Let us denote

\tilde{h}(y,t)=\frac{2\,h(y,t)}{U(y)}

Then Equation (
9.16
) gets transformed into

(9.20)

-{\mathop{\rm div}}_{S^{2}}(\mathcal{P}\nabla_{S^{2}}\psi)=\tilde{h}{\quad\hbox{in }}S^{2}.

From (
9.10
) we get that
\mathcal{P}(y_{1},t)=1+O(\varepsilon R)
, for all
t\in[0,T]
,
y_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
. Assumptions (
9.17
) and (
9.18
) become

\int_{S^{2}}\tilde{h}^{2}(z)\,d\pi(z)\ =\ 2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h^{2}(y)U(y)^{-1}\,dy<\infty,\quad\int_{S^{2}}\tilde{h}(z)\,d\pi(z)\ =\ 0.

The latter condition in the above formula implies the existence of a unique solution of (
9.20
) with mean value zero. This solution is in
H^{2}(S^{2})
, hence it is Hölder continuous of any order. After adding a proper constant, we choose the solution which vanishes at the sounth pole
S=(0,0,1)
. Pulling back this function to a
\psi(y)
defined in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
we see that
it satisfies equation (
9.16
) and it is the only solution that vanishes as
|y|\to\infty
. This and the Hölder condition yields that
\psi
satisfies

(9.21)

|\psi(y)|\ \leq\ \frac{C}{1+|y|^{1-\sigma}}\,\|h\,U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

for an arbitrarily small
\sigma>0
. Moreover, for all
p>2
we have an
L^{p}(S^{2})
-gradient estimate for
\tilde{\psi}(z)
of the form

\|{\nabla}_{S^{2}}\tilde{\psi}\|_{L^{p}(S^{2})}\ \leq\ C\|\tilde{\phi}\|_{L^{2}(S^{2})}

which yields for
\psi
in (
9.16
)

(9.22)

\|U^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{p}}{\nabla}\psi\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\ \leq\ C\|h\,U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

If in addition we have that
\tilde{h}\in L^{q}(S^{2})
for some
q>2
, then a solution of
(
9.20
) satisfies

\|{\nabla}_{S^{2}}\tilde{\psi}\|_{C^{0,\sigma}(S^{2})}\ \leq\ C\|\tilde{h}\|_{L^{q}(S^{2})}.

for some
0<\sigma<1
.
This estimate translates for
\psi
into

|\psi(y)|\,+\,(1+|y|)\,|{\nabla}\psi(y)|+(1+|y|)^{1+\sigma}[{\nabla}\psi]_{\sigma}(y)\ \leq\frac{C}{1+|y|}\|U^{\frac{1}{q}-1}h\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

The proof is completed.
∎

We shall make a decomposition of the functions
\phi_{j}
,
\psi_{j}
introduced in (
9.1
).

Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{20}(y)=\Gamma_{0}(y),\quad\mathcal{Z}_{23}(y)=2\Gamma_{0}(y)+{\nabla}_{y}\Gamma_{0}(y)\cdot y,

where
\Gamma_{0}
is given in (
2.1
).
We write
\phi_{j}(y,t)
in the form

(9.23)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\phi_{j}(y,t)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}\hat{\phi}_{j}(y,t)+\sum_{l=0,3}\beta_{jl}(t)\mathcal{P}\mathcal{Z}_{1l}(y,t),\quad{\mbox{where}}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}_{1\ell}

\displaystyle=-{1\over{\mathcal{P}}}\,\mathop{\rm div}({\mathcal{P}}\nabla\mathcal{Z}_{2\ell}),\quad\ell=0,3,

for some smooth parameter functions
\beta_{j\ell}
that we will determine later on.
A direct inspection gives

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}_{10}

\displaystyle=U(y)-{3\varepsilon_{j}\over(r_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}\chi)}\,(\chi+y_{1}\chi^{\prime})\,{\partial}_{y_{1}}\mathcal{Z}_{20}(y),\quad

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}_{13}

\displaystyle=2U(y)+{\nabla}_{y}U(y)\cdot y-{3\varepsilon_{j}\over(r_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}\chi)}\,(\chi+y_{1}\chi^{\prime})\,{\partial}_{y_{1}}\mathcal{Z}_{23}(y),

where
\chi
is defined in (
9.10
).
On
\hat{\phi}_{j}

we impose the following orthogonality conditions

(9.24)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{P}\hat{\phi}_{j}{\bf Z}_{\ell}\,dy\,=\,0,\quad\ell=0,1,2,3\quad\mbox{for all}\quad t\in[0,T],

with

(9.25)

\displaystyle{\bf Z}_{0}(y)

\displaystyle=1,\quad p^{2}\,{\bf Z}_{3}(y,t)=G\left(\Gamma_{0j}\right)+b_{3}(y,t)

\displaystyle\,\,p^{2}{\bf Z}_{1}(y,t)

\displaystyle=y_{1}(1+b_{1}(y_{1},t))\,{\bf 1}_{B_{8R}}(y),\quad\,p^{2}\,{\bf Z}_{2}(y,t)=\left(y_{2}+\varepsilon y_{1}y_{2}\right){\bf 1}_{B_{8R}}(y),\quad

where
p(y_{1},t)
is defined in (
9.10
),
{\bf 1}_{B_{8R}}(y)=1
for
y\in B(0,8R)
,
=0
otherwise. The function
\Gamma_{0j}
is given in (
9.11
) and
G
is such that

G(\Gamma_{0}(y))=\nabla\Gamma_{0}\cdot y+2={1-|y|^{2}\over 1+|y|^{2}}.

Since
G^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0}(y))\sim|y|^{-2}
as
|y|\to\infty
, one has

G(\Gamma_{0j})=G(\Gamma_{0})(1+O(\varepsilon R)).

Besides, the functions

b_{3},b_{1}
will be explicitly defined in (
10.21
); for the moment we think at it as
b_{3}=O(\varepsilon^{2})
and
b_{1}=o(1)
, as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

For
\phi_{j}
as in (
9.23
), we write

(9.26)

\hat{\phi}=\left(\hat{\phi}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\phi}_{k}\right),\quad\beta=\left(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}\right),\quad\beta_{j}=\left(\beta_{j0},\beta_{j3}\right).

We assume that

(9.27)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U^{-1}(y)(\mathcal{P}\hat{\phi}_{j})^{2}(y,t)\,dy<\infty,\quad\forall t\in[0,T].

Let
\hat{\psi}_{j}(y,t)
be the solution of

(9.28)

-\mathop{\rm div}(\mathcal{P}\nabla\hat{\psi}_{j})=\mathcal{P}\hat{\phi}_{j},\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}\quad\mathbb{R}^{2},

predicted by Lemma
9.1
,
and define

(9.29)

\psi_{j}(y,t)=\hat{\psi}_{j}(y,t)+\sum_{l=0,3}\beta_{jl}(t)\mathcal{Z}_{2l}(y).

Then
\psi_{j}

satisfies the second condition in (
9.5
), namely

-\Delta_{5,j}\psi_{j}=\phi_{j}\quad(y,t)\in B(0,3R)\times[0,T).

Recall from (
9
) that

(9.30)

R={1\over\varepsilon|\log\varepsilon|^{5}}.

The decomposition for
\phi_{j}
in (
9.23
) is motivated by the validity of a key estimate for a quadratic form.

Lemma 9.2
.

There exists a number
\gamma>0
such that for any sufficiently small
\varepsilon
and all
\phi
satisfying conditions
(\ref{orto1})
-
(\ref{inte})
, the following holds:
let
g
be given by

g=\left(f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j})\right)^{-1}\left(\mathcal{P}\phi-\mathcal{P}f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j})\,p^{2}\,\psi\right),

where
\psi
solves
-\mathop{\rm div}(\mathcal{P}\nabla\psi)=\mathcal{P}\phi
. Then
we have

(9.31)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{P}\phi\,g\geq\frac{\gamma}{|\log R|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\mathcal{P}\phi)^{2}U^{-1},

where
R
is given by (
9.30
). We recall the definition of
\mathcal{P}(y_{1},t)
and
p(y_{1},t)
in (
9.10
),
\Gamma_{0j}
as in (
9.11
) and that
f_{0}(s)=e^{s}
.

Proof.

The proof is divided into two steps: we first prove (
9.31
) with the test function
g
replaced by
g_{0}
, with

g_{0}=U^{-1}\left(\mathcal{P}\phi-U\psi\right).

where
U
is defined in (
1.12
). Then we prove (
9.31
).

Let

\tilde{\phi}=2\,U^{-1}\mathcal{P}\phi
. Using the stereographic projection
\pi
introduced in (
9.19
), we get

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{P}\phi g_{0}=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\mathcal{P}\phi)^{2}U^{-1}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{P}\phi\psi=\int_{S^{2}}\tilde{\phi}^{2}-\int_{S^{2}}\tilde{\phi}\psi.

Consider the orthonormal basis in
L^{2}(S^{2})
of spherical harmonics
(e_{\ell})_{\ell}
, where
-\Delta_{S^{2}}e_{\ell}=\lambda_{\ell}e_{\ell}
.
Here
\lambda_{0}=0
and
e_{0}
is constant, while
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=\lambda_{3}=2
, with
e_{\ell}(z)=z_{\ell}
, for
\ell=1,2,3
. We decompose
\tilde{\phi}
as

\tilde{\phi}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\tilde{\phi}_{\ell}e_{\ell}(z)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{3}\tilde{\phi}_{\ell}e_{\ell}(z)+\tilde{\phi}^{\perp},\quad\tilde{\phi}_{\ell}=\int_{S^{2}}\phi\,e_{\ell}\,d\pi(z).

Since
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{P}\phi=\int_{S^{2}}\tilde{\phi}=0
, we get
\tilde{\phi}_{0}=0
. For
\ell\geq 1
, let
\psi_{\ell}
be defined by

-{\mathop{\rm div}}_{S^{2}}(\mathcal{P}\nabla_{S^{2}}\psi_{\ell})=e_{\ell}.

From Lemma
9.1
we get that
\psi_{\ell}\in H^{2}(S^{2})
and
\|\psi_{\ell}\|_{H^{2}(S^{2})}\leq C
for some
C>0
. Since
-{\mathop{\rm div}}_{S^{2}}(\mathcal{P}\nabla_{S^{2}}\psi)=\tilde{\phi}
, we have that
\psi=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\phi}_{\ell}\psi_{\ell}.

The equation satisfied by
\psi_{\ell}
becomes
-\mathop{\rm div}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\mathcal{P}\nabla\psi_{\ell})=Ue_{\ell}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

It is convenient to write

\psi_{\ell}=(-\Delta_{S^{2}})^{-1}e_{\ell}+\bar{\psi}_{\ell}={2\over\lambda_{\ell}}e_{\ell}+\bar{\psi}_{\ell}.

Then

-{\mathop{\rm div}}_{S^{2}}(\nabla_{S^{2}}\bar{\psi}_{\ell})=h_{\ell},\quad h_{\ell}={\nabla_{S^{2}}\mathcal{P}\nabla_{S^{2}}\psi_{\ell}\over\mathcal{P}}+{(1-\mathcal{P})e_{\ell}\over\mathcal{P}}.

A direct computation gives
\int_{S^{2}}h_{\ell}=0
. Besides,

\displaystyle\int_{S^{2}}h_{\ell}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{S^{2}}|{\nabla_{S^{2}}\mathcal{P}\over\mathcal{P}}\nabla_{S^{2}}\psi_{\ell}|^{2}+\int_{S^{2}}|{(1-\mathcal{P})\over\mathcal{P}}e_{\ell}|^{2}\leq c|\log\varepsilon|^{-6}\left(\|\psi_{\ell}\|_{H^{2}(S^{2})}^{2}+1\right)

for some
c>0
.
We argue as in Lemma
9.1
to get
\psi_{\ell}\in H^{2}(S^{2})
.
Thus

(9.32)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{P}\phi\,g_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=4}^{\infty}\left(1-\frac{2}{\lambda_{\ell}}\right)\tilde{\phi}_{\ell}^{2}+O(\varepsilon R)\|\tilde{\phi}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(S^{2})}\ \geq\

\displaystyle c_{1}\|\tilde{\phi}^{\perp}\|_{L^{2}(S^{2})}^{2}+O(\varepsilon R)\|\tilde{\phi}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(S^{2})}

for some uniform
c_{1}>0
. We also have, for
j=1,2

0=\int_{B(0,5R)}\mathcal{P}\phi{\bf Z}_{j}=c_{2}\tilde{\phi}_{j}+O(\|\tilde{\phi}^{\perp}\|_{L^{2}(S^{2})})\,|\log R|^{\frac{1}{2}}

for some uniform
c_{2}>0
, as
\varepsilon\to 0
. On the other hand, we have

0=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{P}\phi{\bf Z}_{3}=\tilde{\phi}_{3}+O(\varepsilon R)\|\tilde{\phi}\|_{L^{2}(S^{2})}.

From the above relations we get that for some
c>0
independent of
\varepsilon
,

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\phi}^{\perp}\|_{L^{2}(S^{2})}^{2}\,

\displaystyle\geq\,\|\tilde{\phi}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(S^{2})}-c\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}|\tilde{\phi}_{\ell}|^{2},

\displaystyle\geq\,(1-O(\varepsilon R))\,\|\tilde{\phi}\|_{L^{2}(S^{2})}^{2}\,-\,c|\log R|\,\|\tilde{\phi}^{\perp}\|_{L^{2}(S^{2})}^{2}.

From here we get

(1+c|\log R|)\,\|\tilde{\phi}^{\perp}\|_{L^{2}(S^{2})}^{2}\,\geq(1-o(1))\,\|\tilde{\phi}\|_{L^{2}(S^{2})}^{2}

with
o(1)\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
,
which combines with (
9.32
) to get

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{P}\phi\,g_{0}\geq\frac{\gamma}{|\log R|}\int_{S^{2}}\tilde{\phi}^{2}

for some uniform
\gamma>0
.

We next estimate
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{P}g
in terms of
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{P}g_{0}
. To this purpose, we write

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=g_{0}+\left(\left(f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j})\right)^{-1}-U^{-1}\right)\left(\mathcal{P}\phi-U\psi\right)+\left(f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j})\right)^{-1}\left(U-\mathcal{P}f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j})p^{2}\right)\psi

Recalling the definition of
\Gamma_{0j}
in (
9.11
), we get

f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j})=U(y)\left(1+O(|\log\varepsilon|^{-2})\right);

hence we get

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{P}\phi\,\,\left(\left(f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j})\right)^{-1}-U^{-1}\right)\left(\mathcal{P}\phi-U\psi\right)=O(|\log\varepsilon|^{-2})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{P}\phi\,g_{0}.

Besides

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{P}\phi\left(f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j})\right)^{-1}\left(U-\mathcal{P}f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j})p^{2}\right)\psi\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim|\log\varepsilon|^{-2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\mathcal{P}\phi\psi|\lesssim|\log\varepsilon|^{-2}\|U^{-{1\over 2}}\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}.

Thus we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{P}\phi\,g\geq(1+O(|\log\varepsilon|^{-2}))\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{P}\phi\,g_{0}-O(|\log\varepsilon|^{-2})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U^{-1}(\mathcal{\phi})^{2}\geq{\tilde{\gamma}\over|\log R|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U^{-1}(\mathcal{\phi})^{2}

for some new
\tilde{\gamma}
which is uniformly positive as
\varepsilon\to 0
. This concludes the proof.
∎

We will make use of this result to establish a-priori bounds for solutions to a projected version of the inner problem (
9.5
). In Section
10
we will first establish a-priori bounds in weighted
L^{2}
-spaces, which will be used to establish the weighted
L^{\infty}
a-priori bounds. Before entering Section
10
, we give a sketch of the proof to solve in
(\phi^{in},\psi^{in},\phi^{out},\psi^{out})
the whole inner-outer gluing system (
9.5
)-(
9.6
)-(
9.8
).

9.3.
Strategy for the rest of the proof

Let

b_{j}(\beta_{j},\psi^{out},{\bf a})=(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\chi)^{2}\,\eta_{4\varepsilon}\,(\hat{\psi}_{j}+\sum_{l=0,3}\beta_{jl}(t)\mathcal{Z}_{1l}+r_{j}\psi^{out})

where
\eta_{4\varepsilon}
is defined in (
9.9
) and
introduce the following operators, depending on a homotopy parameter
\lambda\in[0,1]

(9.33)

\displaystyle E_{j,\lambda}

\displaystyle[\hat{\phi}_{j},\beta_{j}\,\psi^{out},{\bf a}](y,t):=|\log\varepsilon|\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\chi)\,{\partial}_{t}\hat{\phi}_{j}

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\perp}\left(\Gamma_{0j}+\lambda\eta_{4\varepsilon}b_{j}^{*}+\lambda\eta_{4\varepsilon}b_{j}^{**}+\lambda b_{j}(\beta_{j},\psi^{out},{\bf a})\right)\cdot\nabla\hat{\phi}_{j}

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\perp}((1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\chi)^{2}\hat{\psi}_{j})\cdot\nabla\left(f_{0}\left(\Gamma_{0}+\lambda b_{j}^{*}\right)+\eta_{4\varepsilon}U^{*}\right)

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\perp}\left[\varepsilon_{j}\left(|\log\varepsilon|{\partial}_{t}{\bf a}_{j}+D_{x}{\nabla}_{x}\varphi_{j}({\bf P}_{j};{\bf P})[{\bf a}]+\lambda\eta_{4\varepsilon}(1+{\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}\over r_{1}})^{2}r_{j}\psi^{out}\right)\cdot y\right]\nabla U

\displaystyle+\lambda\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{j}(\beta_{j},\psi^{out},{\bf a})+|\log\varepsilon|\varepsilon_{j}^{2}(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\chi)\sum_{\ell=0,3}{\partial}_{t}(\beta_{j\ell}\mathcal{Z}_{1\ell})\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]

where
f_{0}(s)=e^{s}
,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{j}

\displaystyle=|\log\varepsilon|B_{0}(\sum_{\ell=0,3}\beta_{j\ell}\mathcal{Z}_{1\ell})+\nabla^{\perp}\left(\Gamma_{0j}+\eta_{4\varepsilon}b_{j}^{*}+\eta_{4\varepsilon}b_{j}^{**}+b_{j}\right)\cdot\nabla(\sum_{\ell=0,3}\beta_{j\ell}\mathcal{Z}_{1\ell})

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\perp}\left((1+{\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}\over r_{1}}\chi)^{2}(r_{j}\psi^{out})\right)\nabla(\eta_{4\varepsilon}U^{*})

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\perp}\left[\left(\varepsilon_{j}|\log\varepsilon|\dot{\bf a}_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}{\nabla}_{x}\varphi_{j}(P_{j};P)-{\nabla}_{x}\varphi_{j}({\bf P};{\bf P})-D_{x}{\nabla}_{x}\varphi_{j}({\bf P}_{j};{\bf P})[{\bf a}]\right)\cdot y\right]\nabla U

\displaystyle+{\mathcal{E}}_{j}[\phi_{j}^{*},\psi_{j}^{*}\,\psi^{*,out},{\bf a}](y,t)

where
{\mathcal{E}}_{j}[\phi_{j}^{*},\psi_{j}^{*}\,\psi^{*,out},P]
is defined
and estimated in Proposition
5.1
.

If
\lambda=1
and we restrict the problem to
B(0,3R)
, we get the operator
E_{j}^{in}
defined in (
9
), or equivalently (
9.15
).

Recalling the notation introduced in (
9.26
), we define

\displaystyle E_{1,\lambda}^{out}(\phi^{out},\psi^{out},\hat{\phi},\beta,{\bf a})

\displaystyle\,=\,|\log\varepsilon|\,r\,{\partial}_{t}\phi^{out}\,

\displaystyle+\,{\nabla}^{\perp}\big(r^{2}(\Psi^{*}-r_{0}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|+\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\bar{\eta}_{j2}\over r_{j}}\psi_{j}({x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}})+\psi^{out}))\cdot{\nabla}\phi^{out}

\displaystyle\ +\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{out}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out},{\bf a})\quad(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T)

where

(9.34)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}^{out}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out},{\bf a})\,:=\,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{k}\left[r\,|\log\varepsilon|\,{\partial}_{t}\bar{\eta}_{j1}+\nabla_{x}^{\perp}(r^{2}(\Psi^{*}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\bar{\eta}_{j2}\over r_{j}}\psi_{j}({x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}})+\psi^{out}-r_{0}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|))\nabla\bar{\eta}_{1j}\right]{\phi_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}^{2}r_{j}}

\displaystyle+\left[\sum_{j=1}^{k}(\bar{\eta}_{2j}-\bar{\eta}_{1j})\nabla_{x}^{\perp}(r^{2}({\psi_{j}\over r_{j}}+\psi^{out}))+{r^{2}\psi_{j}\over r_{j}}\nabla_{x}^{\perp}\bar{\eta}_{2j}\right]\nabla_{x}W^{*}

\displaystyle+(1-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\bar{\eta}_{2j})\nabla^{\perp}(r^{2}\psi^{out})\cdot\nabla W^{*}+(1-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\bar{\eta}_{j1})S_{1}(W^{*},\Psi^{*})=0\quad(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T),

We also define

\displaystyle E_{1,\lambda}^{out}(\psi^{out},\phi^{out},\hat{\phi},\beta,{\bf a})\ =\ \Delta_{5}\psi^{out}+\lambda\phi^{out}+\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{k}(\bar{\eta}_{j1}-\bar{\eta}_{j2}){\phi_{j}\over r_{j}\varepsilon_{j}^{2}}

\displaystyle+\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{k}({\psi_{j}\over r_{j}}\Delta_{5}\bar{\eta}_{j2}+2\nabla_{x}\bar{\eta}_{j2}\nabla_{x}{\psi_{j}\over r_{j}}),\quad(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T)

The key observation is that for

\phi_{j}
,
\psi_{j}
given by (
9.23
)-(
9.29
) we have the identities, when
\lambda=1

\displaystyle{E}_{j,1}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\phi^{out},\psi^{out},{\bf a})\,=

\displaystyle\,E_{j}^{in}(\phi_{j},\psi_{j},\psi^{out};\beta,{\bf a}){\quad\hbox{in }}B(0,3R)\times[0,T],

\displaystyle E_{1}^{out}(\phi^{out},\psi^{out},\hat{\phi},\beta,{\bf a})\,=

\displaystyle\,E^{out}(\phi^{out},\psi^{out},\phi^{in},\psi^{in};\beta,{\bf a}){\quad\hbox{in }}\Sigma\times[0,T),

\displaystyle E_{1,1}^{out}(\psi^{out},\phi^{out},\hat{\phi},\beta,{\bf a})\,=

\displaystyle\,E_{1}^{out}(\psi^{out},\psi^{in},\phi^{out};\beta,{\bf a}){\quad\hbox{in }}\Sigma\times[0,T),

with boundary and decay conditions

{{\partial}\over{\partial}r}\psi^{out}(x,t)=0,\quad(x,t)\in{\partial}\Sigma\times[0,T],\quad|\psi^{out}(x,t)|\to 0,\quad\quad\mbox{as}\quad|x|\to\infty.

Here
E_{j}^{in}
,
E^{out}
and
E_{1}^{out}
are defined respectively in (
9
), (
9.4
) and (
9.7
). In other words, solving the
inner-outer gluing
system (
9.5
)-(
9.6
) coupled with the boundary and decay conditions (
9.8
) amounts to make the three quantities above equal to zero (keeping the boundary and decay conditions).
We will do this by a continuation argument that involves finding uniform a-priori estimates for the corresponding equations along the deformation parameter
\lambda
imposing in addition initial condition
0
for all the parameter functions.

We use the following strategy. We consider the functions
\beta_{j},\psi^{out},{\bf a}
as given
and require that
\hat{\phi}_{j}
satisfies an initial value problem of the form

(9.35)

{E}_{j,\lambda}(\hat{\phi}_{j},\beta_{j},\phi^{out},\psi^{out},{\bf a})=\sum_{l=0}^{3}c_{lj}(t)z_{1l}(y){\quad\hbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T],\quad\hat{\phi}_{j}(y,0)=0{\quad\hbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{2}

where

(9.36)

\displaystyle z_{10}(y)=U_{0}(y),

\displaystyle z_{11}(y)={\partial}_{y_{1}}U_{0}(y),

\displaystyle z_{12}(y)={\partial}_{y_{2}}U_{0}(y),

\displaystyle z_{13}(y)=2U_{0}(y)+{\nabla}_{y}U_{0}(y)\cdot y.

for some explicit functions
c_{lj}(t)
. We prove that
c_{lj}(t)
are linearly dependent on
\hat{\phi}_{j}
and can be computed after integrating the equation against
{\bf Z}_{\ell}
in space variable. See (
9.25
) for the definition of
{\bf Z}_{\ell}
.

Solving
E_{\lambda,j}\equiv 0

is equivalent to
solving the initial value problems

(9.37)

\displaystyle c_{lj}[\hat{\phi}_{j},\beta_{j},\phi^{out},\psi^{out},{\bf a},\lambda](t)

\displaystyle=0\quad\mbox{for all}\quad t\in[0,T],\,\ell=0,1,2,3

\displaystyle{\bf a}_{j}(0)

\displaystyle=\beta_{j}(0)=0

for all
j=1,\ldots,k
.
We require

(9.38)

E_{\lambda}^{out}(\phi^{out},\psi^{out},\hat{\phi}_{j},\beta_{j},{\bf a})=0{\quad\hbox{in }}\Sigma\times[0,T],\quad\phi^{out}(\cdot,0)=0{\quad\hbox{in }}\Sigma

and

(9.39)

\displaystyle E_{1,\lambda}^{out}(\psi^{out},\phi^{out},\hat{\phi}_{j},\beta_{j},{\bf a})

\displaystyle=0{\quad\hbox{in }}\ \ \Sigma\times[0,T],

\displaystyle{{\partial}\over{\partial}r}\psi^{out}=0{\quad\hbox{on }}{\partial}\Sigma\times[0,T],

\displaystyle|\psi^{out}(x,t)|\to 0,\quad\quad\mbox{as}\quad|x|\to\infty.

We recall the form of
\phi_{j}
and
\psi_{j}
, as in (
9.23
)-(
9.29
)

\displaystyle\phi_{j}(y,t)\,=

\displaystyle\,\hat{\phi}_{j}(y,t)+\sum_{l=0,3}\beta_{jl}(t)Z_{1l}(y,t),

\displaystyle\psi_{j}(y,t)\,=

\displaystyle\,\hat{\psi}_{j}(y,t)+\sum_{l=0,3}\beta_{jl}(t)\mathcal{Z}_{2l}(y).

We can write the system of equations (
9.35
), (
9.37
), (
9.38
), (
9.39
) in the form of a fixed point problem for the variable

\vec{p}=(\hat{\phi},\beta,\phi^{out},\psi^{out},{\bf a}).

The fix point problem has the form

(9.40)

\vec{p}={\mathcal{F}}(\vec{p},\lambda),\quad\vec{p}\in{\mathcal{O}}\,.

Here
{\mathcal{O}}
designates a bounded open set in an appropriate Banach space with
\vec{p}=\vec{0}\in{\mathcal{O}}
and

{\mathcal{F}}(\cdot,\lambda)
is a homotopy of nonlinear compact operators on
\bar{\mathcal{O}}

with
{\mathcal{F}}(\cdot,0)
linear.

We shall prove that a suitable choice of a small
{\mathcal{O}}
yields that for all
\lambda\in[0,1]

no solution of (
9.40
) with
\vec{p}\in{\partial}{\mathcal{O}}
exists.
Existence of a solution of (
9.40
) for
\lambda=1
thus follows from standard degree theory. But this precisely corresponds to a solution of the original problem. The definition of the norm and the set
{\mathcal{O}}
will yield the desired properties of the solution of Euler equation thus obtained.

In order to find the desired a priori estimates we need several preliminary considerations which we make in the next section.

10.
Some a-priori estimates

Let us consider functions
\phi(y,t)
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
t\in[0,T]
that satisfy

\|\mathcal{P}\phi\,U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}<\infty,\quad{\mbox{where}}\quad\mathcal{P}(y_{1},t)=p^{3}(y_{1},t),\quad p(y_{1},t)=(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\chi).

We recall that the function
U
is defined in (
1.12
),
\mathcal{P}
,
p
were introduced in (
9.10
) and
\chi
is in (
9.9
).
We also assume the orthogonality conditions on
\mathcal{P}\phi

(10.1)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{P}\phi(y,t){\bf Z}_{\ell}(y)\,dy\,=\,0,\quad\ell=0,1,2,3,\quad\forall t\in[0,T]

where the function
{\bf Z}_{\ell}
,
\ell=0,1,2,3
are defined in (
9.25
).
Let
\psi(y,t)
be the solution, predicted by Lemma
9.1
of

(10.2)

-\mathop{\rm div}(\mathcal{P}\nabla\psi)=\mathcal{P}\phi,\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}\quad\mathbb{R}^{2}.

We let
f_{0}(v)=e^{v}
. This section is devoted to establish a series of a-priori estimates for solutions to
a linear transport equation of the form

(10.3)

\displaystyle|\log\varepsilon|\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle p\,{\partial}_{t}\phi+\nabla_{y}^{\perp}\left(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}+a\right)\cdot\nabla_{y}\left(\phi-f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,\psi\right)+\,E(y,t)\,=\,0{\quad\hbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(0,T),

\displaystyle\phi(\cdot,0)\,=\,0{\quad\hbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{2}

These estimates will be used to treat (
9.35
).

On the functions

a_{*}(y,t)
, and
a(y,t)
that appear in (
10.3
) we assume

(10.4)

a_{*}(y,t),\,a(y,t)\,=0\quad\hbox{for }|y|\geq 8R,\quad\Delta_{y}(a+a_{*})\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(0,T))

and
for some numbers
C>0
,
\nu>0
,

\displaystyle|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}|{\partial}_{t}a_{*}(y,t)|+(1+|y|)|\,{\nabla}_{y}a_{*}(y,t)|+|a_{*}(y,t)|\ \leq

\displaystyle\ C\varepsilon^{2}\,(1+|y|^{2})\,\log(1+|y|)

(10.5)

\displaystyle|{\nabla}_{y}a(y,t)|\ \leq

\displaystyle\ \varepsilon^{2+\nu}.

These assumptions are consistent with the description of Problem (
9.35
), in the version contained in (
9.33
): we will take

(10.6)

a_{*}=\lambda\eta_{4\varepsilon}\,b_{j}^{*},\quad a=\lambda\left(\eta_{4\varepsilon}b_{j}^{**}+b_{j}\right),

where
b_{j}^{*}
,
b_{j}^{**}
satisfy bounds similar to (
9.13
) and
b_{j}
is defined as in (
9.14
).

10.1.
An
L^{2}
-weighted a priori estimate

Our first result is an
L^{2}
-weighted a-priori estimate on a solution to (
10.3
).
We have the following

Lemma 10.1
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that for any

a
,
a_{*}
satisfying
(\ref{nu0})
-
(\ref{nu})
,
R
given by (
9.30
), all sufficiently small
\varepsilon

and any solution
\phi
of
(\ref{innerL})
-
(\ref{orto40})
with

(10.7)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,\mathcal{P}\phi(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\ <\ +\infty

we have

(10.8)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,\mathcal{P}\phi(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\ \leq\ C\,\varepsilon^{-2}\,|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}}\,\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\,\|E(\cdot,t)\,U^{-1/2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Proof.

Let us assume that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\,\|E(\cdot,t)\,U^{-1/2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,<\,+\infty

and define the functions

U_{1}=f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}),\quad U_{1}g_{1}=\phi-f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,\psi,

where
f_{0}(s)=e^{s}

and

P(y_{1},t)=p^{3}(y_{1},t),\quad p(y_{1},t)=(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\chi).

We multiply
equation (
10.3
) against
g_{1}
and integrate in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. One term is

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}p\,{\partial}_{t}\phi\,g_{1}\,dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}p{\partial}_{t}\phi{\phi\over U_{1}}\,dy-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{\partial}_{t}\phi\,\mathcal{P}\psi dy

\displaystyle={1\over 2}{\partial}_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}pU_{1}^{-1}\phi^{2}\,dy-{1\over 2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{\partial}_{t}\left(pU_{1}^{-1}\right)\phi^{2}

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{\partial}_{t}\left(\mathcal{P}\phi\,\right)\psi dy+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi\,{\partial}_{t}\mathcal{P}\psi dy.

Since
-\mathop{\rm div}(\mathcal{P}\nabla\psi)=\mathcal{P}\phi
,

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{\partial}_{t}\left(\mathcal{P}\phi\,\right)\psi dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{\partial}_{t}\left(\mathop{\rm div}(\mathcal{P}\nabla\psi)\,\right)\psi dy=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathop{\rm div}({\partial}_{t}\mathcal{P}\nabla\psi)\,\psi dy+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathop{\rm div}(\mathcal{P}\nabla{\partial}_{t}\psi)\,\psi dy

On the other hand, using the symmetry of the form
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathop{\rm div}(\mathcal{P}\nabla\psi_{1})\psi_{2}dy
we have that

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathop{\rm div}(\mathcal{P}\nabla\psi)\psi\,dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathop{\rm div}({\partial}_{t}\mathcal{P}\nabla\psi)\psi\,dy+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathop{\rm div}(\mathcal{P}\nabla{\partial}_{t}\psi)\psi\,dy+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathop{\rm div}(\mathcal{P}\nabla\psi){\partial}_{t}\psi\,dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathop{\rm div}({\partial}_{t}\mathcal{P}\nabla\psi)\psi\,dy+2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathop{\rm div}(\mathcal{P}\nabla{\partial}_{t}\psi)\psi\,dy

from which we get that

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{\partial}_{t}\left(\mathcal{P}\phi\right)\psi dy

\displaystyle={1\over 2}{\partial}_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathop{\rm div}(\mathcal{P}\nabla\psi)\psi\,dy+{1\over 2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathop{\rm div}({\partial}_{t}\mathcal{P}\nabla\psi)\psi\,dy.

Thus we conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}p\,{\partial}_{t}\phi\,g_{1}\,dy

\displaystyle={1\over 2}{\partial}_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}p\phi g_{1}\,dy-{1\over 2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{\partial}_{t}\left(p\,U_{1}^{-1}\right)\phi^{2}

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi\,{\partial}_{t}\mathcal{P}\psi dy+{1\over 2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathop{\rm div}({\partial}_{t}\mathcal{P}\nabla\psi)\psi\,dy.

Moreover

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}

\displaystyle\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}+a)\cdot\nabla_{y}(U_{1}g_{1})g_{1}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U_{1}^{-1}\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}+a)\cdot\nabla_{y}({U_{1}^{2}g_{1}^{2}\over 2})\,dy
