\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla\cdot\left(U_{1}^{-1}\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}+a)\right){U_{1}^{2}g_{1}^{2}\over 2}\,dy=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}+a){\nabla U_{1}\over U_{1}}{U_{1}g_{1}^{2}\over 2}\,dy,

and we conclude that

(10.9)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle|\log\varepsilon|\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\over 2}{\partial}_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\chi)\phi g_{1}\,dy-{|\log\varepsilon|\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\over 2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{\partial}_{t}\left((1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\chi)U_{1}^{-1}\right)\phi^{2}

\displaystyle+|\log\varepsilon|\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi\,{\partial}_{t}\mathcal{P}\psi dy+{|\log\varepsilon|\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\over 2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathop{\rm div}({\partial}_{t}\mathcal{P}\nabla\psi)\psi\,dy

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}+a){\nabla U_{1}\over U_{1}}{U_{1}g_{1}^{2}\over 2}\,dy+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Eg_{1}\,dy=0.

Next, we estimate the last four terms in the above expression.
Using estimates on
U_{1}
, the bounds on
\psi
as in Lemma
9.1
and the explicit definition of
\mathcal{P}
in (
9.10
), we get

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi^{2}{\partial}_{t}(U_{1}^{-1}p)\,dy\right|\lesssim|\log\varepsilon|^{-{9\over 2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi^{2}U^{-1}

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi\psi{\partial}_{t}\mathcal{P}\,dy\right|\lesssim|\log\varepsilon|^{-{9\over 2}}(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi^{2}U^{-1})^{1\over 2}(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\psi^{2}U)^{1\over 2}\lesssim|\log\varepsilon|^{-{9\over 2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi^{2}U^{-1}

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathop{\rm div}({\partial}_{t}\mathcal{P}\nabla\psi)\psi\,dy\right|\lesssim|\log\varepsilon|^{-{9\over 2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi^{2}U^{-1},

To estimate
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}+a){\nabla U_{1}\over U_{1}}{U_{1}g_{1}^{2}\over 2}\,dy
, we observe that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}

\displaystyle\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}+a){\nabla U_{1}\over U_{1}}{U_{1}g_{1}^{2}\over 2}\,dy=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla_{y}^{\perp}a{\nabla U_{1}\over U_{1}}{U_{1}g_{1}^{2}\over 2}\,dy.

From (
10.5
) we get

\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla_{y}^{\perp}a{\nabla U_{1}\over U_{1}}{U_{1}g_{1}^{2}\over 2}\,dy\right|\lesssim\varepsilon^{2+\nu}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{U_{1}g_{1}^{2}\over 2}\,dy.

Finally

\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Eg_{1}\,dy\right|\leq\|EU^{-{1\over 2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,\|U^{1\over 2}g_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Since

\mathcal{P}(y_{1},t)=1+O(\varepsilon R)

uniformly as
\varepsilon\to 0
for
y_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
,
t\in[0,T]
,
from Lemma
9.2
and (
9.31
) we obtain

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U_{1}g_{1}^{2}\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi^{2}U^{-1}\leq C|\log\varepsilon|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}p\,\phi g_{1}\,dy,

for some
C>0
. Define

f(t)=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}p\,\phi g_{1}\,dy\right)^{1\over 2}.

We conclude that

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi^{2}{\partial}_{t}(U_{1}^{-1}\,p)\,dy\right|\lesssim\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon||\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}}f^{2}(t),

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi\psi{\partial}_{t}\mathcal{P}\,dy\right|\lesssim\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon||\log\varepsilon|^{-{3\over 2}}f^{2}(t),

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathop{\rm div}({\partial}_{t}\mathcal{P}\nabla\psi)\psi\,dy\right|\lesssim\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon||\log\varepsilon|^{-{3\over 2}}f^{2}(t),

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Eg_{1}\,dy\right|\lesssim\|EU^{-{1\over 2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}f(t).

Inserting these estimates in (
10.9
), we get

\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|{d\over dt}f^{2}(t)\leq\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|A_{\varepsilon}f^{2}(t)+C\|EU^{-{1\over 2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}f(t),

with
A_{\varepsilon}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Then we find

\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\frac{df}{dt}(t)\,\leq\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|A_{\varepsilon}f(t)+C\|E(\cdot,t)\,U^{-1/2}\|_{L^{2}}.

Performing the change of variable
\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|{d\over dt}={d\over d\tau}
, Gronwall’s inequality yields

f(t)\,\leq\,C\,\varepsilon^{-2}|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\,\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|E(\cdot,t)\,U^{-1/2}\|_{L^{2}}.

This inequality and Lemma
9.2
yield (
10.8
).
∎

10.2.
An
L^{\infty}
-weighted a priori estimate

We consider now a class of functions
E(y,t)
such that for a number
0<\beta<1
we have

(10.10)

\|E\|_{3+\beta}:=\sup_{(y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]}|(1+|y|)^{3+\beta}E(y,t)\|<+\infty.

We observe that there exists
C_{*}>0
such that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\,\|E(\cdot,t)\,U^{-1/2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\ \leq\ C_{*}\,\|E\|_{3+\beta}.

Hence Lemma
10.1
is applicable.

Lemma 10.2
.

Under the assumptions of Lemma
10.1
, there exists a small
\sigma>0
such that, for some
0<\alpha<1
and all small
\varepsilon
, we have that

(10.11)

\displaystyle|\psi(y,t)|\,+\,(1+|y|)|{\nabla}_{y}\psi(y,t)|+(1+|y|)^{1+\alpha}[{\nabla}\psi(\cdot,t)]_{\alpha}(y)

\displaystyle\leq\,\frac{\varepsilon^{-2-\sigma}}{1+|y|}\,\|E\|_{3+\beta}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad(y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T].

where
\psi(y,t)
is given by
(\ref{psi1})
where
\phi(y,t)
is a solution of
(\ref{innerL})
-
(\ref{orto40})
satisfying
(\ref{aaa})
.

Proof.

From (
10.3
) we get that
\phi(y,t)
satisfies the transport equation

(10.12)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\,p\,\partial_{t}\phi+

\displaystyle\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}+a)\cdot\nabla_{y}\phi+\,\tilde{E}(y,t)\,=\,0{\quad\hbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(0,T),

\displaystyle\phi(\cdot,0)\,=\,0{\quad\hbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{2}

where

\tilde{E}(y,t)\,=\,E(y,t)-\nabla_{y}^{\perp}\left(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}+a\right)\cdot\nabla_{y}\left(f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})p^{2}\psi\right).

The result of Lemma
7.1
is still valid for a transport equation of the form (
10.12
).
Let us fix a number
p
with
2<p<\frac{2}{1-\beta}
. Then we have that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|U^{\frac{1}{p}-1}E(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\ \leq\ C\,\|E\|_{3+\beta}.

Since
\phi
solves equation (
10.12
) and Lemma
7.1
apply to yield

\|U^{{1\over p}-1}\phi\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\varepsilon^{-2}|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\|U^{{1\over p}-1}\tilde{E}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Let us estimate this weighted
L^{p}
norm for the second term in
\tilde{E}
. We have

\big|\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}+a)\cdot\nabla_{y}(f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})p^{2}\psi)\big|\ \leq\ C\,\big[\frac{1}{1+|y|^{5}}|{\nabla}\psi|+\frac{1}{1+|y|^{6}}|\psi|\,\big].

Since

\left\|\,U^{{1\over p}-1}\frac{|{\nabla}\psi|}{1+|y|^{5}}\,\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,\leq\,C\left\|U^{-{1\over 2}+{1\over p}}|\nabla\psi|\,\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq\,C\,\left\|U^{-{1\over 2}}\phi\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

where the last inequality follows from (
9.22
).
From (
9.21
) we get

\left\|U^{{1\over p}-1}\frac{|\psi|}{1+|y|^{6}}\,\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,\leq\,C\left\|U^{-{1\over 2}}\phi\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Combining the above estimates, we conclude

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|U^{\frac{1}{p}-1}\tilde{E}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\ \leq C\left(\|E\|_{3+\beta}+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|U^{-{1\over 2}}\phi|\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\right).

We now apply estimate (
10.8
) and we conclude that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|U^{{1\over p}-1}\phi(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\varepsilon^{-4}\,|\log\varepsilon|^{-{3\over 2}}\,\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|U^{-{1\over 2}}E(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Next we use an interpolation argument to control
\|U^{{1\over q}-1}\phi\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
for some

q\in(2,p)
. Write
q
in the form
in the form

q=2(1-\lambda)+\lambda p,\quad\lambda\in(0,1).

Using Hölder’s inequality we check that

\begin{split}\|U^{{1\over q}-1}\phi\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{q}&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U^{1-q}|\phi|^{q}\,dy\leq\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U^{-1}|\phi|^{2}\,dy\right)^{(1-\lambda)}\,\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U^{1-p}|\phi|^{p}\,dy\right)^{\lambda}\\
&=\|U^{-{1\over 2}}\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2(1-\lambda)}\,\|U^{{1\over p}-1}\phi\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{p\lambda},\end{split}

We thus get that

\displaystyle\|U^{{1\over q}-1}\phi\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq C\,\varepsilon^{-2-2{p\lambda\over q}}\,|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1-\lambda\over q}-{3p\lambda\over 2q}}\,\|E\|_{3+\beta}.

Inserting this information in the above estimate we get

\displaystyle\|U^{{1\over q}-1}\phi\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq C\,\varepsilon^{-2-\sigma}\,\|E\|_{3+\beta},

for some
\sigma>0
.
Estimate (
10.11
) follows from Lemma
9.1
. The number
\sigma>0
can be taken arbitrarily small (asking
\lambda
to be close to
0
), in particular satisfying
\sigma<\beta
.
The proof is concluded.
∎

As a consequence of the above result we can also get an
L^{\infty}
-weighted estimate for
\phi
.

Corollary 10.1
.

Under the assumptions of Lemma
10.1
, we also have the estimate

(10.13)

|\phi(y,t)|\ \leq\ C\,\Big[\frac{\varepsilon^{-2-\sigma}}{1+|y|^{3+\beta}}+\frac{\varepsilon^{-4-\sigma}}{1+|y|^{7}}\Big]\,\|E\|_{3+\beta}.

Proof.

Recall that
\phi(y,t)
solves (
10.12
). From Lemma
10.2
, we get

|\tilde{E}(y,t)|\leq C\Big[\frac{1}{1+|y|^{3+\beta}}+\frac{\varepsilon^{-2-\sigma}}{1+|y|^{7}}\Big]\,\|E\|_{3+\beta}.

Estimate (
10.13
) then follows as a direct application of Lemma
7.1
for
p=+\infty
.
∎

We next revisit the estimates for
\psi
given in Lemma (
9.1
) and in Lemma
10.2
, in view of Corollary
10.1
.
This will be useful in the sequel.

Lemma 10.3
.

Assume the validity of the assumptions of Lemma
10.1
, and let
\psi(y,t)
be given by
(\ref{psi1})
where
\phi(y,t)
is a solution of
(\ref{innerL})
-
(\ref{orto40})
satisfying
(\ref{aaa})
. Then

\psi=\psi_{1}+\psi_{2}

where, for
y=r\,e^{i\theta}
,

(10.14)

\displaystyle\psi_{1}(y,t)

\displaystyle=A_{1}(r,t)\cos\theta+A_{2}(r,t)\sin\theta,

\displaystyle(1+|y|)|{\partial}_{r}A_{j}(r,t)|

\displaystyle+|A_{j}(r,t)|\lesssim{1\over 1+|y|^{1+\sigma}}\|U^{{1\over q}-1}\phi\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\,{R^{{2\over q}-1}\over 1+|y|}\,\|U^{{1\over q}-1}\phi\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},\quad j=1,2

and

(10.15)

\displaystyle(1+|y|)|\nabla_{y}\psi_{2}(y,t)|+|\psi_{2}(y,t)|

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big[\frac{\varepsilon^{-2-\sigma}}{1+|y|^{1+\beta}}+\frac{\varepsilon^{-4-\sigma}}{1+|y|^{5}}\Big]\,\|E\|_{3+\beta},

for some
\sigma>0
.

Proof.

Under the orthogonality conditions (
10.1
) on
\mathcal{P}\phi
, the result in Lemma
9.1
gives the existence and uniqueness of a solution
\psi
to (
10.2
) such that
|\psi(y,t)|\to 0
as
|y|\to\infty
, for all
t\in[0,T]
.

Decompose
h:=\mathcal{P}\phi
in Fourier series

h(r,\theta,t)=H_{1}+H_{2},\quad,\quad H_{1}=\sum_{n=\pm 1}h_{n}(r,t)e^{in\theta},\quad H_{2}=\sum_{n\not=0,\pm 1}h_{n}(r,t)e^{in\theta}.

We let
\psi_{1}
to be the solution to
-\Delta\psi_{1}=H_{1}
with decay to
0
at infinity. It has the form in (
10.14
). Next we show the validity of the bounds in (
10.14
).

Using the orthogonality conditions (
9.24
) for
\ell=0,1,2
, we write

\displaystyle\psi_{1}(y,t)

\displaystyle={1\over 2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\log{1\over|y-z|}-\log{1\over|y|}-{y\cdot z\over|y|^{2}}\right)\,H_{1}(z,t)\,dz

\displaystyle-{1\over 2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B(0,5R)}{y\cdot z\over|y|^{2}}\,H_{1}(z,t)\,dz.

The second integral can be easily estimated

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B(0,5R)}{y\cdot z\over|y|^{2}}\,H_{1}(z,t)\,dz\right|

\displaystyle\leq{C\over 1+|y|}\|U^{{1\over q}-1}\phi\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\left(\int_{\rho>5R}{\rho^{q\over q-1}\over 1+\rho^{{4q-4\over q-1}}}\rho\,d\rho\right)^{{q-1\over q}}

\displaystyle\leq R^{{2\over q}-1}\,{C\over 1+|y|}\,\|U^{{1\over q}-1}\phi\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

To estimate the first integral, we split the region of integration in
|z|<{|y|\over 2}
and its complement.
For
|z|<{|y|\over 2}
, we Taylor expand the quantity inside the bracket and get

\displaystyle\Bigl|\int_{|z|<{|y|\over 2}}\left(\log{1\over|y-z|}-\log{1\over|y|}-{y\cdot z\over|y|^{2}}\right)\,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(z,t)\phi(z,t)\,dz\Bigl|\leq{C\over 1+|y|^{2}}\int_{|z|<{|y|\over 2}}|z|^{2}\phi\,dz

\displaystyle\leq{C\over 1+|y|^{2}}\|U^{{1\over q}-1}\phi\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\left(\int_{|z|<{|y|\over 2}}{\rho^{2q\over q-1}\over 1+\rho^{{4q-4\over q-1}}}\rho\,d\rho\right)^{{q-1\over q}}

\displaystyle\leq{C\over 1+|y|^{2-{2\over q}}}\|U^{{1\over q}-1}\phi\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

In the complementary region, we get

\displaystyle\Bigl|\int_{|z|>{|y|\over 2}}\left(\log{1\over|y-z|}-\log{1\over|y|}-{y\cdot z\over|y|^{2}}\right)\,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(z,t)\phi(z,t)\,dz\Bigl|\leq\Bigl|\int_{|z|>{|y|\over 2}}\left(\log{1\over|y-z|}-{y\cdot z\over|y|^{2}}\right)\,\mathcal{P}(z,t)\phi(z,t)\,dz\Bigl|

\displaystyle\leq{C\over 1+|y|^{2-{2\over q}}}\,\log(1+|y|)\|U^{{1\over q}-1}\phi\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Thus we conclude that

|\psi_{1}(y,t)|\lesssim{1\over 1+|y|^{2-{2\over q}-\sigma^{\prime}}}\|U^{{1\over q}-1}\phi\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\,{R^{{2\over q}-1}\over 1+|y|}\,\|U^{{1\over q}-1}\phi\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

for any
\sigma^{\prime}>0
. Proceeding in a similar way, we get the estimate for
\nabla\psi_{1}
and the validity of (
10.14
).

Consider the function

H=H_{2}+(\mathcal{P}-1)H_{1}+\nabla\mathcal{P}\nabla\psi_{1},

and let
\psi_{2}
solve
-\mathop{\rm div}(\mathcal{P}\nabla\psi_{2})=H.

A direct computation gives

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}

\displaystyle\left[(\mathcal{P}-1)H_{1}+\nabla\mathcal{P}\cdot\nabla\psi_{1}\right]dy=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}H_{1}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{P}H_{1}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{P}\Delta\psi_{1}=0.

A direct inspection gives that
H
has no mode
1
in its Fourier decomposition and

|H|\lesssim\Big[\frac{\varepsilon^{-2-\sigma}}{1+|y|^{3+\beta}}+\frac{\varepsilon^{-4-\sigma}}{1+|y|^{7}}\Big]\,\|E\|_{3+\beta}.

Define
\bar{\psi}=(-\Delta)^{-1}\Big(\frac{\varepsilon^{-2-\sigma}}{1+|y|^{3+\beta}}+\frac{\varepsilon^{-4-\sigma}}{1+|y|^{7}}\Big)
given by the Newtonnian potential in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
:

\bar{\psi}(y,t)={1\over 2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\log{1\over|y-z|}\,\Big(\frac{\varepsilon^{-2-\sigma}}{1+|z|^{3+\beta}}+\frac{\varepsilon^{-4-\sigma}}{1+|z|^{7}}\Big)\,dz.

One can show that

(1+|y|)|\nabla\bar{\psi}|+|\bar{\psi}|\,\lesssim\Big[\frac{\varepsilon^{-2-\sigma}}{1+|y|^{1+\beta}}+\frac{\varepsilon^{-4-\sigma}}{1+|y|^{5}}\Big]\,\|E\|_{3+\beta}.

Then
\hat{\psi}=:M\,\|E\|_{3+\beta}\,\bar{\psi}
satisfies

\mathop{\rm div}(\mathcal{P}\nabla\hat{\psi})+H\leq 0,

provided the constant
M>0
is taken large enough. This gives the bound (
10.15
) on
\psi_{2}
.
∎

10.3.
Estimates for a projected problem

Here we consider the “projected version” of Problem (
10.3
),

(10.16)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\,p\,{\partial}_{t}\phi

\displaystyle+\nabla_{y}^{\perp}\left(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}+a\right)\cdot\nabla_{y}\left(\phi-f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})p^{2}\psi\right)\,+\,E(y,t)=\,\sum_{l=0}^{3}c_{l}(t)z_{1l}(y){\quad\hbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(0,T)

\displaystyle\phi(\cdot,0)\,=\,0{\quad\hbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{2}

under the same assumptions on
a
and
a_{*}
as in (
10.3
), and

(10.17)

\|E\|_{3+\beta}\ <\ +\infty,\quad

for some
0<\beta<1
(see (
10.10
) for the definition of this norm). We recall that

p(y_{1},t)=\left(1+{\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}\over r_{j}}\chi\right)\quad{\mbox{and}}\quad\mathcal{P}(y_{1},t)=\left(1+{\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}\over r_{j}}\chi\right)^{3},

as defined in (
9.10
).
Besides
\psi
is given by (
10.2
) and
\phi
satisfies the orthogonality conditions (
10.1
). The functions
z_{1\ell}
are given in (
9.36
), we recall them here

\displaystyle z_{10}(y)=U_{0}(y),

\displaystyle\quad z_{11}(y)={\partial}_{y_{1}}U_{0}(y),\quad z_{12}(y)={\partial}_{y_{2}}U_{0}(y),\quad z_{13}(y)=2U_{0}(y)+{\nabla}_{y}U_{0}(y)\cdot y.

We next compute
c_{\ell}(t)
in (
10.16
).
In accordance with (
9.12
) we write

\displaystyle p^{2}(\psi_{j}^{0}-r_{j}\alpha_{j}|\log\varepsilon_{j}|+\psi_{j}^{*})=\Gamma_{0j}(y)-(4-\alpha_{j}r_{j})\log\varepsilon_{j}-\log 8+K(P_{j};P_{j})+\eta_{4\varepsilon}b_{j}^{*}

and we also write

\displaystyle p^{2}(\psi_{j}^{0}-r_{j}\alpha_{j}|\log\varepsilon_{j}|+\psi_{j}^{*})=p^{2}\left(\psi_{0j}+g_{*}\right).

where
\psi_{0j}
is defined in (
9.11
) up to constant, and
g_{*}
is

g_{*}=-r_{j}\alpha_{j}|\log\varepsilon_{j}|+g_{**}

with
g_{**}

|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}|{\partial}_{t}g_{**}(y,t)|+(1+|y|)|\,{\nabla}_{y}g_{**}(y,t)|+|g_{**}(y,t)|\ \leq\ C\varepsilon^{2}\,(1+|y|^{2})\,\log(1+|y|).

Taking
a_{*}=\eta_{4\varepsilon}b_{j}^{*}
(see (
10.6
) with
\lambda=1
)
we get

(10.18)

\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}-(4-\alpha_{j}r_{j})\log\varepsilon_{j}-\log 8+K(P_{j};P_{j})=p^{2}(\psi_{0j}+g_{*}).

Setting again
f_{0}(s)=e^{s}
, we have

f_{0}\left(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}\right)=\tilde{f}\left(p^{2}(\psi_{0j}+g_{*})\right):=8e^{-K(P_{j};P_{j})}\varepsilon_{j}^{4-\alpha_{j}r_{j}}e^{p^{2}(\psi_{0j}+g_{*})}

Let

(10.19)

\displaystyle{\bf B}(y,t)

\displaystyle:=\mathop{\rm div}\left(\mathcal{P}\nabla(\psi_{0j}+g_{*})\right)+\mathcal{P}\tilde{f}\left(p^{2}\,(\psi_{0j}+g_{*})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\rm div}\left(\mathcal{P}\nabla(\psi_{0j}+g_{*})\right)+\mathcal{P}\,f_{0}\left(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}\right),\quad{\mbox{and}}

\displaystyle{\bf L}(\psi)

\displaystyle:=\mathop{\rm div}\left(\mathcal{P}\nabla\psi\right)+\mathcal{P}\,f_{0}^{\prime}\left(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}\right)\,p^{2}\psi.

The construction in Proposition
5.1
gives that
for some
\nu>0
, for all
(y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]

|{\bf B}(y,t)|\leq\ {\varepsilon^{2+\nu}\over(1+|y|)^{1+\nu}}.

We also have, after differentiating first with respect to
y_{2}
, then with respect to
y_{1}
,

(10.20)

\displaystyle|{\bf L}({\partial}_{2}(\psi_{0j}+g_{*}))|=\left|\mathop{\rm div}(\mathcal{P}\nabla{\partial}_{2}(\psi_{0j}+g_{*}))+\mathcal{P}f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})p^{2}{\partial}_{2}(\psi_{0j}+g_{*})\right|\leq\ {\varepsilon^{2+\nu}\over(1+|y|)^{2+\nu}}

\displaystyle\Delta_{5,j}{\partial}_{1}(\psi_{0j}+g_{*})+f^{\prime}_{0}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,\,{\partial}_{1}(p^{2}(\psi_{0j}+g_{*}))-{3\varepsilon^{2}\over p^{2}}{\partial}_{1}(\psi_{0j}+g_{*})=R_{1},\quad{\mbox{with}}

\displaystyle\left|R_{1}(y,t)\right|\leq\ {\varepsilon^{2+\nu}\over(1+|y|)^{2+\nu}}

Besides, we choose the functions
b_{3}
in the definition of
{\bf Z}_{3}
in (
9.25
), and
b_{1}
in
{\bf Z}_{1}
: we choose

(10.21)

b_{3}(y,t)\,:=\,G^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j})\,a_{*}(y,t),\quad p^{2}{\bf Z}_{1}=\left(\int_{0}^{y_{1}}(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}s)^{3}\,ds\right)

where
G
has been introduced in (
9.25
).
We will also assume conditions (
10.5
) on
a_{*}
and
a
.

Under these further assumptions, we prove the following

Proposition 10.1
.

For all sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
and any functions
E(y,t)
and
\phi(y,t)
that satisfy
(\ref{orto40})
,
(\ref{innerL2})
and
(\ref{EEnu})
, we have that the numbers
c_{\ell}(t)
define linear functionals of
E
which satisfy the estimate

\gamma_{\ell}c_{\ell}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(\cdot,t){\bf Z_{\ell}}\,dy\,+\,o(1)\|E\|_{3+\beta},\quad{\mbox{with}}\quad o(1)\to 0,\quad\quad\mbox{as}\quad\varepsilon\to 0.

Besides
\phi
and
\psi
satisfy the estimates
(\ref{pass1})
,
(\ref{pass2})
,
(\ref{pass3})
.

Proof.

We define

\tilde{E}(y,t)=E(y,t)-\,\sum_{l=0}^{3}c_{l}(t)z_{1l}(y),

so that
\|\tilde{E}\|_{3+\beta}<\infty
.
Lemma
10.1
gives that

\|U^{-{1\over 2}}\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-2}|\log\varepsilon|^{-{1\over 2}}\mathcal{M},

where

\mathcal{M}\,:=\,\|E\|_{3+\beta}+\sum_{\ell=0}^{3}\|c_{\ell}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T)}\,.

Arguing as in the proof of Lemma
10.2
we have that for any
\sigma>0
small, we can find
q>2
such that
\phi
solution to (
10.16
) satisfies

\|U^{{1\over q}-1}\phi\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-2-\sigma}\mathcal{M}.

Thanks to Corollary
10.1
we also have the validity of the pointwise estimate

|\phi(y,t)|\ \leq\ C\,\Big[\frac{\varepsilon^{-2-\sigma}}{1+|y|^{3+\beta}}+\frac{\varepsilon^{-4-\sigma}}{1+|y|^{7}}\Big]\,\mathcal{M}.

Recalling the relations (
10.1
)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{P}\phi\,{\bf Z}_{\ell}=0,\quad\ell=0,1,2,3,

where
{\bf Z}_{\ell}
are defined in (
9.25
), we multiply (
10.16
) against
p^{2}{\bf Z}_{\ell}
and integrate on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
to get

\displaystyle\gamma_{l}c_{l}(t)

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi\,{\partial}_{t}(\mathcal{P}{\bf Z}_{\ell})\,dy\,-\sum_{m\not=\ell}c_{m}(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}p^{2}z_{1m}{\bf Z}_{\ell}\,dy\,+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(\cdot,t)\,p^{2}{\bf Z}_{\ell}\,dy

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla_{y}^{\perp}\left(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}+a\right)\cdot\nabla_{y}\left(\phi-f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,\psi\right)\,p^{2}{\bf Z}_{\ell}\,dy.

where

\gamma_{\ell}\,=\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}p^{2}\,z_{1\ell}{\bf Z}_{\ell}\,dy,\quad l=0,1,2,3.\\

A direct computation gives

\gamma_{\ell}=(1+O(\varepsilon R))\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\,z_{1\ell}{\bf Z}_{\ell}\,dy,

as
\varepsilon\to 0
, for all
\ell
.
Besides, for
m\not=\ell
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}p^{2}z_{1m}{\bf Z}_{\ell}\,dy=O(\varepsilon R).

Take
\ell=3
and recall that

p^{2}\,{\bf Z}_{3}=G*\Gamma_{0j})+b_{3}
.
It is straighforward to see that

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi\,{\partial}_{t}(\mathcal{P}{\bf Z}_{3})\,dy

\displaystyle=O(\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2})\|\phi(\cdot,t)U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Integrating by parts we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla_{y}^{\perp}\left(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}+a\right)\cdot\nabla_{y}\left(\phi-f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,\psi\right)(\cdot,t)\,p^{2}{\bf Z}_{3}\,dy\,.

\displaystyle=\ -\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\phi-f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,\psi\right){\nabla}_{y}(p^{2}{\bf Z}_{3})\cdot\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}+a).

Recalling that
p^{2}\,{\bf Z}_{3}=G(\Gamma_{0j}(y))+G^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j})\,a_{*}(y,t)
, we write

p^{2}{\bf Z}_{3}(y,t)=G(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}\,)+\tilde{\bf Z}_{3}(y,t),

and we get

\displaystyle{\nabla}_{y}(p^{2}{\bf Z}_{3}\cdot\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}+a))

\displaystyle={\nabla}_{y}{\bf Z}_{3}\cdot\nabla_{y}^{\perp}a+{\nabla}_{y}\tilde{\bf Z}_{3}\cdot\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})

and, using (
10.21
),

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}

\displaystyle\left(\phi-f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,\psi\right)\,\,{\nabla}_{y}(p^{2}{\bf Z}_{3})\cdot\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}+a)=O(\varepsilon^{2})\|\phi(\cdot,t)U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

We conclude that

\displaystyle\gamma_{3}c_{3}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(\cdot,t)\,{\bf Z}_{3}\,dy\,+O(\varepsilon^{2})\|\phi(\cdot,t)U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+O(\varepsilon R)\sum_{\ell=0}^{2}|c_{\ell}(t)|

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(\cdot,t)\,{\bf Z}_{3}\,dy\,+o(1)\mathcal{M}+O(\varepsilon R)\sum_{\ell=0}^{2}|c_{\ell}(t)|,\quad o(1)\to 0,\quad\quad\mbox{as}\quad\varepsilon\to 0.

Take now
\ell=0
. We have

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi\,{\partial}_{t}(\mathcal{P}{\bf Z}_{0})\,dy=\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi\,{\partial}_{t}(\mathcal{P})\,{\bf Z}_{0}\,dy=O(\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2})\|\phi(\cdot,t)U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla_{y}^{\perp}\left(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}+a\right)\cdot\nabla_{y}\left(\phi-f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,\psi\right)\,p^{2}\,{\bf Z}_{0}\,dy

\displaystyle=\ -\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\phi-f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,\psi\right)\,\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}+a)\cdot\nabla_{y}p^{2}

\displaystyle=\ -2{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\phi-f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,\psi\right)\,(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\chi)\,(\chi+y_{1}\chi^{\prime}){\partial}_{2}\Gamma_{0}+O(\varepsilon^{2})\|\phi(\cdot,t)U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

as

\nabla p^{2}=2{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}\,y_{1}\,\chi)\,(\chi+y_{1}\chi^{\prime}){\bf e}_{1}

with
\chi
given in (
9.10
). We recall now that

-\phi=\Delta\psi+{3\varepsilon_{j}\over(r_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}\chi)}{\partial}_{1}\psi.

Integrating by parts we get

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi

\displaystyle(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\chi)\,(\chi+y_{1}\chi^{\prime}){\partial}_{2}\Gamma_{0}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Delta({\partial}_{2}\Gamma_{0})(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\chi)\,(\chi+y_{1}\chi^{\prime})\psi

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Delta\left((1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\chi)\,(\chi+y_{1}\chi^{\prime})\right)\,{\partial}_{2}\Gamma_{0}\psi+{3\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\,(\chi+y_{1}\chi^{\prime}){\partial}_{2}\Gamma_{0}{\partial}_{1}\psi

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Delta({\partial}_{2}\Gamma_{0})\psi+O(\varepsilon)\|\phi(\cdot,t)U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Since
\Delta({\partial}_{2}\Gamma_{0})+f^{\prime}_{0}(\Gamma_{0}){\partial}_{2}\Gamma_{0}=0
, we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\phi-f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,\psi\right)\,(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\chi)\,(\chi+y_{1}\chi^{\prime}){\partial}_{2}\Gamma_{0}=O(\varepsilon)\|\phi(\cdot,t)U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

and we conclude that

\gamma_{0}c_{0}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(\cdot,t)\,{\bf Z}_{3}\,dy\,+o(1)\mathcal{M}+O(\varepsilon R)\sum_{\ell\not=0}|c_{\ell}(t)|,\quad o(1)\to 0,\quad\quad\mbox{as}\quad\varepsilon\to 0.

Consider now
l=1
. Recalling that
\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}=p^{2}(\psi_{0j}+g_{*})
in (
10.18
) and that
\nabla\left(p^{2}\,{\bf Z}_{1}\right)=p^{3}\,{\bf 1}_{B_{8R}}\,{\bf e}_{1}
we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}+a)\cdot\nabla_{y}\left(\phi-f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,\psi\right)\,p^{2}\,{\bf Z}_{1}\,dy

\displaystyle=-\ \int_{{B_{8R}}}\left(\phi-f^{\prime}_{0}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,\psi\right)\,\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}+a)\cdot\,p^{3}\,{\bf e}_{1}\,dy

\displaystyle+\,\int_{{\partial}B_{8R}}\left(\phi-f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j})\psi\right)\,\nabla_{y}^{\perp}\Gamma_{0j}\cdot\nu(y)\,p^{2}\,{\bf Z}_{1}\,d\sigma

\displaystyle=\ \int_{{B_{8R}}}\left(\phi-f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,\psi\right)\mathcal{P}{\partial}_{2}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,dy+\,O(\varepsilon^{2})\|\phi(\cdot,t)U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Since
-{1\over\mathcal{P}}\mathop{\rm div}(\mathcal{P}\nabla\psi)=\phi
, we have, for an arbitrary
h

\displaystyle\int_{{B_{8R}}}\mathcal{P}\phi h

\displaystyle=-\int_{{B_{8R}}}\mathop{\rm div}(\mathcal{P}\nabla\psi)h=-\int_{{B_{8R}}}{1\over\mathcal{P}}\mathop{\rm div}(\mathcal{P}\nabla h)\mathcal{P}\psi+\int_{{{\partial}B_{8R}}}\mathcal{P}(\nabla\psi\cdot\nu h-\psi\nabla h\cdot\nu).

We now take
h={\partial}_{2}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,
. Using the estimates in Lemma
10.3
, (
10.14
) and (
10.15
), we obtain

\int_{{{\partial}B_{8R}}}\mathcal{P}(\nabla\psi\cdot\nu h-\psi\nabla h\cdot\nu)=o(1)\,\mathcal{M}.

Moreover we get

\int_{{B_{8R}}}\left(\phi-f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,\psi\right)\,\mathcal{P}\,{\partial}_{2}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,dy=-\int_{{B_{8R}}}{\bf L}\left({\partial}_{2}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\right)\,\psi\,dy+o(1)\mathcal{M},

where
{\bf L}
is defined in (
10.19
).
From (
10.18
) we observe that

\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}
and
p^{2}(\psi_{0j}+g_{*})
differ by a constant term, so

{\partial}_{2}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})={\partial}_{2}\left(p^{2}(\psi_{0j}+g_{*})\right)
. Hence we conclude that

\int_{{B_{8R}}}\left(\phi-f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,\psi\right)\,\mathcal{P}\,{\partial}_{2}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,dy=O(\varepsilon^{2+\nu})\|U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+O(\varepsilon^{2})\|\phi(\cdot,t)U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+o(1)\mathcal{M}

thanks to (
10.20
). Besides

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}^{2}|\log\varepsilon|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi\,{\partial}_{t}(\mathcal{P}{\bf Z}_{1})\,dy=O(\varepsilon^{3}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2})\|\phi(\cdot,t)U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

so that

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}c_{1}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(\cdot,t)\,p^{2}{\bf Z}_{1}\,dy+o(1)\mathcal{M}+O(\varepsilon R)\sum_{\ell\not=1}|c_{\ell}(t)|,\quad o(1)\to 0,\quad\quad\mbox{as}\quad\varepsilon\to 0.

The last case to consider is
l=2
. Recall that
p^{2}\,{\bf Z}_{2}=\,\left(y_{2}+\varepsilon y_{1}y_{2}\right)\,{\bf 1}_{B_{8R}}
, so

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}+a)\cdot\nabla_{y}\left(\phi-f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,\psi\right)\,p^{2}\,{\bf Z}_{2}\,dy

\displaystyle=-\ \int_{{B_{8R}}}\left(\phi-f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,\psi\right)\,\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}+a)\cdot\,\nabla(p^{2}{\bf Z}_{2})\,dy

\displaystyle+\,\int_{{\partial}B_{8R}}\left(\phi-f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j})\psi\right)\,\nabla_{y}^{\perp}\Gamma_{0j}\cdot\nu(y)\,p^{2}{\bf Z}_{2}\,d\sigma

\displaystyle=\ -\int_{{B_{8R}}}\left(\phi-f^{\prime}_{0}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,\psi\right)\cdot\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\cdot\,\nabla(p^{2}{\bf Z}_{2})\,dy+\,O(\varepsilon^{2})\|\phi(\cdot,t)U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle=-\int_{{B_{8R}}}\left(-\Delta_{5,j}\psi-f^{\prime}_{0}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,\psi\right)\,\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\cdot\,\nabla(p^{2}{\bf Z}_{2})\,dy+\,O(\varepsilon^{2})\|\phi(\cdot,t)U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Use the fact that
\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}=p^{2}(\psi_{0j}+g_{*})
in (
10.18
) to compute

\displaystyle h_{0}

\displaystyle:=\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\cdot\,\nabla(p^{2}{\bf Z}_{2})=h_{1}+h_{2}

\displaystyle h_{1}

\displaystyle={\partial}_{1}(p^{2}(\psi_{0j}+g_{*}))

\displaystyle h_{2}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\,\left(y_{1}{\partial}_{1}(p^{2}(\psi_{0j}+g_{*}))-y_{2}{\partial}_{2}(p^{2}(\psi_{0j}+g_{*}))\right)

Integrating by parts and using estimates (
10.3
), (
10.14
) and (
10.15
) we get

\displaystyle\int_{{B_{8R}}}

\displaystyle\left(-\Delta_{5,j}\psi-f^{\prime}_{0}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,\psi\right)\,\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\cdot\,\nabla(p^{2}{\bf Z}_{2})\,dy

\displaystyle=\int_{{B_{8R}}}\left(-\Delta_{5,j}h_{0}+{6\varepsilon\over p}{\partial}_{1}h_{0}-{3\varepsilon^{2}\over h^{2}}h_{0}-f^{\prime}_{0}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,h_{0}\right)\,\psi\,dy

\displaystyle=\int_{{B_{8R}}}\left(-\Delta_{5,j}h_{0}+6\varepsilon{\partial}_{1}h_{0}-f^{\prime}_{0}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,h_{0}\right)\,\psi\,dy+\,O(\varepsilon^{2})\|\phi(\cdot,t)U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

We have

\displaystyle\int_{{B_{8R}}}

\displaystyle\left(-\Delta_{5,j}h_{1}+6\varepsilon{\partial}_{1}h_{1}-f^{\prime}_{0}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,h_{1}\right)\,\psi\,dy

\displaystyle=\int_{{B_{8R}}}p^{2}\left(-\Delta_{5,j}{\partial}_{1}(\psi_{0j}+g_{*})-f^{\prime}_{0}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,\,{\partial}_{1}(p^{2}(\psi_{0j}+g_{*}))\right)\,\psi\,dy

\displaystyle+\int_{{B_{8R}}}p^{2}\left(4\varepsilon{\partial}_{1}^{2}(\psi_{0j}+g_{*})-2\varepsilon\Delta(\psi_{0j}+g_{*})\right)\,\psi\,dy+\,O(\varepsilon^{2})\|\phi(\cdot,t)U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle=\int_{{B_{8R}}}p^{2}\left(-\Delta_{5,j}{\partial}_{1}(\psi_{0j}+g_{*})-f^{\prime}_{0}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,\,{\partial}_{1}(p^{2}(\psi_{0j}+g_{*}))\right)\,\psi\,dy

\displaystyle+\int_{{B_{8R}}}\left(2\varepsilon{\partial}_{1}^{2}\Gamma_{0}-2\varepsilon{\partial}_{2}^{2}\Gamma_{0}\right)\,\psi\,dy+\,O(\varepsilon^{2})\|\phi(\cdot,t)U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

and

\displaystyle\int_{{B_{8R}}}

\displaystyle\left(-\Delta_{5,j}h_{2}+6\varepsilon{\partial}_{1}h_{2}-f^{\prime}_{0}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,h_{2}\right)\,\psi\,dy

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\int_{{B_{8R}}}\left(-\Delta(y_{1}{\partial}_{1}\Gamma_{0})-U\,\,y_{1}{\partial}_{1}\Gamma_{0}+\Delta(y_{2}{\partial}_{2}\Gamma_{0})-U\,\,y_{2}{\partial}_{2}\Gamma_{0}\right)\,\psi\,dy+\,O(\varepsilon^{2})\|\phi(\cdot,t)U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\int_{{B_{8R}}}\left(-2{\partial}_{1}^{2}\Gamma_{0}+2{\partial}_{2}^{2}\Gamma_{0}\right)\,\psi\,dy+\,O(\varepsilon^{2})\|\phi(\cdot,t)U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

In the latter computation we use that

\Delta(y_{j}{\partial}_{j}\Gamma_{0})+U\,\,y_{j}{\partial}_{j}\Gamma_{0}=y_{j}\left(\Delta({\partial}_{j}\Gamma_{0})+U\,\,{\partial}_{j}\Gamma_{0}\right)+2{\partial}_{j}^{2}\Gamma_{0}=2{\partial}_{j}^{2}\Gamma_{0},\quad j=1,2.

We conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{{B_{8R}}}

\displaystyle\left(-\Delta_{5,j}\psi-f^{\prime}_{0}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,\psi\right)\,\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\cdot\,\nabla(p^{2}{\bf Z}_{2})\,dy=\int_{{B_{8R}}}\left(-\Delta_{5,j}\psi-f^{\prime}_{0}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,\psi\right)\,h_{0}\,dy

\displaystyle=\int_{{B_{8R}}}p^{2}\left(-\Delta_{5,j}{\partial}_{1}(\psi_{0j}+g_{*})-f^{\prime}_{0}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,\,{\partial}_{1}(p^{2}(\psi_{0j}+g_{*}))\right)\,\psi\,dy

\displaystyle+\,O(\varepsilon^{2})\|\phi(\cdot,t)U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

We now use the second estimate in (
10.20
) to conclude that

\int_{{B_{8R}}}\left(-\Delta_{5,j}\psi-f^{\prime}_{0}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,\psi\right)\,\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\cdot\,\nabla(p^{2}{\bf Z}_{2})\,dy=O(\varepsilon^{2})\|\phi(\cdot,t)U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

and hence

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*}+a)\cdot\nabla_{y}\left(\phi-f_{0}^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0j}+a_{*})\,p^{2}\,\psi\right)\,p^{2}\,{\bf Z}_{2}\,dy=O(\varepsilon^{2})\|\phi(\cdot,t)U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Thus we get

\displaystyle\gamma_{2}c_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(\cdot,t)\,p^{2}{\bf Z}_{2}\,dy+o(1)\mathcal{M}+O(\varepsilon R)\sum_{\ell\not=1}|c_{\ell}(t)|,\quad o(1)\to 0,\quad\quad\mbox{as}\quad\varepsilon\to 0.

Combining the above estimates, we obtain the expected result.
∎

We want to apply Proposition
10.1

to obtain a priori estimates for the projected non-linear problem (
9.35
). We take

a_{*}=\lambda\eta_{4\varepsilon}\,b_{j}^{*},\quad a=\lambda\left(\eta_{4\varepsilon}b_{j}^{**}+b_{j}\right)

in the inner operator defined in (
9.33
), that we write in the form

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle|\log\varepsilon|\,p\,{\partial}_{t}\phi+\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0}+a_{*}+a)\cdot\nabla_{y}(\phi-f^{\prime}(\Gamma_{0}+a_{*})p^{2}\psi)+\,\Theta_{j,\lambda}

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\perp}((1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\chi)^{2}\hat{\psi}_{j})\cdot\nabla\left(\lambda\,\eta_{4\varepsilon}U^{*}\right)=\,\sum_{l=0}^{3}c_{l}(t)\mathcal{Z}_{1l}(y){\quad\hbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(0,T)

\displaystyle\phi(\cdot,0)\,=\,0{\quad\hbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{2}

where

\displaystyle\Theta_{j,\lambda}

\displaystyle=\nabla^{\perp}\left[\varepsilon_{j}\left(|\log\varepsilon|\dot{\bf a}_{j}+D_{x}{\nabla}_{x}\varphi_{j}({\bf P}_{j};{\bf P})[{\bf a}]+\lambda\eta_{4\varepsilon}(1+{\varepsilon_{j}y_{1}\over r_{1}})^{2}r_{j}\psi^{out}\right)\cdot y\right]\nabla U

(10.22)

\displaystyle+\lambda\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{j}(\beta_{j},\psi^{out},{\bf a})+|\log\varepsilon|\varepsilon_{j}^{2}(1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}y_{1}\chi)\sum_{\ell=0,3}{\partial}_{t}(\beta_{j\ell}\mathcal{Z}_{1\ell})\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]

Besides we have
a priori bounds of the form

(10.23)

\gamma_{\ell}c_{l}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Theta_{j,\lambda}(\cdot,t){\bf Z_{\ell}}\,dy\,+\,o(1)\|\Theta_{j,\lambda}\|_{3+\beta}.

for some
o(1)\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
readily follow, of course provided that
a(y,t)
satisfies the required smallness assumptions. This is guaranteed by the choice of spaces for the parameter functions
\beta_{j},\psi^{out},{\bf a}
. This is what we shall specify in the next section.

11.
Fixed point formulation and conclusion of the proof

In this section we set up the system (
9.35
), (
9.37
), (
9.38
), (
9.39
) as a fixed point problem in the form (
9.40
) in an appropriate Banach space for the parameter functions

\vec{p}=(\hat{\phi},\beta,\phi^{out},\psi^{out},{\bf a}).

11.1.
The space for the parameter functions.

We begin by defining an appropriate norm for the functions

\hat{\phi}_{j}(y,t)
,
\phi^{out}(x,t)
,
\psi^{out}(x,t)
,
\beta(t)
and
{\bf a}(t)
.

Let us fix a small number
0<\beta<1
.
For and arbitrary functions
\phi(y,t)
we define the inner norm

\displaystyle\|\phi\|_{i}\,:=

\displaystyle\,\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\mathcal{P}\,\phi(\cdot,t)U^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle+\sup_{(y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]}|\,(1+|y|)^{3+\beta}\min\{1,\varepsilon^{2+\frac{\beta}{4}}(1+|y|)^{4-\beta}\}\phi(y,t)\,|

For the outer functions
\psi^{out}
,
\phi^{out}
we consider the following norms
for functions
\phi(x,t)
,
\psi(x,t)
defined in
\Sigma\times[0,T]
.

\displaystyle\|\phi\|_{o1}\,:=

\displaystyle\,\|(1+|x|)^{2+\nu}\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma\times[0,T])},

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{o2}\,:=

\displaystyle\,\|(1+|x|)^{\nu}\psi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma\times[0,T])}+\|(1+|x|)^{1+\nu}{\nabla}_{x}\psi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma\times[0,T])}\,.

We consider the space
X
of all continuous functions

\vec{p}=(\hat{\phi},\beta,\phi^{out},\psi^{out},{\bf a})

such that

{\nabla}_{y}\hat{\psi}(y,t),\quad{\nabla}_{x}\psi^{out}(x,t),\quad{d\over dt}\beta(t),\quad{d\over dt}{\bf a}(t)

exist and are continuous and such that

\|\vec{p}\,\|_{X}\,:=\,\|\phi^{out}\|_{o1}+\|\psi^{out}\|_{o2}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\big(\|\hat{\phi}_{j}\|_{i}+\|\beta_{j}\|_{C^{1}[0,T]}+\|{\bf a}\|_{C^{1}[0,T]}\big)\,<\,+\infty.

We define the set
{\mathcal{O}}
as a “deformed ball” centered at
\vec{p}=\vec{0}
. We fix
\nu=1
in the definition of
\|\phi^{out}\|_{o1}-\|\psi^{out}\|_{o2}
and let
{\mathcal{O}}
be the set of all functions

\vec{p}=(\tilde{\phi},\alpha,\tilde{\xi},\phi^{out},\psi^{out})\in X
such that

(11.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum_{j=1}^{k}\|\hat{\phi}_{j}\|_{i}\ <\ \varepsilon^{3-3\sigma_{*}},\\
&\sum_{j=1}^{k}\|\beta_{j}\|_{C^{1}[0,T]}\,<\,\varepsilon^{3-3\sigma_{*}},\quad\sum_{j=1}^{k}\|{\bf a}\|_{C^{1}[0,T]}\,<\,\varepsilon^{4-3\sigma_{*}},\\
&\|\phi^{out}\|_{o1}\ <\ \varepsilon^{4-3\sigma_{*}},\quad\|\psi^{out}\|_{o2}\ <\ \varepsilon^{4-3\sigma_{*}}.\end{aligned}\right.

The number
\sigma_{*}
is introduced in Proposition
5.1
.

11.2.
Fixed point formulation

Let us express System (
9.35
), (
9.37
), (
9.38
), (
9.39
) in the fixed point form (
9.40
)
for a suitable operator
{\mathcal{F}}(\cdot,\lambda)
, in a region of
the form
(\ref{OO})
.

We start with (
9.35
).
For a given function
a(y,t)
let us consider the transport operator

{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(a)[\phi]:=|\log\varepsilon|\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,p\,{\partial}_{t}\phi+{\nabla}_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a)\cdot{\nabla}_{y}\phi,

and for a bounded function
E(y,t)
the linear equation

{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(a)[\phi]+E=0{\quad\hbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]\quad\phi(\cdot,0)=0{\quad\hbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{2}.

The result of Lemma
7.1
is still valid for a transport equation of the form

\displaystyle|\log\varepsilon|\varepsilon_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle p\,{\partial}_{t}\phi+\nabla_{y}^{\perp}(\Gamma_{0j}+a)\cdot\nabla_{y}\phi+\,E(y,t)\,=\,0{\quad\hbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(0,T),

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\phi(\cdot,0)\,=\,0{\quad\hbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{2}

Here the function
\chi(y_{1})
is defined in (
9.9
) and the function

a(y,t)
satisfy

a(y,t)\,=0\quad\hbox{for }|y|\geq 8R,\quad\Delta_{y}(a)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(0,T))

and
for some numbers
C>0
,
\nu>0
,

\displaystyle(1+|y|)^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|^{1\over 2}|{\partial}_{t}a(y,t)|+|\,{\nabla}_{y}a(y,t)|\ \leq

\displaystyle\ C\varepsilon^{2}(1+|y|)\,\log(1+|y|).

We call

\phi=\mathcal{{\mathcal{T}}}^{-1}_{j}(a)[E]

the unique solution of this problem, through the representation formula (
7.4
), which defines a linear operator of
E
.
Let us write the operator
E_{j,\lambda}
in (
9.33
) in the form

\displaystyle E_{j,\lambda}(\hat{\phi}_{j},\beta_{j},\psi^{out};{\bf a})

\displaystyle={\mathcal{T}}_{j}(\lambda\eta_{4\varepsilon}b_{j}^{*}({\bf a})+\lambda\eta_{4\varepsilon}b_{j}^{**}({\bf a})+\,\lambda{b}_{j}(\hat{\psi}_{j},\beta_{j},\psi^{out},{\bf a}\,))[\hat{\phi}_{j}]

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\perp}(p^{2}\hat{\psi}_{j})\cdot\nabla\left(\lambda\,\eta_{4\varepsilon}U^{*}\right)+\Theta_{j\lambda}(\hat{\psi}_{j},\beta_{j},\psi^{out};{\bf a}).

and we reformulate equations (
9.35
) as

(11.2)

\displaystyle\hat{\phi}_{j}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}^{in}(\hat{\phi}_{j},\beta_{j},\psi^{out},{\bf a}),

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mbox{where}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}^{in}(\hat{\phi}_{j},\beta_{j},\psi^{out},{\bf a})

\displaystyle={\mathcal{T}}_{j}^{-1}(\lambda\eta_{4\varepsilon}b_{j}^{*}({\bf a})+\lambda\eta_{4\varepsilon}b_{j}^{**}({\bf a})+\,\lambda{b}_{j}(\hat{\psi}_{j},\beta_{j},\psi^{out},{\bf a}\,))\circ

\displaystyle\left[\nabla^{\perp}(p^{2}\hat{\psi}_{j})\cdot\nabla\left(\lambda\,\eta_{4\varepsilon}U^{*}\right)+\Theta_{j\lambda}(\hat{\psi}_{j},\beta_{j},\psi^{out};{\bf a})-\sum_{l=0}^{3}c_{lj}z_{1l}\right]

We reformulate the outer equations (
9.38
)-(
9.39
) in a similar way.
For a given function
e(x,t)
with
r\Delta_{x}e,\,\nabla e\in L^{\infty}(\Sigma\times[0,T])

let us consider the transport operator

{\mathcal{T}}_{o}(e)[\phi]:=|\log\varepsilon|\,r\,{\partial}_{t}\phi+{\nabla}_{y}^{\perp}(r^{2}(\Psi_{*}-r_{0}^{-1}|\log\varepsilon|+e))\cdot{\nabla}_{x}\phi,

and for a bounded function
E(x,t)
, the linear equation

{\mathcal{T}}_{o}(e)[\phi]+E=0{\quad\hbox{in }}\Sigma\times[0,T],\quad\phi(\cdot,0)=0{\quad\hbox{in }}\Sigma.

We call

\phi={\mathcal{T}}_{o}^{-1}(e)[E]

the unique solution of this problem, through the representation formula (
6.32
).
We write (
9.38
)-(
9.39
) in the form

(11.3)

\displaystyle\phi^{out}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{1\lambda}^{out}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out},{\bf a})

\displaystyle\psi^{out}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{2\lambda}^{out}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out},{\bf a})

where

\mathcal{F}_{1\lambda}^{out}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out},{\bf a}):={\mathcal{T}}_{o}^{-1}(\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\bar{\eta}_{j2}\over r_{j}}\psi_{j}({x-P_{j}\over\varepsilon_{j}})+\lambda\psi^{out})\left[\lambda\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{out}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out};{\bf a})\right]

with
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{out}
given by (
9.34
)
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{2\lambda}^{out}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out},{\bf a}):={\mathcal{T}}^{-1}

\displaystyle\Biggl[\lambda\,\phi^{out}+\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{k}(\bar{\eta}_{j1}-\bar{\eta}_{j2}){\phi_{j}\over r_{j}\varepsilon_{j}^{2}}+\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{k}({\psi_{j}\over r_{j}}\Delta_{5}\bar{\eta}_{j2}+2\nabla_{x}\bar{\eta}_{j2}\nabla_{x}{\psi_{j}\over r_{j}})\Biggl]

where
\psi={\mathcal{T}}^{-1}h
is the unique solution of Problem (
4.14
)

\Delta_{5}\psi+h=0,\quad{\mbox{in }}\Sigma,\quad{\partial\psi\over\partial r}=0\quad{\mbox{on}}\,\,{\partial}\Sigma,\quad\psi(x)\to 0,\,\,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|x|\to\infty,

for a smooth function
h
satisfying (
4.15
).
Recall that
\Delta_{5}={{\partial}^{2}\over{\partial}r^{2}}+{3\over r}{{\partial}\over{\partial}r}+{{\partial}^{2}\over{\partial}z^{2}}
, for
x=(r,z)\in\Sigma
.

Using (
10.23
), we write equations
(
9.37
)
c_{\ell j}=0
as

(11.4)

\displaystyle|\log\varepsilon|\dot{\bf a}_{j}

\displaystyle=-D_{x}{\nabla}_{x}\varphi_{j}({\bf P}_{j};{\bf P})[{\bf a}]+\lambda\varepsilon_{j}^{-1}\mathcal{G}_{j}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out};{\bf a}),

\displaystyle|\log\varepsilon|\dot{\beta}_{j}

\displaystyle=\lambda\varepsilon_{j}^{-2}\mathcal{H}_{j}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out};{\bf a}),\quad j=1,\ldots,k,

\displaystyle{\bf a}(0)

\displaystyle=0,\quad\beta(0)=0,

where

\beta_{j}=(\alpha_{0j},\alpha_{3j}),\quad{\bf a}_{j}=(a_{j1},a_{j2}),\quad\beta=(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}),\quad{\bf a}=({\bf a}_{1},\ldots,{\bf a}_{k})

and, for

\mathcal{G}_{j}=(\mathcal{G}_{1j},\mathcal{G}_{2j}),\quad\mathcal{H}_{j}=(\mathcal{H}_{0j},\mathcal{H}_{3j}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\ell j}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out},{\bf a})(t)\ :=

\displaystyle\ \gamma_{\ell}\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big[\,\nabla^{\perp}[\eta_{4\varepsilon}p^{2}r_{j}\psi^{out}\cdot y]\nabla U+\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{j}(\beta_{j},\psi^{out},{\bf a})\,\big]{\bf Z}_{\ell}\,dy

\displaystyle{\mbox{for }}\ell=1,2,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{mj}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out},{\bf a})(t)\ :=

\displaystyle\gamma_{m}\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big[\,\nabla^{\perp}[\eta_{4\varepsilon}p^{2}r_{j}\psi^{out}\cdot y]\nabla U+\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{j}(\beta_{j},\psi^{out},{\bf a})\,\big]{\bf Z}_{m}\,dy

\displaystyle+\gamma_{m}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}p\sum_{i=0,3}\beta_{ji}{\partial}_{t}\mathcal{Z}_{1i}){\bf Z}_{m}

\displaystyle{\mbox{for }}m=0,3.

Recall that
p=1+{\varepsilon_{j}\over r_{j}}\,y_{1}\,\chi
.
Equations (
11.4
) can be written in fixed point form as

(11.5)

\displaystyle{\bf a}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{1\lambda}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out},{\bf a})=|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}\big(B(s)[{\bf a}]+\lambda\varepsilon_{j}^{-1}\mathcal{G}_{j}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out};{\bf a})\big)\,ds

\displaystyle\beta(t)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{0\lambda}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out},{\bf a})=|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}\lambda\varepsilon_{j}^{-2}\mathcal{H}_{j}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out};{\bf a})\,ds

where

\big(B(t)[{\bf a}]\big)_{j}=-D_{x}{\nabla}_{x}\varphi_{j}({\bf P}_{j};{\bf P})[{\bf a}].

System (
9.35
), (
9.37
), (
9.38
), (
9.39
) can be written as a fixed point problem (
11.2
)-(
11.3
)-(
11.5
), which we write as

\displaystyle\hat{\phi}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}^{in}(\hat{\phi},\beta_{j},\psi^{out},{\bf a}),\quad\phi^{out}=\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{1\lambda}^{out}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out},{\bf a}),\quad\psi^{out}=\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{2\lambda}^{out}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out},{\bf a})

\displaystyle{\bf a}(t)=

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{1\lambda}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out},{\bf a}),\quad\beta(t)=\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{0\lambda}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out},{\bf a})

where

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{1\lambda}^{out}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out},{\bf a})

\displaystyle=F_{1\lambda}^{out}(\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}^{in}(\hat{\phi},\beta_{j},\psi^{out},{\bf a}),\beta,\psi^{out},{\bf a})

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{2\lambda}^{out}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out},{\bf a})

\displaystyle={\mathcal{F}}_{2\lambda}^{out}(\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}^{in}(\hat{\phi},\beta_{j},\psi^{out},{\bf a}),\beta,\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{1\lambda}^{out}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out},{\bf a}),{\bf a})

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{1\lambda}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out},{\bf a})

\displaystyle={\mathcal{F}}_{1\lambda}(\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}^{in}(\hat{\phi},\beta_{j},\psi^{out},{\bf a}),\beta,\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{1\lambda}^{out}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out},{\bf a}),{\bf a})

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{0\lambda}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out},{\bf a})

\displaystyle={\mathcal{F}}_{0\lambda}(\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}^{in}(\hat{\phi},\beta_{j},\psi^{out},{\bf a}),\beta,\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{1\lambda}^{out}(\hat{\phi},\beta,\psi^{out},{\bf a}),{\bf a}).

In a more compact form, the above system can be expressed as

(11.6)

\vec{p}\ =\ \tilde{\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}}(\vec{p}),\quad\vec{p}\in\bar{\mathcal{O}}

where
{\mathcal{O}}
is defined in (
11.1
) and

(11.7)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \tilde{\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}}(\vec{p})\,:=&\,({\mathcal{F}}^{in}_{\lambda}(\vec{p}),\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{0\lambda}(\vec{p}),\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{1\lambda}(\vec{p}),\tilde{\mathcal{F}}^{out}_{1\lambda}(\vec{p}),\tilde{\mathcal{F}}^{out}_{2\lambda}(\vec{p})),\\
\vec{p}\,=&\,(\hat{\phi},\beta,\phi^{out},\psi^{out},{\bf a}).\end{aligned}\right.

Lemma 11.1
.

The operator
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{O}}\times[0,1]\to X
given by

\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(\cdot,\lambda)=\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\lambda}
in
(\ref{opera})

is compact.

Proof.

We check that each of the five operators defining
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\lambda}(\vec{p})
is compact in
{\mathcal{O}}
(uniformly in
\lambda
).
This operator
\mathcal{F}^{in}_{\lambda}(\vec{p})
is defined through

g=\mathcal{T}^{-1}_{j}(b)[h]
which has the property in Lemma
8.2
. This gives that a uniform bound in
\Delta_{y}b
and a control of the modulus of continuity in
y
of
h(y,t)
uniformly in
t
yields a uniform control of the modulus of continuity of
g
in both variables
(y,t)
.
We see in (
11.2
) that for a certain
C_{\varepsilon}>0
we have

\|\Delta_{y}(b^{*}_{j}+b_{j})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T])}\leq C_{\varepsilon}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad\vec{p}\in\bar{\mathcal{O}}.

and it vanishes outside a compact set. Moreover, we have a uniform Hölder
control in space variables on the corresponding arguments
h
for
\vec{p}\in\bar{\mathcal{O}}
as it follows from the Hölder estimates for the gradients of
\hat{\psi}_{j}
and
\psi^{out}
inherited from the uniform bounds holding for

\tilde{\psi}
and
\phi^{out}
in the definition of
{\mathcal{O}}
(see the argument in the proof of (
10.11
)). Also, the numbers

c_{lj}(t)
have a uniform bound, thanks to (
10.23
).
Uniform Lipschitz bounds hold for the remaining errors, as it follows in particular from the control of the terms involving

{\nabla}_{y}\phi_{j}^{*}
. Lemma
8.2
then implies that

\tilde{\mathcal{F}}^{in}_{\lambda}(\bar{\mathcal{O}})
is a set of continuous functions

g:\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
whose restrictions to any compact set defines a uniformly bounded, equicontinuous set. Hence, any sequence
\phi_{n}\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}^{in}_{\lambda}({\mathcal{O}})

has a subsequence
\phi_{n^{\prime}}
which is uniformly convergent on each compact set.
Finally, we observe that
\|\phi_{n}\|_{4}\leq C_{\varepsilon}
since the argument of
the transport operator has this property. This implies that
\phi_{n^{\prime}}
is actually convergent in the space of continuous functions with finite

\|\cdot\|_{3+\beta}
-norm, since
0<\beta<1
. Hence

\tilde{\mathcal{F}}^{in}_{\lambda}({\mathcal{O}})
is precompact in this space.
The compactness of the operator
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{1\lambda}^{out}
into
C(\bar{\Omega}\times[0,T])
follows directly from Arzela-Ascoli’s theorem, again from the corresponding control for the transport equation and the
uniform controls on space and time variables valid for the operator
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{1\lambda}^{in}
. From here the compactness for
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{2\lambda}^{out}
follows in similar manner. Finally, the compactness of the operators

\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{0\lambda}(\vec{p}),\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{1\lambda}(\vec{p})

into
C^{1}([0,T])
follows again from the equicontinuity in
t
inherited for the
different terms involved in their definition. The proof is concluded.
∎

11.3.
Conclusion of the proof of Theorem
1

The original problem has been so far reduced to finding a solution of the fixed point problem
(
11.6
) for
\lambda=1
. To do this, we will prove that for all
\lambda\in[0,1]
this equation has no solution
\vec{p}\in{\partial}{\mathcal{O}}
, at least whenever
\varepsilon
is chosen sufficiently small. Let us assume that
\vec{p}\in\bar{\mathcal{O}}
satisfies
(
11.6
) for some
\lambda
. We claim that actually
\vec{p}\in{\mathcal{O}}
.
Take
0<\beta<{\sigma_{*}\over 2}
, with
\sigma_{*}
given in Proposition
5.1
. From (
10.3
) we get

\|\Theta_{j}(\beta,\psi^{out},{\bf a})\|_{3+\beta}\leq\varepsilon^{5-\frac{3}{2}\sigma_{*}}

Corollary
10.1
and Lemma
10.1
then yield, by definition of the inner norm,

\|\hat{\phi}\|_{i}\leq\varepsilon^{3-2\sigma_{*}}\ll\varepsilon^{3-3\sigma_{*}},

the latter number being that involved in the definition of
{\mathcal{O}}
in (
11.1
). We recall here that
\sigma
appearing in Corollary
10.1
and Lemma
10.1
can be made as small as wished.
Let us consider the outer equations.
Examining expression (
9.34
) that determines the size of
\phi^{out}
,
we see that its magnitude does not exceed the order
O(\varepsilon^{4-{\sigma_{*}\over 2}})
. Here we have used the remote size of
\hat{\phi}
implicit in the norm

\|\hat{\phi}_{j}\|_{i}
. Indeed using the size induced in
\hat{\psi}
, we find
that

\|\phi^{out}\|_{o1}+\|\psi^{out}\|_{o2}\leq\varepsilon^{4-2\sigma_{*}}\ll\varepsilon^{4-3\sigma_{*}}.

Finally from the size of
\Theta_{j}
we readily see that

\|{\bf a}_{j}\|_{C^{1}[0,T]}+\varepsilon\|\beta_{j}\|_{C^{1}[0,T]}\leq\varepsilon^{4-2\sigma_{*}}\ll\varepsilon^{4-3\sigma_{*}}.

As a conclusion, we get that
\vec{p}\in{\mathcal{O}}
and the claim has been proven.

Standard degree theory applies then to yield that the degree

\deg(I-\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(\cdot,\lambda),{\mathcal{O}},0)
is well-defined and it is constant in
\lambda\in[0,1]
. Since
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(\cdot,0)
is a linear compact operator, this constant is actually non-zero.
Existence of a solution in
{\mathcal{O}}
for
\lambda=1
then follows. The proof is concluded. ∎
